Our results shed light on future directions for GenAI-based data augmentation methods and data-driven research on satellite network measurement.

</details>


### [26] [Reasoning with Memory: A Temporal Granularity-Adaptive Framework for Training-Free Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24794)
*Linghao Meng,Qiankun Li,Junyuan Mao,Pujin Liao,Zhicheng He,Enbo Zhang,Kun Wang,Yang Liu,Huazhu Fu,Yueming Jin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReMem is a training-free framework that uses dual-level memory-augmented adaptation to select keyframes adaptively based on query temporal granularity, enabling better long video understanding for MLLMs without requiring additional training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing keyframe selection methods for long video understanding in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) use uniform sampling or static query-guided approaches, which often miss critical temporal context and fail to adapt to varying query temporal granularities.

Method: ReMem introduces dual-level memory-augmented adaptation: 1) Memory-Driven Question Parsing at query level uses LLM long-term memory to decode question temporal granularity and extract semantic entities; 2) Synergistic Dual-Semantic Frame Alignment at video level uses intrinsic structural memory to align frames with query semantics; 3) Structure-Aware Dynamic Frame Routing clusters events and optimally distributes sampling budgets.

Result: Evaluations across four LongVideoQA benchmarks using three MLLMs show state-of-the-art zero-shot performance: LLaVA-Video with ReMem achieves 54.5% (+12.3% improvement) on LVBench and 67.1% (+8.2% improvement) on LongVideoBench.

Conclusion: ReMem effectively preserves temporal information through memory mechanisms, suppresses redundancy, and enables robust multi-granular video reasoning in MLLMs for long video understanding without requiring additional training.

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) demonstrate superior generalization in fundamental video tasks, restricted context windows limit their long video understanding. To accommodate this constraint, models typically resort to keyframe selection. However, uniform sampling or static query-guided selection often overlooks critical temporal context, failing to adapt to the varying query temporal granularities. In this paper, we propose ReMem, a temporal granularity-adaptive keyframe selection framework for training-free LongVideoQA. ReMem introduces a dual-level memory-augmented adaptation. At the query level, Memory-Driven Question Parsing leverages LLM long-term memory to decode question temporal granularity and extract semantic entities. At the video level, Synergistic Dual-Semantic Frame Alignment exploits intrinsic structural memory to align frames with query semantics, guiding Structure-Aware Dynamic Frame Routing to cluster events and optimally distribute sampling budgets. By explicitly preserving temporal information with memory mechanisms, ReMem suppresses redundancy and empowers MLLMs to perform robust multi-granular video reasoning. Evaluations across four popular LongVideoQA benchmarks using three MLLMs demonstrate highly efficient, state-of-the-art zero-shot performance; notably, LLaVA-Video with ReMem reaches 54.5% (+12.3%) on LVBench and 67.1% (+8.2%) on LongVideoBench.

</details>


### [27] [When Shortest Isn't Safest: A Design Science Approach to Senior-Friendly Pedestrian Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24795)
*Erdi Ünal,Daniel Eisenhardt,Christian Meske,Seyed Nima Afzali,Aysegül Dogangün*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A senior-friendly pedestrian routing system was developed using Design Science Research, incorporating older adults' mobility constraints, amenities, and safety concerns into route planning, resulting in preferred routes over traditional distance/time-optimized baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current pedestrian navigation systems optimize primarily for distance or time, overlooking barriers, safety thresholds, and supportive infrastructure that are crucial for older adults' walking decisions and independent mobility.

Method: Used echeloned Design Science Research with 11 semi-structured interviews to derive design requirements/principles. Implemented an A*-based routing engine on OpenStreetMap enriched with amenities and height data. Conducted field study with 14 older adults comparing artifact-generated routes with baselines.

Result: Senior-friendly routes were preferred overall. Thematic analysis revealed infrastructure maintenance, seasonal conditions, traffic exposure, and social context significantly shape route acceptance. These insights led to refined design requirements and principles.

Conclusion: The research provides actionable guidance for developing senior-friendly pedestrian navigation systems, emphasizing context-aware hazard modeling, multi-route transparency, landmark-grounded explanations, social-context sensitivity, and stage-appropriate information.

Abstract: Older adults' independent mobility enables out-of-home participation, well-being and health, yet pedestrian navigation systems still optimize primarily for distance or time, often overlooking barriers, safety thresholds, and supportive infrastructure that shape late-life walking decisions. We present a senior-friendly pedestrian routing artefact developed through echeloned Design Science Re-search, translating lived mobility constraints into prescriptive design knowledge. Based on 11 semi-structured interviews, we derive initial Design Requirements (DRs) and Design Principles (DPs) for barrier-aware, amenity-sensitive routing and execution-relevant explanations. We instantiate these in an OpenStreetMap pedestrian network enriched with amenities (benches, toilets, and shelters) and height data, and implemented an A*-based routing engine with configurable costs and explanation payloads. In a field-based walking study, 14 older adults com-pared artefact-generated routes with baselines and provided ratings and qualitative feedback; the senior-friendly route was preferred overall. Thematic analysis further showed that infrastructure maintenance, seasonal conditions, traffic exposure, and social context shape route acceptance. We synthesize these insights into refined DRs and DPs emphasizing context-aware hazard modeling, multi-route transparency, landmark-grounded explanations, social-context sensitivity, and stage-appropriate information. Our contributions provide actionable guidance for practitioners developing senior-friendly pedestrian navigation systems.

</details>


### [28] [RRS-10K: A Multitask Vision-Language Model Benchmark for Rare Remote Sensing Image Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24810)
*Yuqiao Lai,Jiancheng Qi,Fei Wang,Yuxin Liu,Kun Li,Ye Chen,Yan Gao,Yanyan Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RRS-10K: A benchmark of 10,738 military-related remote sensing images for evaluating vision-language models on rare scenes, revealing current VLMs' weaknesses in visual grounding, referring segmentation, and complex reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing remote sensing benchmarks are dominated by common urban/rural imagery, leaving VLMs' capability for rare scenes insufficiently understood. There's a gap in evaluating VLMs on long-tail, rare remote sensing scenarios.

Method: Created RRS-10K benchmark with 10,738 military-related images from first-hand sources, organized into 3 capability dimensions (perception, reasoning, robustness), 6 sub-dimensions, and 20 leaf tasks. Introduced similarity-based distractor filtering strategy (SDFS) for quality multiple-choice questions.

Result: Evaluated 52 representative models showing current VLMs achieve only moderate zero-shot performance on rare remote sensing image interpretation, with clear weaknesses in visual grounding, referring segmentation, and complex semantic reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: RRS-10K enables systematic analysis of failure modes in long-tail remote sensing interpretation and provides guidance for developing more reliable remote sensing VLMs that can handle rare scenarios.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on general remote sensing tasks. However, their capability for rare scenes remains insufficiently understood, because existing benchmarks are dominated by common urban and rural imagery. To address this gap, we present RRS-10K, a benchmark for rare remote sensing image interpretation. RRS-10K contains 10,738 military-related remote sensing images and corresponding multiple format question-answer pairs for comprehensive evaluation. All of the images are collected from first-hand sources and organized into three capability dimensions, six sub-dimensions, and 20 leaf tasks, covering perception, reasoning, and robustness. To improve the quality of multiple-choice questions, we introduce a similarity-based distractor filtering strategy (SDFS) during benchmark construction. We further evaluate 52 representative models and show that current VLMs achieve only moderate zero-shot performance on rare remote sensing image interpretation, with clear weaknesses in visual grounding, referring segmentation, and complex semantic reasoning tasks. RRS-10K enables systematic analysis of failure modes in long-tail remote sensing interpretation and provides guidance for developing more reliable remote sensing VLMs.

</details>


### [29] [Aletheia: An Offline-First Clinical Decision Support System for Differential Diagnosis in Low-Resource Healthcare Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24814)
*Joseph Walusimbi,Ann Move Oguti,Abubakhari Sserwadda,Precious Boss Kasasira,Charles Brian Okoboi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Aletheia is an offline-first AI clinical decision support system for low-resource African healthcare settings, achieving 80% diagnostic accuracy with a 3.6B parameter model running without internet connectivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Severe shortage of clinical specialists in sub-Saharan Africa, with physician-to-patient ratios as low as 1:25,000 in rural areas. Existing AI diagnostic tools require reliable internet and high-spec hardware, making them impractical for frontline healthcare workers in district hospitals and health centers.

Method: Developed Aletheia using Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct fine-tuned with Quantised Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA) on a curated dataset of 27,000 clinical reasoning samples covering 50 prevalent East African diseases. System designed to be offline-first with strict memory constraints for deployment in resource-limited settings.

Result: Achieved Top-1 diagnostic accuracy of 80.0%, Top-3 accuracy of 100.0%, BERTScore-F1 of 0.909, and METEOR of 0.467. Met ADTC 2026 memory budget constraint (7,168 MB) with peak inference RAM of ~3,630 MB. Expected Calibration Error of 0.275 demonstrates reliable confidence estimation.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of deploying large language model-based clinical reasoning at primary care level in resource-constrained settings without cloud infrastructure, offering a practical solution for healthcare access gaps in sub-Saharan Africa.

Abstract: Access to specialist clinical expertise remains severely limited across sub-Saharan Africa, where physician-to-patient ratios can fall below 1:25,000 in rural settings. Existing AI-assisted diagnostic tools predominantly require reliable internet connectivity and high-specification hardware, rendering them impractical for frontline healthcare workers in district hospitals and health centres. This paper presents Aletheia, an offline-first clinical decision support system designed for low-resource healthcare contexts across sub-Saharan Africa. Aletheia is built upon Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, fine-tuned using Quantised Low- Rank Adaptation (QLoRA) on a curated dataset of 27,000 clinical reasoning samples spanning 50 disease conditions with elevated prevalence in East Africa. Evaluation demonstrates a Top-1 diagnostic accuracy of 80.0%, Top-3 accuracy of 100.0%, BERTScore-F1 of 0.909, and METEOR of 0.467 across ten representative clinical case categories. The system achieves an Expected Calibration Error (ECE) of 0.275 and passes the Africa Deep Tech Challenge 2026 (ADTC 2026) memory budget constraint of 7 168 MB, achieving a peak inference RAM of approximately 3 630 MB on the standardised benchmark laptop. These results demonstrate the feasibility of deploying large language model-based clinical reasoning at the primary care level in resource-constrained settings without cloud infrastructure.

</details>


### [30] [AdaKP: Online Adaptive Knowledge-Point Selection for Reasoning-Oriented Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24833)
*Zibin Meng,Zhenyu Zhao,Chunqiang Run*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdaKP is an online selector that dynamically chooses which atomic knowledge points (KPs) to inject during RL training for math problem-solving, using an entropy reduction proxy with lightweight mechanisms, improving performance over static selection baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards suffers from severe reward sparsity on competition-level mathematics problems. While injecting knowledge points (KPs) helps, existing methods either use fixed offline selection or just scale the quantity of text, ignoring the critical choice of which specific KPs to inject and when during training.

Method: AdaKP introduces an online selector that dynamically re-chooses KP subsets during RL training. Core innovations: (1) An entropy proxy scoring KPs by next-token entropy reduction via inexpensive forward passes with provable bias bounds; (2) Three lightweight mechanisms: momentum smoother for noise reduction, retirement-and-revival manager to prune weak KPs while maintaining exploration, adaptive scheduler to front-load early training re-evaluations; (3) Pre-flight validation gate that certifies the proxy against leave-one-out ground truth before expensive runs.

Result: AdaKP improves over strong static-selection baselines on all eight competition-mathematics benchmarks at negligible added computational cost. The method is implemented as a fully additive fork of standard DAPO+GRPO trainer without optimizer changes.

Conclusion: Online, validated KP-subset selection represents a practical and previously underexplored axis for improving reasoning-oriented reinforcement learning in mathematical domains, offering efficient dynamic selection of the most informative knowledge points during training.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is a powerful paradigm for eliciting reasoning in large language models, yet it suffers from severe reward sparsity on competition-level mathematics. A common remedy injects atomic knowledge points (KPs) - short natural-language hints distilled from gold solutions - into the prompt. Existing methods, however, either fix this selection once offline or merely scale the monolithic quantity of injected text, leaving untouched the most informative axis of choice: which subset of atomic KPs to inject, and when. We introduce AdaKP, an online selector that re-chooses each problem's KP subset over the course of RL training. At its core is an entropy proxy that scores a KP by the reduction in next-token entropy it induces - a single inexpensive forward pass, with a provable bound on its truncation bias - in place of expensive rollout-based estimation. Three lightweight mechanisms make this signal usable online: a momentum smoother that absorbs per-step noise, a retirement-and-revival manager that prunes weak KPs while preserving exploration, and an adaptive scheduler that front-loads re-evaluations into early training. AdaKP further contributes a pre-flight validation gate that certifies the proxy against a leave-one-out ground truth before any expensive run is launched, turning method-level risk into a falsifiable check. Realized as a fully additive fork of a standard DAPO+GRPO trainer with no optimizer changes, AdaKP improves over a strong static-selection baseline on all eight competition-mathematics benchmarks at negligible added cost, positioning online, validated KP-subset selection as a practical and as-yet under-explored axis for reasoning-oriented reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [31] [MusiChat: Vibe Composing for Music Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24873)
*Callie C. Liao,Duoduo Liao,Ellie L. Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MusiChat is a conversational AI system for interactive music composition that enables iterative refinement through natural language dialogue, rather than regenerating compositions from scratch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI music generation systems follow a "prompt-and-regenerate" paradigm that makes iterative refinement difficult, forcing users to recreate compositions instead of evolving existing musical ideas.

Method: Hierarchical controllable music generation framework separating lyric-aligned structure generation from expressive surface realization, integrated with LLM through memory-augmented architecture, and hybrid intent-routing mechanism for precise musical edits and open-ended creative requests.

Result: Achieved 95.31% accuracy for single-turn and 100% for multi-turn interactions, with like-to-dislike ratios of 2:1 for melody naturalness and 3:1 for musical quality, supporting coherent multi-turn music authoring.

Conclusion: MusiChat successfully enables interactive human-AI co-creation through conversational interfaces by incrementally transforming evolving musical artifacts while preserving structure and intent.

Abstract: Recent advances in AI music generation have enabled users to create complete musical pieces from natural-language prompts. However, most existing systems follow a prompt-and-regenerate paradigm, making iterative refinement difficult because users must repeatedly recreate compositions instead of directly evolving existing musical ideas. We present MusiChat, a conversational vibe composing system that enables collaborative human-AI music creation through natural-language interaction and iterative refinement. At the core of MusiChat is a hierarchical controllable music generation framework that separates lyric-aligned musical structure generation from expressive surface realization, allowing flexible stylistic transformations and structure-preserving edits. The system integrates a large language model with a hybrid symbolic music engine through a memory-augmented architecture that maintains the active composition state and user history across interactions. A hybrid intent-routing mechanism further enables efficient interpretation of both precise musical edits and open-ended creative requests. Rather than regenerating compositions from scratch, MusiChat incrementally transforms an evolving musical artifact while preserving relevant musical structure and user intent. We evaluate MusiChat through objective analysis and human studies, achieving 95.31% and 100% accuracy for single- and multi-turn interactions, respectively, and obtaining like-to-dislike ratios of 2:1 for melody naturalness and 3:1 for musical quality. Our results demonstrate that MusiChat supports coherent multi-turn music authoring and interactive human-AI co-creation through a conversational interface.

</details>


### [32] [Understanding Semantic IDs: From Item Representation to Item Selection in Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24995)
*Junting Wang,Xinrui He,Yunzhe Li,Hari Sundaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Semantic IDs in generative recommendation systems lose fine-grained local structure, causing significant target loss during generation; Item-Supported Decoding improves results by supporting SID prefixes with user-specific item rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic IDs (SIDs) serve three roles in generative recommendation systems but current implementations lose much of the encoder's fine local structure while their exact tokens aren't determined solely by item meaning, which negatively impacts generation quality.

Method: Systematic investigation of SIDs across three Amazon domains and eight SID constructions, analyzing item encoding, SID construction, autoregressive generation, and recommendation. Proposed Item-Supported Decoding (ISD) - a lightweight inference-time method that uses user-specific item rankings to support SID prefixes before beam search discards them.

Result: SID neighborhoods recover only 32.2% of encoder's nearest neighbors; alternative descriptions change 38.4% of exact SIDs; after final semantic token, TIGER retains only 29.9% of plausible recommendations. ISD improves NDCG@10 in all evaluated settings with up to 31.2% relative gains.

Conclusion: SIDs provide useful coarse item organization but their fine boundaries shouldn't alone determine which items remain available during generation; ISD effectively leverages both SID structure and user preferences to improve recommendation quality without additional training.

Abstract: Semantic IDs (SIDs) are now a central component of generative recommendation. Current SID-based systems assign three roles to the same token sequence. Shared prefixes are intended to organize related items, the complete SID identifies an individual item, and each generated token narrows the items that can still be returned. We systematically investigate SIDs from item encoding and SID construction to autoregressive generation and final recommendation. We examine how SID construction changes item representations and how those changes affect generation. Across three Amazon domains and eight SID constructions, SID neighborhoods recover only 32.2% of the encoder's ten nearest neighbors on average. Alternative item descriptions still retrieve the corresponding item first in 99.57% of controlled cases, yet change 38.4% of exact SIDs. These results show that SIDs retain broad organization but lose much of the encoder's fine local structure, while their exact tokens are not determined by item meaning alone. This loss becomes consequential during generation. After the final semantic token, TIGER retains only 29.9% of held-out targets that were plausible recommendations before SID filtering. Motivated by these findings, we propose Item-Supported Decoding (ISD), a lightweight inference-time method that allows a user-specific item ranking to support corresponding SID prefixes before beam search discards them. The same ranking then orders the generated items. ISD requires no additional parameters or retraining of the SID constructor or decoder. We empirically show that ISD improves NDCG@10 over the corresponding SID backbone in every evaluated setting, with relative gains of up to 31.2%. Our results show that SIDs provide useful coarse item organization, but their fine boundaries should not alone determine which items remain available during generation.

</details>


### [33] [Localized Anomaly Detection via Differentiable D-vine Copulas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25020)
*Nicholas Andrea Pearson,Francesca Zanello,Davide Russo,Luca Bortolussi,Francesca Cairoli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel gradient-based beam search method for fitting D-vine copulas that enables more comprehensive exploration of configuration space, combined with an interpretable anomaly detection framework using hierarchical decomposition for localization and uncertainty quantification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vine copula fitting methods use greedy sequential decisions that commit to locally optimal choices at each step, potentially discarding configurations that would yield better global fit due to the combinatorial explosion of possible configurations.

Method: 1) Differentiable D-vine implementation enabling gradient-based maximum likelihood estimation, 2) Beam-search strategy maintaining multiple competing configurations throughout fitting, 3) Localized anomaly detection exploiting hierarchical decomposition for edge-level explanations, 4) Mondrian conformal prediction for statistical guarantees.

Result: The framework allows broader exploration of configuration space while remaining computationally tractable, enabling both global anomaly scores and edge-level explanations with uncertainty quantification through conformal prediction.

Conclusion: The proposed framework demonstrates effectiveness for interpretable anomaly detection with uncertainty quantification on benchmark and real-world datasets, overcoming limitations of existing greedy fitting procedures for vine copulas.

Abstract: Vine copulas provide a flexible framework for modeling complex multivariate distributions through a hierarchical decomposition into bivariate pair-copulas. Fitting a D-vine requires selecting a copula family and parameter configuration for each pair-copula from a set of candidates encoding different dependence patterns. As the number of variables and candidate families increases, the number of possible configurations grows combinatorially. Existing fitting procedures address this challenge through sequential greedy decisions, committing to a single locally optimal family at each step and potentially discarding configurations that would yield a better global fit. To overcome this limitation, we propose a novel estimation framework that combines gradient-based maximum likelihood estimation, enabled by our fully differentiable implementation, with a beam-search strategy that maintains multiple competing D-vine configurations throughout the fitting process. This allows a broader exploration of the configuration space while remaining computationally tractable. Building on the fitted D-vine, we introduce a localized anomaly detection framework that exploits the hierarchical decomposition to produce both global anomaly scores and edge-level explanations. Statistical guarantees are provided through Mondrian conformal prediction, while the pair-copula structure enables the localization of anomalies to specific variable relationships. We evaluate the proposed framework on both benchmark and real-world datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness for interpretable anomaly detection with uncertainty quantification.

</details>


### [34] [SAFAARI: Schema-Aware Framework for Accelerated Advertiser Response Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25042)
*Bhanu Teja Rangaraju,Chandan Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAFAARI is a multi-agent framework that automates schema linking in NL-to-SQL systems using specialized agents, achieving 81.66% on the novel SEAL metric with 6.65% improvement over baseline and 8x reduction in development time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic chatbots face limitations accessing enterprise data without predefined APIs, creating a bottleneck in customer support systems that require efficient natural language to SQL conversion.

Method: A multi-agent framework (SAFAARI) with specialized content, metadata, and orchestration agents to address schema linking bottlenecks, plus a novel composite metric (SEAL) for holistic evaluation.

Result: Achieved 81.66% SEAL score (6.65% improvement), with 5.51% datapoint accuracy gain and 4.69% schema-linking precision improvement. Human-in-the-loop validation shows adaptability across domains and 8x development time reduction.

Conclusion: SAFAARI effectively automates schema linking and query generation, streamlining API development and enhancing self-service capabilities for customer support enterprises with complex data ecosystems.

Abstract: The evolution of customer support systems is rapidly advancing with agentic chatbots, yet these systems face significant limitations when accessing enterprise data without predefined API endpoints. This paper presents SAFAARI (Schema-Aware Framework for Accelerated Advertiser Response Intelligence), a multi-agent framework that addresses the critical bottleneck of schema linking in Natural Language to SQL (NL-to-SQL) systems through specialized content, metadata, and orchestration agents. We also introduce SEAL (Schema Evaluation and Accuracy in Language-to-SQL), a novel composite metric that holistically evaluates system performance while penalizing inconsistent results. Through systematic experimentation with five feature set configurations, SAFAARI achieves an 81.66% SEAL score (6.65% improvement over baseline), with notable gains in datapoint accuracy (5.51%) and schema-linking precision (4.69%). The framework's effectiveness is validated through human-in-the-loop evaluation with domain experts, which proves its adaptability across diverse support domains. By automating the labor-intensive process of schema linking and query generation, our framework demonstrates 8x reduction in development time while maintaining high accuracy. The solution streamlines API development and enhances self-service capabilities, particularly benefiting customer support enterprises with complex data ecosystems.

</details>


### [35] [CogEEGAgent: Toward Autonomous Cognitive EEG Analysis with Grounded Execution and Selection-Aware Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25045)
*Dengzhe Hou,Lingyu Jiang,Fangzhou Lin,Kazunori D Yamada*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CogEEGAgent is an LLM-powered cognitive-EEG analysis agent that combines flexible natural language understanding with fail-closed scientific controls to prevent adaptive search bias and ensure valid confirmatory analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG analysis requires specialized expertise and involves many defensible choices. While LLM agents can translate natural language questions into analysis choices, fluent reports alone cannot guarantee that agents select requested analyses independently of adaptive search or properly evaluate confirmatory claims. There's a need for automated systems that combine flexible language understanding with fail-closed control over inference and release.

Method: CogEEGAgent uses a scientific harness grounded in MNE-Python that separates semantic from scientific authority. The LLM interprets intent and proposes registered analyses, while deterministic components validate typed contracts, control confirmation access, and authorize evidence-bound release. The system includes prespecified routing benchmarks, external model-authored outcome-blind campaigns, and policy stress testing to validate performance.

Result: CogEEGAgent maps language to registered analyses more accurately than matched deterministic routers. In external outcome-blind campaigns, it releases supported analyses with participant-disjoint confirmation while blocking prespecified capability hazards and lifecycle-reuse requests. Policy stress testing shows held-out confirmation curbs false positives from uncorrected adaptive search. Both systems abstain whenever required through matched preflight controls.

Conclusion: CogEEGAgent establishes bounded autonomy and an auditable automation framework for cognitive-EEG workflows. More broadly, it demonstrates how scientific agents can combine flexible language understanding with fail-closed control over inference and release, addressing concerns about adaptive search bias in automated scientific analysis systems.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) analysis in cognitive studies requires specialized expertise and involves many defensible choices over contrasts, channels, time windows, and statistical tests. LLM agents can translate varied natural-language questions into analysis choices, offering a flexible interface for automation. Yet fluent reports alone cannot establish that an agent selected the requested analysis or evaluated a confirmatory claim independently of adaptive search. We present CogEEGAgent, a cognitive-EEG analysis agent grounded in MNE-Python. Its EEG-specific scientific harness separates semantic from scientific authority. The LLM interprets intent and proposes registered analyses, while deterministic components validate typed contracts, control confirmation access, and authorize evidence-bound release. On a prespecified routing benchmark, CogEEGAgent maps language to registered analyses more accurately than a matched deterministic router, while matched preflight makes both systems abstain whenever required. In an externally model-authored, outcome-blind campaign, the complete system releases supported analyses with participant-disjoint confirmation and blocks prespecified capability hazards and lifecycle-reuse requests. Policy stress testing shows that held-out confirmation curbs false positives from uncorrected adaptive search. Together, these studies establish bounded autonomy and an auditable automation framework for cognitive-EEG workflows. More broadly, they show how scientific agents can combine flexible language understanding with fail-closed control over inference and release.

</details>


### [36] [Psychological Influences of Conversational AI: Research and Design Directions for Reducing Harm and Promoting Well-Being](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25057)
*Jina Suh,Mihaela Vorvoreanu,Forough Poursabzi-Sangdeh,Emily Tseng,Eugenia Kim,Luke Nicholls,James W. Pennebaker,Eric Horvitz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes directions for designing general-purpose AI systems to reduce psychological harms and support user well-being, acknowledging both benefits and risks of conversational AI while framing proposals as testable hypotheses for future research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As conversational AI systems become more integrated into daily life, there's growing concern about their potential psychological effects on users. While these systems offer benefits like improved information access, learning, and companionship, they also introduce risks including emotional entanglement, unhealthy dependence, and amplification of psychological vulnerabilities.

Method: The paper draws on prior research and empirical observations of AI chatbot behavior to propose aspirational directions for guiding AI system behavior. The authors frame these directions as hypotheses for studying how AI behavior influences users across general interactions, role-playing scenarios, and contexts resembling psychological support.

Result: The paper presents a set of proposed directions for AI system design to reduce potential psychological harms and support user well-being. Some directions are supported by existing research and expert insights, while others identify open questions requiring deeper study.

Conclusion: The authors hope their formulation encourages further discussion, empirical investigation, and exploration of interactive design approaches aimed at better accommodating users' psychological needs and promoting their well-being. They acknowledge the difficulty of assessing long-term impacts and position their proposals as testable hypotheses rather than definitive guidelines.

Abstract: As conversational AI systems become increasingly integrated into daily life, their potential effects on user well-being require ongoing attention. While consumer-facing generalist models can provide benefits, including improved access to information, learning, productivity, self-reflection, and companionship, they also introduce risks, such as emotional entanglement, unhealthy dependence, and the amplification of psychological vulnerabilities. Drawing on prior research and empirical observations of AI chatbot behavior, we propose a set of aspirational directions for guiding the behavior of general-purpose AI systems in ways that may reduce potential psychological harms and support user well-being. We acknowledge the difficulty of systematically assessing the long-term impacts of AI chatbot use and frame these directions as hypotheses for studying how AI behavior may influence users across general interactions, role-playing scenarios, and contexts that could be characterized as providing psychological support. While some proposed directions are supported by existing research and expert insights, others identify open questions and areas requiring deeper study. We hope that this formulation and these hypotheses encourage further discussion, empirical investigation, and exploration of interactive design approaches aimed at better accommodating users' psychological needs and promoting their well-being.

</details>


### [37] [Similar Models Learn Differently: Final-Window Pretraining Shapes Post-Training Beyond SFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25063)
*Cen Lu,Yung-Chen Tang,Andrea Cavallaro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The order of pretraining data matters: models with safety text last maintain refusal behavior after alignment, while other data sources don't provide similar protection, even when benchmarks show identical post-SFT performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current practice judges model checkpoints based on post-SFT benchmark performance, assuming similar-performing checkpoints are interchangeable for further alignment. This overlooks potential "pretraining imprint" - hidden differences from the final pretraining data that affect how models respond to subsequent alignment training.

Method: Controlled experiment: take one partially pretrained checkpoint and create six branches that differ only in the final 500M tokens (0.1-1% of total pretraining). Each branch trains on different data sources: generic web text, filtered web text, normative discourse, safety text, mathematical text, or synthetic educational text. Then apply identical SFT and post-training (both direct preference optimization and RL with verifiable rewards).

Result: After SFT, all branches perform nearly identically (within ~1 point on instruction following, refusal, and capability). However, after identical post-training, they diverge dramatically in refusal behavior: only the safety-text-last branch maintains refusal of harmful requests, while others lose refusal capability. The protection effect is selective to safety text being last and reproduces across model families.

Conclusion: Models should not be evaluated solely on post-SFT benchmark performance. The final pretraining data creates a hidden "imprint" that determines how models respond to alignment training. Checkpoints should be accompanied by information about what they were trained on last, as this significantly impacts alignment outcomes.

Abstract: Developers judge a model checkpoint by how it behaves. After supervised fine-tuning (SFT), two checkpoints that perform about the same across relevant benchmarks are treated as interchangeable, equally ready for the next alignment stage, typically preference optimization. We ask whether this judgment misses a pretraining imprint: a difference that no post-SFT benchmark reveals, yet that decides how each checkpoint responds to further training. To find out, we run a controlled experiment on the final window of pretraining, the last data trained on before instruction tuning. Six branches fork from one partially pretrained checkpoint and differ only in this window: 500 million tokens, 0.1% to 1% of the tokens that precede it. Each branch trains its window on a single data source: generic web text, filtered web text, normative discourse, safety text, mathematical text, or synthetic educational text. SFT and post-training are then identical. After SFT the branches behave near-identically, within about one point on instruction following, refusal, and capability, yet the same post-training carries them to very different endpoints, under both a direct preference optimization update and a reinforcement learning update with a verifiable reward. We measure this deviation through refusal of harmful requests: when post-training begins the safety text branch refuses no more than the web text branch, yet by the end it has lost far less of its refusal. The other four branches gain little or no protection, so the effect is selective to what the window contained. The protection requires the safety text to arrive last rather than earlier in pretraining, and it reproduces on a second model family. What a model is pretrained on last shapes how it reacts to alignment. Therefore, a checkpoint should not be evaluated by its post-SFT behavior alone, and what it was trained on last should be reported with it.

</details>


### [38] [Addressable Recall Compaction for Long Context-Window Control in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25066)
*Thang Dang,Yuma Ichikawa,Sakina Fatima,Koichi Shirahata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARC is a context management framework for LLM agents that separates archival storage from active context, allowing efficient retrieval of past tool observations through addressable citations rather than traditional summarization or retrieval methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate extensive reasoning traces, actions, and tool observations that exceed fixed context windows. Existing compaction methods (discarding, summarizing, or retrieving) risk losing task-critical details or fail to reliably recover needed information.

Method: ARC separates archival storage from active-context presentation, storing tool observations in an append-only, ID-addressable log. It replaces older observations with compact citations when compaction is needed, allowing agents to retrieve stored content using identifiers without re-executing tools or relying solely on similarity-based retrieval.

Result: On Needle-in-a-Haystack evaluation: 99.40% accuracy vs 88.12% for best baseline. Reduces estimated serving time and HBM traffic. On LongBench-v2 Hard subset: 29.97% accuracy vs 28.25% for best baseline.

Conclusion: Explicit, address-based recall improves information retention and serving efficiency compared to evaluated context-management baselines, demonstrating that structured archival with addressable citations outperforms traditional compaction methods for long-horizon LLM agents.

Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate reasoning traces, actions, and tool observations that can eventually exceed a model's fixed context window. Existing compaction methods address this limitation by discarding, summarizing, or retrieving earlier information, but they may remove task-critical details or fail to recover them reliably. We propose ARC (Addressable Recall Compaction), a context-management framework that separates archival storage from active-context presentation. ARC stores tool observations in an append-only, ID-addressable log and replaces older observations with compact citations when compaction is required. The agent can subsequently use these identifiers to request stored content without re-executing the corresponding tools or depending solely on similarity-based retrieval. We evaluate ARC using Qwen3-8B with a 16k context window and Qwen3-32B with a 32k context window. On the Needle-in-a-Haystack evaluation, ARC achieves an average exact-answer accuracy of 99.40%, compared with 88.12% for the best-performing baseline in our evaluation. ARC also reduces estimated serving time and HBM traffic under our hardware-cost model. On the LongBench-v2 Hard subset, ARC obtains an average accuracy of 29.97%, compared with 28.25% for the best-performing baseline. These results indicate that explicit, address-based recall can improve information retention and serving efficiency relative to the evaluated context-management baselines under the tested settings.

</details>


### [39] [How Often Should a Recommender Call an LLM? Value-Weighted Routing, Monitoring, and Seasonal Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25068)
*Bhavtosh Rath*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Value Router uses difficulty and business value estimates (not ground truth) for LLM routing decisions, showing value-weighting improves precision over difficulty-only approaches and revealing aggregate metrics can hide between-category discrimination failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM routing approaches focus only on difficulty, ignoring that difficult cheap items and difficult costly items have different costs of error. Business value should be considered alongside difficulty for cost-aware routing decisions.

Method: Three-stage synthetic simulation: 1) Value-weighted threshold router vs difficulty-only baseline on inverse-correlation catalog, 2) Decision logger/monitor to reveal hidden failure modes, 3) Simulated Black Friday demand surge with static/tuned routers and budget policies.

Result: Value-weighting matched difficulty-only recall (60%) while achieving higher precision (98.3% vs 94.3%). Aggregate metrics hid that results were driven by between-category differences, not per-item discrimination. Seasonal tuning and budget policies handled demand surges.

Conclusion: Difficulty and business value are distinct axes for routing decisions. Value-aware routing can improve precision, but aggregate metrics can mask discrimination failures. Design principles emphasize cost-awareness, monitoring, and handling seasonal variations.

Abstract: Routing decisions between a cheap heuristic and an expensive large language model (LLM) are typically framed as a difficulty problem: send the hard cases to the expensive path. We argue this framing is incomplete because difficulty and business value are distinct axes - a difficult cheap item and a difficult costly item do not have the same cost of error. We present Value Router, a fully synthetic simulation of a retail merchandising pipeline that routes items using only estimated difficulty and estimated value, never ground truth. The study has three stages. First, a value-weighted threshold router is compared with a difficulty-only and a random baseline on a synthetic catalog with an inverse correlation between category volume and value. Value-weighting matches the difficulty-only baseline's recall of true high-value items (60%) while achieving substantially higher precision (98.3% vs. 94.3%). Second, a decision logger and monitor expose a failure mode hidden by aggregate metrics showing that the aggregate result is driven almost entirely by between-category differences rather than per-item discrimination. Third, a simulated Black Friday demand surge (2.5 volume with a shift toward higher-value categories) compares a static router, a seasonally tuned router, and two slow-path budget policies. All results are from a controlled synthetic simulation with experimenter-defined ground truth and illustrate design principles for cost-aware routing systems rather than validated real-world claims.

</details>


### [40] [Towards an Agent Operating System - Lessons from Classical and Cloud OS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25076)
*Gosia Steinder,Hubertus Franke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic AI systems need standardized abstractions similar to POSIX for classical OS and Kubernetes for cloud, as current fragmented frameworks prevent portable applications and reliable composition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems (autonomous LLM-driven agents) are in an experimentation phase with dozens of competing frameworks but no community consensus on core abstractions or guarantees. This fragmentation prevents portable application development, reliable platform composition, and advancement beyond prototype deployments.

Method: Propose following the methodology of prior platform waves (POSIX for OS, Kubernetes for cloud): derive new agentic abstractions by extending classical OS and cloud OS primitives to stochastic, natural-language-mediated execution, specify their semantics precisely, and consolidate around them.

Result: The paper argues for a systematic approach to standardization rather than continuing with fragmented frameworks, advocating for community consensus on core abstractions with well-defined semantics.

Conclusion: The agentic AI field must follow historical patterns of platform evolution by establishing standardized abstractions to enable portable applications, reliable platform composition, and move beyond prototype stages to mature deployment.

Abstract: Every major wave of platform software follows the same arc: an initial period of experimentation with competing frameworks and ad-hoc implementations, followed by the articulation of a small set of stable abstractions with well-defined semantics, and finally consolidation around those abstractions into a platform that applications can portably target. POSIX did this for classical operating systems; Kubernetes did it for the cloud. Agentic AI systems - autonomous, LLM-driven agents that plan, use tools, maintain memory, and collaborate - are currently in the experimentation phase of the third such wave. dozens of frameworks and protocols have emerged, but no community consensus exists on what the core abstractions are or what guarantees they carry. Without that consensus, agentic applications cannot be written portably, platforms cannot compose reliably, and the field cannot advance beyond prototype deployments. We argue that the path forward is to follow the prior-wave methodology: derive new agentic abstractions by extending classical OS and cloud OS primitives to stochastic, natural-language-mediated execution, specify their semantics precisely, and consolidate around them - just as POSIX and Kubernetes consolidated their respective waves.

</details>


### [41] [Matryoshka Agent: Unfolding Sub-Agents for Long-Horizon Machine Learning Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25090)
*Rushi Qiang,Changhao Li,Haotian Sun,Yuchen Zhuang,Chao Zhang,Bo Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Matryoshka Agent is a hierarchical framework that decomposes complex machine learning engineering tasks into coordinated orchestration and execution layers, enabling efficient long-horizon problem solving through strategic exploration separated from costly execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning engineering tasks require long-horizon decision making in expensive feedback-driven environments, but developing monolithic agents for such tasks is challenging due to long/noisy contexts, vast solution spaces, and limited model capacity/computational budgets.

Method: Matryoshka Agent uses a hierarchical approach with an Orchestrator maintaining compact exploration states and issuing strategic instructions, while Sub-Agents execute concrete solutions through environment interaction via standardized Tool interfaces, decoupling strategy from execution.

Result: The framework demonstrates effectiveness across diverse MLE tasks and model types/scales, enabling Qwen3-4B-Instruct to reach Orchestrator performance comparable to o4-mini, and achieving up to 36.7% relative performance gain with Qwen3-30B-Coder.

Conclusion: Matryoshka Agent provides an effective and scalable paradigm for long-horizon MLE tasks and complex agentic problem solving through hierarchical decomposition that reduces long-context reasoning burdens and enables efficient iterative refinement.

Abstract: Machine learning engineering (MLE) tasks require long-horizon decision making over iterative solution debugging and refinement, under expensive and feedback-driven environment interactions. Developing and training a monolithic agent for such tasks is fundamentally challenging, as it must simultaneously manage extremely long and noisy contexts, explore vast solution spaces, and remain effective under limited model capacity and computational budgets. To address these challenges, we propose Matryoshka Agent, a unified hierarchical agent framework for complex long-horizon tasks. Matryoshka Agent decomposes agentic problem solving into a coordinated hierarchy of decision making and execution: a high-level Orchestrator maintains compact, long-horizon exploration states and issues strategic instructions, while lower-level Sub-Agents execute concrete solution attempts through direct environment interaction, mediated by standardized Tool interface. This design decouples strategic exploration from costly execution, substantially reducing the burden of long-context reasoning and enabling efficient iterative refinement. We further develop an efficient training paradigm for Matryoshka Agent. Experimental results on a broad range of MLE tasks with diverse model types and scales demonstrate that Matryoshka Agent is an effective and scalable paradigm for long-horizon MLE tasks and complex agentic problem solving. Notably, Matryoshka Agent enables Qwen3-4B-Instruct to reach Orchestrator performance comparable to o4-mini. Applying Matryoshka Agent to Qwen3-30B-Coder results in at most 36.7% relative performance gain.

</details>


### [42] [Towards Robust Reinforcement Learning for Small-Scale Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25091)
*Md Rezwanul Haque,Md. Milon Islam,Fakhri Karray*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematically investigates RL alignment failure modes in small language models (70-500M params), identifies three reproducible failure mechanisms, proposes solutions, and introduces a capacity-headroom hypothesis showing PPO performance depends on model fluency and reward quality rather than parameter count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning alignment for small language models (70-500M parameters) is often considered unstable, but the underlying failure mechanisms haven't been systematically investigated. The paper aims to understand why RL alignment fails with SLMs and provide reproducible solutions.

Method: Trained 15 (model, corpus) configurations using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with Pythia-70M/160M/410M and SmolLM2-135M/360M on TinyStories, CNN/DailyMail, and Wikitext-103. Identified three failure modes: silent LoRA parameter freezing, numerical overflow in bfloat16 importance ratios, and catastrophic policy collapse from reward-model error. Proposed solutions include merge-and-reinitialize adapter technique, float32 precision for PPO updates, and three-layer safety mechanism (reward whitening, importance-ratio guarding, weight rollback).

Result: The proposed system converged stably in all experiments and improved preference win rate over supervised fine-tuning baselines in configurations with fluent prior (PPL<20) and informative reward signal. Outperformed instruction-tuned baselines while requiring significantly less training data. All code, models, and data publicly released.

Conclusion: Proposes the capacity-headroom hypothesis: PPO performance at SLM scale depends on model fluency (PPL<20) and discriminative reward signal quality, not just parameter count. Provides practical solutions to make RL alignment stable for small language models, enabling more accessible and efficient preference alignment.

Abstract: The alignment of Small Language Models (SLMs) in the 70--500M parameter range using reinforcement learning is often considered unstable, though the underlying failure mechanisms have not been systematically investigated. In the State-of-the-Art (SOTA) research, fifteen (model, corpus) configurations were trained using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). The experiments included Pythia-70M, 160M, 410M and SmolLM2-135M, 360M on the TinyStories, CNN/DailyMail, and Wikitext-103 corpora. Three reproducible failure modes were identified in small-scale language models: silent LoRA parameter freezing in standard PEFT/TRL pipelines, numerical overflow in importance ratios when using bfloat16, and catastrophic policy collapse due to reward-model error. These issues were addressed using a merge-and-reinitialize adapter technique, float32 precision during PPO updates, and a three-layer safety mechanism comprising reward whitening, importance-ratio guarding, and weight rollback. In this paper, a capacity-headroom hypothesis is proposed, which states that PPO performance at the SLM scale depends on both a fluent supervised model ($\text{PPL}<20$) and a discriminative reward signal, rather than on the number of model parameters. The proposed system converged stably in all experiments and improved preference win rate over the SFT baseline in configurations with a fluent prior and an informative reward signal. Furthermore, it outperformed instruction-tuned baselines while requiring significantly less training data. All checkpoints, preference datasets, and training scripts are publicly released$^§$.

</details>


### [43] [ScalableRAG: High-Quality RAG at Zero Ingestion Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25135)
*Hilaf Hasson,Aditya Chakravarty,Jayant Thomas,Krishna Gogineni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ScalableRAG introduces zero-ingestion and limited-ingestion approaches for RAG that eliminate or minimize knowledge ingestion costs while maintaining or improving performance over traditional knowledge graph methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG systems incur high ingestion costs by building knowledge graphs or extracting SQL tables for optimal performance. The authors aim to achieve similar or better performance with minimal or zero ingestion costs.

Method: Zero-Ingestion ScalableRAG uses a workspace of document sets and value sets for on-the-fly aggregative reasoning without any database. Limited-Ingestion ScalableRAG adds a minimal vector database and automated pattern discovery from document samples while capping LLM calls by a constant independent of corpus size.

Result: Zero-Ingestion ScalableRAG outperforms all baselines (including knowledge graph approaches) in 3 out of 6 corpora and comes close on the other 3, with average accuracy 7.36% above the next best baseline. Limited-Ingestion version further improves accuracy at scale.

Conclusion: High-performance RAG can be achieved with minimal or zero ingestion costs through intelligent workspace management and on-the-fly reasoning, challenging the conventional wisdom that knowledge graphs or SQL tables are necessary for optimal RAG performance.

Abstract: Recent advances in RAG aim to optimize for performance by paying high ingestion costs for knowledge ingestion: building knowledge graphs or extracting SQL tables. In this work we show that the operations that such knowledge bases allow can be replicated with zero ingestion costs (not even a vector database); in fact our solution, Zero-Ingestion ScalableRAG, handily out-performs all baselines (including knowledge graph approaches) in three out of the six corpora considered here, and only marginally missing maximum performance on the other three, with average accuracy across all six datasets 7.36% above the next most competitive baseline. It achieves this by keeping a workspace of document sets and values sets that it can write into and read from, allowing for on-the-fly aggregative reasoning in all situations where grouping is required on a primary key that is in one to one correspondence with a subset of the total document set.
  Capping the number of LLM calls by a constant independent of the corpus size, we also introduce Limited-Ingestion ScalableRAG, which does use a minimal vector database as well as an automated pattern discovery from a sample of documents, to further improve accuracy at scale. Our code is available at https://github.com/cohesity/ScalableRAG .

</details>


### [44] [Less Data, Better Alignment: Data-Centric Multi-Evaluator Agreement for Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25136)
*Zhengtao Yao,Runhao Li,Xupeng Chen,Jiayi Cheng,Chenqian Le,Michael Yue,Siheng Wang,Haoyan Xu,Yuqi Li,Chenhao Wei,Zhengdao Li,Rongchao Zhang,Guang Yang,Yidong Wang,Junhao Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DMAPO is a data-centric preference optimization method that filters candidate responses using multiple specialized evaluators to keep only high-consensus examples, achieving strong performance with just 3.45% of generated data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most preference optimization research focuses on changing training objectives while keeping data fixed. Instead, the authors explore whether a small, high-confidence set of on-policy responses can provide a reliable learning signal for preference optimization.

Method: DMAPO generates candidate responses from the target policy, evaluates them using specialized evaluators for helpfulness, factuality, and conciseness, applies process-critic corrections, and retains only high-consensus desirable or undesirable examples.

Result: The method accepted only 3.45% of candidates (1,871 of 54,236). When trained with KTO on this filtered set, it achieved: 7.50 on MT-Bench, 95.5% length-controlled win rate against text-davinci-003, and 57.3% IFEval prompt accuracy. It outperformed SimPO significantly in pairwise evaluations across multiple evaluators and datasets.

Conclusion: Consensus filtering offers a data-efficient route to preference optimization for general instructions, though it requires additional curation compute and depends on evaluator judgments. The approach is robust to changes in evaluator models or rubrics.

Abstract: Research on preference optimization often varies the training objective while holding the data fixed. We instead ask whether a small, high-confidence set of on-policy responses can provide a reliable learning signal. Our method, DMAPO (Data-centric Multi-evaluator Agreement for Preference Optimization), generates candidate responses from the target policy, evaluates helpfulness, factuality, and conciseness with rubric-specialized evaluators, applies a process-critic correction, and retains only high-consensus desirable or undesirable examples. This procedure accepts 1,871 of 54,236 Mistral-7B candidates (3.45%). KTO trained on this set reaches 7.50 on MT-Bench, 95.5% length-controlled win rate against a text-davinci-003 reference, and 57.3% IFEval prompt accuracy. Independent pairwise evaluation also favors DMAPO over SimPO: GPT-4o yields a net win rate of 23.3 points on 129 held-out prompts and 24.0 points on 200 out-of-distribution LMSYS-Chat prompts; Claude Opus 4.7 yields 24.1 points on the held-out set. Changing the evaluator model or rubric alters the selected examples but has little effect on downstream performance. A second-backbone study yields a similar 3.41% acceptance rate, although its performance gains are more modest. Across these experiments, consensus filtering offers a data-efficient route to preference optimization for general instructions, at the cost of additional curation compute and dependence on evaluator judgments.

</details>


### [45] [Inferring Missing Trajectory Data with Temporal Convolutional Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25147)
*Ilinca Tiriblecea,Gabriel Turinici*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Temporal Convolutional Network with symmetric dilation is proposed for trajectory inpainting, allowing reconstruction of missing trajectory segments by leveraging both past and future context, achieving good performance metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trajectory data collected in real-world settings is frequently incomplete due to sensor failure, communication loss, or occlusion, creating a need to reconstruct missing contiguous segments.

Method: Proposes a Temporal Convolutional Network (TCN) with symmetric dilation that relaxes the standard causality constraint, allowing each time step to use both past and future observations. The model is trained with a composite loss combining weighted mean squared error, boundary-continuity penalties, and smoothness regularization.

Result: The model achieves good R², MSE and MAE metrics when trained on a synthetic dataset of 1,000 (train), 200 (validation), and 300 (test) two-dimensional trajectories with randomly placed 20% masked segments.

Conclusion: The proposed symmetric dilation TCN architecture effectively addresses trajectory inpainting by leveraging both temporal directions, overcoming the limitations of forecasting-oriented architectures that only use past context.

Abstract: Trajectory data collected in real-world settings is frequently incomplete due to sensor failure, communication loss, or occlusion. We address the task of \emph{trajectory inpainting}: reconstructing contiguous missing segments from observed context. We propose a Temporal Convolutional Network (TCN) with symmetric dilation that relaxes the standard causality constraint, allowing each time step to draw on both past and future observations, a property that is essential for inpainting, but absent from forecasting-oriented architectures. The model is trained with a composite loss that combines weighted mean squared error, boundary--continuity penalties, and a smoothness regularizer. Trained on a synthetic dataset of $1,000$ (train), $200$ (validation), and $300$ (test) two-dimensional trajectories with randomly placed 20% masked segments, the model achieves good R$^{2}$, MSE and MAE metrics.

</details>


### [46] [When Do Agent Loops Mistake Stagnation for Progress? Self-Evaluation Bias and Externally Grounded Verification in Long-Running Autonomous LLM Agent Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25152)
*Hyundoo Park,Byungho Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autonomous agents exhibit self-evaluation bias called "progress mirage" where they mistakenly perceive improvement despite real-world stagnation or regression, requiring out-of-band evaluation for open-ended tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-running autonomous agents self-evaluate their work, leading to self-evaluation bias where plausible changes are accepted as progress while real-world outcomes stagnate or regress, creating a "progress mirage" problem.

Method: Built a testbed with fixed agent/tool surface, manipulating evaluator information channels. Used world-state oracle enforced by container/network isolation, measured across 54 cycles with various evaluators including self-reports, strong in-band judges (reading artifact text, change diff, verdict history), and boundary tasks.

Result: Self-report was uninformative (56% of cycles had zero or negative measured delta). Self-verdict gate accepted all, eroding best state by 19%. Strong in-band judge accepted 44% real-world regression cycles, rejected 38% of real improvements. Progress mirage vanished on boundary tasks where success is verifiable from artifact. Sign-only feedback showed benefit from gate grounding, not feedback content.

Conclusion: For open-ended objectives where success signals live outside the transcript, scaling up judges is insufficient; out-of-band evaluation with real-world access is structurally required to avoid progress mirage.

Abstract: Long-running autonomous agents plan, act, and judge their own completion without human intervention. When an agent grades its own work, self-evaluation bias takes hold: plausible changes are accepted as progress while real-world outcomes stagnate or regress. We name this failure mode the progress mirage and show, with controlled measurement, that it is a question of what the evaluator is grounded in. We built a testbed that holds the agent and its tool surface fixed and manipulates only the information-channel type of the evaluator that gates the loop. A world-state oracle, unfakeable in principle, is enforced by container and network isolation and verified at every run. Across 54 cycles a frontier agent claimed improvement every time, yet 56 percent had a measured delta of zero or below. Self-report was thus uninformative, and the self-verdict gate degenerated into accept-all, eroding the best deployed state it had reached by 19 percent. Even the strongest in-band judge, reading the full artifact text, the change diff, and its own verdict history, accepted cycles of which 44 percent were real-world regressions and rejected 38 percent of real improvements; the preregistered adversarial hypothesis that a strong judge closes the gap was rejected. On a boundary task whose success specification is verifiable from the artifact itself, the same judge's mirage vanished to zero and the gap collapsed within the registered threshold, showing that the gap depends on where the success signal resides. A sign-only variant returning only the acceptance verdict kept real-world output similar to full feedback (110.0 versus 113.0), locating the benefit in the gate's grounding rather than in feedback content. For open-ended objectives whose success signal lives outside the transcript, scaling up the judge is not enough; out-of-band evaluation with real-world access is a structural requirement.

</details>


### [47] [PreDiff-LM: Pretrained Discrete Masked Diffusion Language Modeling with Hybrid Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25157)
*Zhengtao Yao,Runhao Li,Xupeng Chen,Jiayi Cheng,Chenqian Le,Michael Yue,Jesson Wang,Siheng Wang,Guang Yang,Haoyan Xu,Chenhao Wei,Zhengqing Yuan,Youran Shen,Yanfang Ye,Junhao Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PreDiff-LM is a diffusion language model that adapts pretrained autoregressive transformers for bidirectional generation using hybrid attention - preserving causal attention for observed prompts while allowing full bidirectional attention for masked targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need to reconcile pretrained autoregressive transformers (causal attention) with bidirectional denoising in diffusion models, since discrete masked diffusion models support bidirectional generation but pretrained AR models have different attention patterns.

Method: PreDiff-LM uses hybrid attention: preserves causal attention within observed prompts while allowing full bidirectional attention within masked target regions. This attention adaptation can be combined with objective adaptation (DiffuGPT-style). The approach uses pretrained AR initialization to accelerate diffusion training.

Result: Hybrid attention improves unconditional perplexity from 34.1 to 28.7 and MAUVE from 0.71 to 0.78 over uniform bidirectional attention with same AR initialization. With objective adaptation, reaches 26.9 perplexity. Pretrained initialization reduces steps to reach perplexity below 50 from ~350K to 8K. Improves repetition, distributional quality, four zero-shot downstream tasks, and human preference over prior diffusion baselines.

Conclusion: Hybrid attention is a complementary mechanism for adapting pretrained causal backbones to diffusion models, though quality and inference-efficiency gaps remain compared to optimized AR models. The work makes explicit the trade-offs between AR and diffusion approaches.

Abstract: Discrete masked diffusion language models support bidirectional generation and infilling, but adapting pretrained autoregressive (AR) transformers requires reconciling causal pretraining with bidirectional denoising. We study this problem at the level of attention rather than claiming AR-weight reuse itself as novel. PreDiff-LM preserves causal attention within the observed prompt while allowing full bidirectional attention within the masked target. Under a matched GPT-2 Medium, WikiText-103, 90K-step setup, this hybrid mask improves unconditional perplexity from 34.1 to 28.7 and MAUVE from 0.71 to 0.78 over uniform bidirectional attention with the same AR initialization. Attention adaptation also composes with a DiffuGPT-style objective adaptation, reaching 26.9 perplexity. Pretrained initialization reduces the steps required to reach perplexity below 50 from about 350K to 8K, although a compute-matched fine-tuned AR model remains stronger at equal scale (18.9 versus 28.7). Beyond perplexity, PreDiff-LM improves repetition, distributional quality, four zero-shot downstream tasks, and human preference over prior diffusion baselines. The results position hybrid attention as a complementary mechanism for adapting pretrained causal backbones, while making explicit the remaining quality and inference-efficiency gaps to optimized AR models.

</details>


### [48] [Observing sycophantic AI validate others reduces its appeal but not its persuasiveness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25166)
*Meryl Ye,Robert Kraut,Steve Rathje*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Individual-level interventions like warnings or AI literacy videos fail to protect users from persuasive AI sycophancy, despite reducing perceived objectivity and trustworthiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI chatbots can be overly agreeable and flattering (sycophantic), which can entrench user attitudes, yet users often fail to recognize this sycophancy ("sycophancy blindness"). The researchers wanted to test whether increasing users' awareness of sycophancy would protect them from its harmful effects.

Method: The study included two preregistered experiments: Experiment 1 (n=940) gave participants a brief written warning about sycophancy before conversing with a sycophantic chatbot. Experiment 2 (n=650) had participants watch a video showing sycophantic AI validating multiple users on opposite sides of the same conflict before interacting with the AI. The researchers then pooled these experiments with two prior studies of sycophancy awareness interventions (six interventions total, n=3,982) for meta-analysis.

Result: Both interventions changed how participants evaluated the AI: the warning reduced perceived objectivity, and the video reduced enjoyment (mediated by reduced belief that validation was uniquely earned). Across all six interventions in the pooled analysis, interventions consistently made sycophantic AI appear less objective and trustworthy, but none of the six reduced its persuasiveness.

Conclusion: Individual-level interventions such as warning labels or AI literacy may be insufficient to protect users from AI harms, suggesting a need for more systemic or structural solutions.

Abstract: AI chatbots can be ``sycophantic,'' or overly agreeable and flattering toward users. Sycophantic AI has been shown to entrench attitudes, yet users frequently fail to recognize it (a phenomenon we call ``sycophancy blindness''). We tested whether increasing users' awareness of sycophancy protects them from its harmful effects. In one preregistered experiment (n = 940), participants received a brief written warning about sycophancy before conversing with a sycophantic chatbot. In a second preregistered experiment (n = 650), participants watched a video of a sycophantic AI validating several other users, including users on opposite sides of the same conflict, before interacting with it themselves. Both interventions changed how participants evaluated the AI. The warning reduced the AI's perceived objectivity, and the video reduced enjoyment of the AI, an effect mediated by the reduced belief that its validation was uniquely earned. We then pooled our experiments with two prior studies of sycophancy awareness interventions (six interventions total, n = 3,982). The pattern was consistent: interventions made the sycophantic AI appear less objective and trustworthy, and none of the six reduced its persuasiveness. These results suggest that individual-level interventions, such as warning labels or AI literacy, may not be enough to protect users from AI harms.

</details>


### [49] [Everyone is unique: Towards Behaviorally Heterogeneous Negotiation Dialogue Systems for Debt Collection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25218)
*Yuhang Yang,Kai Tang,Chao Ye,Haobo Wang,Qiqi Luo,Jinguang Zheng,Zhixin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DebtBench is the first public persona-enriched benchmark for debt collection negotiations, featuring behavioral heterogeneity, and DebtGPT is a specialized agent that matches GPT-4o's performance in this complex domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Debt collection is a critical, behaviorally rich negotiation task that serves as an excellent testbed for human-centered dialogue systems. Current benchmarks assume static, rational agents with fixed preferences, failing to capture the real-world behavioral heterogeneity inherent in debt collection negotiations.

Method: The authors propose DebtBench, a public persona-enriched debt collection benchmark that emphasizes behavioral heterogeneity in negotiation. They also develop DebtGPT, a specialized debt collection agent trained to jointly optimize both financial recovery and interaction experience.

Result: Experimental evaluation with 16 state-of-the-art LLMs shows that most existing models struggle with these complex, realistic scenarios. DebtGPT outperforms all open-source baselines and achieves performance on par with GPT-4o, demonstrating its effectiveness in debt collection negotiation.

Conclusion: DebtBench addresses a significant gap in evaluating LLMs for complex negotiation scenarios with behavioral heterogeneity, and DebtGPT represents an effective solution for debt collection that balances financial recovery with user experience. The proposed approach advances practical applications of dialogue systems in high-stakes financial contexts.

Abstract: Debt collection is a critical negotiation task in the financial industry, with strong practical relevance and exceptional academic value as a behaviorally rich, high-stakes testbed for human-centered dialogue systems. While large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in dialogue and negotiation, effectively evaluating their performance in this complex scenarios remains a major challenge: existing benchmarks uniformly assume users to be static, rational agents with fixed preferences, failing to capture the rich behavioral heterogeneity inherent in real-world debt collection. To bridge this gap, we propose DebtBench, the first public persona-enriched debt collection benchmark, that highlights behavioral heterogeneity in negotiation. Moreover, we develop DebtGPT, a debt collection agent trained to jointly optimize financial recovery and interaction experience. Our experimental results, using 16 state-of-the-art LLMs, find that most existing models struggle in this complex but realistic scenarios, whereas DebtGPT outperforms all open-source baselines and achieves performance on par with GPT-4o. The code and data are available at https://github.com/YYuHhhh/DebtNegotiation.

</details>


### [50] [CADENCE: A Cardiac Atom Dictionary for Interpretable Neural Concept Extraction from ECG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25244)
*Yixuan Duan,Arjun Naik,Sadeer Al-Kindi,Wei Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CADENCE is a framework that decomposes ECG foundation model embeddings into sparse, interpretable cardiac concepts that outperform dense representations for clinical prediction and provide human-readable physiological insights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models for 12-lead ECGs transfer well across clinical tasks but their representations remain opaque and lack interpretability of encoded physiological knowledge.

Method: Uses BatchTopK sparse autoencoder to decompose Layer-6 embeddings from over 9 million ECG tokens into 8,192 sparse cardiac atoms; employs automated LLM pipeline to generate and validate atom descriptions.

Result: Sparse atoms achieve mean AUROCs of 0.88 for clinical phenotypes and 0.90 for morphology vs 0.78/0.83 for dense dimensions; phenotype AUROC improves from 0.93 to 0.95; recovers overlapping concepts on external datasets.

Conclusion: CADENCE provides a scalable framework for discovering and auditing physiological knowledge encoded by ECG foundation models through sparse, interpretable concept decomposition.

Abstract: Foundation models for 12-lead electrocardiograms (ECGs) transfer well across clinical tasks, but the physiological knowledge encoded in their representations remains opaque.
