Conclusion: Structured supervision enables a tiny 0.9B backbone to exceed performance of much larger models, demonstrating that careful design of supervision signals is more effective than model scaling for robustness in robotic VLA tasks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models translate natural-language commands into robot action sequences, but leading systems on the LIBERO-Plus robustness benchmark use three- to seven-billion-parameter backbones whose memory demands can exceed embedded robotic budgets. We present CoTinyVLA, a 0.9B-parameter action model on a Qwen3.5-0.8B backbone that obtains that robustness by structuring supervision instead of enlarging the model. Three components target different axes of the problem: dual-view temporal input of 16 history frames per step with textual camera and time markers; hierarchical chain-of-thought (CoT) distillation from a 35B teacher into an episode-level Plan and a chunk-level Think span over task phase, gripper state and next subaction; and paraphrase augmentation expanding 40 base commands into 800 variants. On LIBERO-Plus, spanning 10,030 perturbed tasks across seven perturbation dimensions, CoTinyVLA reaches 90.8% on Spatial, 87.3% on Object, 86.6% on Goal and 80.7% on Long, leading the strongest 7B baseline on all four suites by 4.7, 2.8, 15.9 and 3.0 points, with every margin interval excluding zero. The gains concentrate on the hardest axes of the benchmark: across the eleven published baselines none exceeds 53.2% on Robot Initial States in any suite, whereas CoTinyVLA reaches 73.6% on Goal against 39.9% for the strongest baseline. Ablations show the three components to be separable by perturbation axis, and at a matched image budget how frames are divided between the two cameras and across time accounts for 8.6 points on its own. Closed-loop inference peaks at 2.25 GiB of allocated GPU memory, and paired interventions show the episode Plan to be load-bearing: replacing it with an empty or contradictory span costs 40 to 45 points of success. Structured supervision thus lets a 0.9B backbone exceed all of them. Code: https://github.com/BrainJellyPie/CoTinyVLA

</details>


### [75] [Are the High-weight Neurons the Important Ones in Image Classification Neural Networks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25529)
*Qitao Chen,Dongfu Yin,F. Richard Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: High-weight neurons don't always equate to important neurons - only about 25% overlap at best, and many low-weight neurons also significantly impact accuracy, challenging the weight-importance equivalence assumption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work lacks clarity on the relationship between neuron weights and their actual importance for tasks like pruning, backdoor defense, and interpretability. The paper addresses this gap by investigating whether high-weight neurons truly correspond to important neurons in neural networks.

Method: Three experimental approaches: 1) Quantifying overlap between high-weight neurons and accuracy-impacting neurons, 2) Analyzing effects of perturbing high-weight neurons on model accuracy, and 3) Testing accuracy recovery after ablating high-weight neurons followed by retraining. Experiments conducted on CIFAR-10 and Mini-ImageNet datasets.

Result: 1) Only ~25% overlap between top 10% high-weight neurons and important neurons, dropping further in subsequent intervals. 2) Perturbing top 10% high-weight neurons causes 45-80% accuracy degradation (vs 3-7% for random), but one-third show minimal impact. 3) Ablating top 10% prevents accuracy recovery, while top 0.1% ablation allows near-full recovery. Surprisingly, some low-weight neurons cause 10-17% degradation comparable to mid-range high-weight neurons.

Conclusion: Not all high-weight neurons are important - importance is nonlinear and doesn't directly correlate with weight magnitude. Low-weight neurons also contribute significantly to model performance. This challenges the weight-importance equivalence assumption and offers refined insights for applications like encryption (protecting critical high-weight neurons) and pruning (removing non-critical ones).

Abstract: As neural network models for image classification advance, neurons play critical roles in pruning, backdoor defense, and interpretability. Yet existing work lacks clarity on the weight-importance relationship. We address this with a neuron importance assessment method using three experiments: quantifying overlap between high-weight and accuracy-impacting neurons, analyzing high-weight neuron perturbation effects, and testing post-retraining accuracy after high-weight neuron ablation. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and Mini-ImageNet reveal key patterns. Overlap analysis shows top 10\% high-weight neurons overlap with important ones by only about 25\% at maximum, dropping further in subsequent intervals. Perturbation tests find top 10\% high-weight neurons cause 45-80\% accuracy degradation under certain operations compared to 3-7\% for random perturbations, but a third of them show minimal impact. Ablation-retraining results show removing top 10\% high-weight neurons leaves accuracy 10-20\% below baseline with no recovery, while ablating top 0.1\% allows near-full recovery. Notably, some low-weight intervals show 10-17\% degradation when perturbed, comparable to mid-range high-weight neurons. These results confirm not all high-weight neurons are important: their importance is nonlinear. Low-weight neurons also contribute significantly. This challenges weight-importance equivalence, offering refined neuron role insights. It supports applications like encryption prioritizing critical high-weight neurons and pruning removing non-critical ones, advancing neural network analysis.

</details>


### [76] [Entangled by Design: Spurious Intra-Variable Signal Routing in Tabular In-Context Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25532)
*Athanasios Vlontzos,Giorgos Papanastasiou,Bernhard Kainz,Sotirios Tsaftaris*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In-context learners trained on composite representations (causal + spurious signals) inevitably route predictions through whichever signal dominates in-context correlations, causing silent failures when deployed to new environments. Larger context and more expressive models exacerbate this spurious routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world models trained at single hospitals bundle true health signals with hospital-specific equipment artefacts. These spurious correlations drive predictions but fail silently when deployed to new hospitals with different equipment. Current in-context learning models cannot distinguish causal from spurious signals in composite representations.

Method: Theoretical analysis of ridge in-context learning shows spurious routing is unavoidable regardless of context size. Empirical validation with TabPFN (state-of-the-art tabular ICL model). Formalized as spurious routing in composite representations where X = [C; αS; η]. Derived closed-form characterization of CSR (Composite Spurious Routing) ∝ ρ_S/ρ_C.

Result: Proved spurious routing is unavoidable in linear ICL regardless of context size. Larger context sharpens commitment to dominant in-context signal (amplifying spurious routing by up to 1.74×). More expressive models show greater vulnerability (+2.22 CSR gap at high entanglement). CSR characterization confirmed at r = 0.997 for linear ICL and r = 0.979 for TabPFN.

Conclusion: Two lightweight mitigations introduced: environment-stratified context construction and S-swap augmentation (require only weak environment labels, no knowledge of causal partition). S-swap reduces spurious routing by 74% for linear ICL and 98.8% for TabPFN, increasing causal sensitivity 8.4× - models reroute through causal signal rather than becoming agnostic.

Abstract: Consider a model trained at a single hospital to predict patient recovery, where the measured feature $X$ bundles the patient's true health signal ($C$) with a systematic artefact from that hospital's equipment ($S$). Within that hospital, the artefact correlates with outcomes through unmeasured confounders such as patient demographics; an in-context learner rationally routes predictions through $S$, not $C$, and fails silently when deployed at a new hospital with different equipment. We formalise this as \emph{spurious routing in composite representations}: when a feature $X = [C;\,αS;\,η]$ encodes a causal signal $C$ and a spurious signal $S$ in distinct subspaces, the ICL cannot determine which drives predictions. We prove that under ridge ICL, a linear in-context learner, this routing is unavoidable regardless of context size; TabPFN, a state-of-the-art pretrained tabular ICL model, shows qualitatively consistent behaviour empirically. We derive a closed-form characterisation, $\mathrm{CSR} \propto ρ_S/ρ_C$, confirmed at $r = 0.997$ for linear ICL and $r = 0.979$ for TabPFN. Contrary to intuition, larger context sharpens commitment to the dominant in-context signal, amplifying spurious routing by up to $1.74\times$; in the high-spurious corner, more expressive models show greater vulnerability empirically ($+2.22$ CSR gap at high entanglement). We introduce two lightweight mitigations: environment-stratified context construction and S-swap augmentation, that require only weak environment labels and no knowledge of the causal partition. S-swap reduces spurious routing by $74\%$ for linear ICL and $98.8\%$ for TabPFN, with TabPFN's causal sensitivity increasing $8.4\times$ simultaneously: the model does not become agnostic, it reroutes through the causal signal.

</details>


### [77] [From Training to Deployment: Post-Hoc Causal Feature Identification via Sensitivity Ratios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25546)
*Athanasios Vlontzos,Giorgos Papanastasiou,Bernhard Kainz,Sotirios Tsaftaris*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NSR is a post-hoc diagnostic tool that identifies causal vs. spurious features in trained models by analyzing feature sensitivity variations across environments with structured shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods require access to training procedures and can't answer causal/spurious feature identification post-hoc for already trained models, especially in structured-shift scenarios like multi-site clinical data.

Method: Normalised Sensitivity Ratio (NSR) measures squared coefficient of variation of per-environment sensitivity - causal features have constant sensitivity across environments while spurious features track shifts.

Result: NSR achieves exact identification under linear SCM with K≥3 non-degenerate environments (Theorem 1). Experiments show perfect AUROC (1.000) on synthetic data, consistent rankings across 5 model families, and recovery of 6/8 causal features on bike-sharing data.

Conclusion: NSR provides a practical post-hoc diagnostic for causal feature identification with theoretical guarantees, failure characterization, and empirical validation, requiring no model retraining.

Abstract: Given a model that is already trained, which features does it rely on causally versus spuriously? Existing methods require access to the training procedure and cannot answer this post-hoc. We introduce the \textbf{Normalised Sensitivity Ratio~(NSR)}, a post-hoc, model-agnostic diagnostic for this question under a structured-shift regime: environments differ primarily in the mean of spurious features while the causal mechanism and causal marginals remain stable, as in multi-site clinical data or multi-batch genomics. Within this regime, causal features induce constant model sensitivity across environments while spurious features track shift. NSR formalises this as the squared coefficient of variation of per-environment sensitivity. Under a linear structural causal model (SCM) with $K\ge3$ non-degenerate environments, NSR achieves exact identification (Theorem~1). We fully characterise failure: weak shifts ($O(\varepsilon^4)$ collapse), degenerate geometry, and proxy attenuation ($O((1-α)^4)$), giving practitioners quantitative criteria for assessing whether the regime holds. Finite-sample rates are $O_p(n^{-1})$ under the null and $O_p(n^{-1/2})$ under the alternative. Experiments confirm all theoretical predictions on synthetic data (area under the ROC curve [AUROC] $= 1.000$ under conditions satisfying the regime), show consistent rankings across five model families (Kendall $τ\ge0.529$), and recover six of eight causal features on bike-sharing data (Precision@7 $= 0.75$) without modifying any trained model.

</details>


### [78] [Distilling Temporal Search and Reasoning: Evolving LLMs for Future Prediction via Harness-Assisted Efficient Data Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25554)
*Wanxu Cai,Zhengyu Chen,Huaisheng Zhu,Wei Wang,Jingang Wang,Qiang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A time-truncation harness for LLMs that enforces temporal cut-offs during event prediction to reduce temporal leakage, improve sampling efficiency, and generate higher-quality temporal reasoning data for model distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Future event prediction is important but challenging. Current approaches with external agent frameworks lose predictive capability when the harness is removed. Existing methods suffer from temporal leakage (where historical queries leak future information), degrade forecasting into retrieval, and use inefficient data collection methods like rejection sampling or unresolved queries that discard most data.

Method: Proposes a time-truncation harness that enforces a temporal cut-off at every reasoning step, enabling Tool-Integrated Reasoning (TIR)-style sampling from historical events. This reduces temporal leakage and dependency on inefficient rejection sampling or unresolved queries. Also builds a large-scale corpus and process-based metric.

Result: The harness induces broader temporal search breadth and raises the proportion of high-quality data. Distillation experiments show that students trained on harness-intervened data achieve best performance, demonstrating harness-assisted model evolution where higher-quality temporal reasoning data leads to parametric advancement.

Conclusion: The time-truncation harness effectively addresses temporal leakage in event prediction, improves data sampling efficiency, and enables the generation of higher-quality temporal reasoning data that can be used to train better student models through distillation.

Abstract: Future event prediction carries broad social impact yet remains challenging. SOTA approaches augment LLMs with external agent frameworks whose predictive capability vanishes once the harness is removed. While recent Tool-Integrated Reasoning (TIR) internalizes deep search for multi-hop retrieval of facts, forecasting further demands temporal search and reasoning over historical trends and dynamic shifts. The key obstacle is data: historical queries induce temporal leakage that degrades forecasting into retrieval. Prior works either freeze information gathering with static observations, or rely on rejection sampling or unresolved fresh queries that discard vast amounts of data, degrading synthesis efficiency. We propose a time-truncation harness that enforces a temporal cut-off at every turn, enabling TIR-style sampling from historical events, reducing temporal leakage and reliance of rejection sampling or unsolved queries, increasing the sampling efficiency. We further build a large-scale corpus and a process-based metric and show that our harness naturally induces a broader temporal breadth of search and raises the proportion of high-quality data, further increasing the efficiency and reducing the reliance on complex rubrics. Distillation experiments show that students trained on harness-intervened data achieve the best performance, demonstrating harness-assisted model evolving that turns higher quality temporal search and reasoning data into a parametric advancement of the students.

</details>


### [79] [Agent Skills Matter: Inferring Proprietary Skills from Execution Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25560)
*Jianing Geng,Ruiqi He,Zekun Fei,Biao Yi,Ruijie Wang,Zheli Liu,Xia Hu,Xuansheng Wu,Qingkai Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SigLeak is a framework that reconstructs proprietary agent skills by analyzing execution trajectories, exploiting observable behavioral patterns even when the actual skill artifacts are hidden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent skills are often kept proprietary for commercial reasons, but their behavioral effects remain observable in execution trajectories. This creates a "behavioral side channel" that could expose valuable procedural knowledge even when the actual skill implementation is hidden.

Method: SigLeak is a black-box framework that: 1) identifies recurring skill signatures in agent behavior, 2) constructs diverse diagnostic tasks to elicit skill usage, 3) contrasts trajectories with and without skills enabled, and 4) iteratively refines reconstructed skills from isolated behavioral patterns.

Result: Across 5 scenarios, 3 model families, and 3 agent frameworks, SigLeak outperforms or matches three baselines, achieving 6.88 percentage point improvement over skill-disabled reference and highest SkillSim scores for both coarse- and fine-grained semantic similarity.

Conclusion: Benign execution trajectories can expose proprietary procedural knowledge through behavioral side channels, highlighting a security/privacy vulnerability in agent skill ecosystems where hiding artifacts doesn't conceal behavioral effects.

Abstract: Agent skills package reusable procedures that improve downstream performance. Their lightweight, portable form enables marketplace monetization and private deployment behind cloud-hosted agent interfaces, giving providers incentives to keep high-value skills proprietary. Yet hiding the artifacts does not conceal their behavioral effects, which remain observable in execution trajectories and form a behavioral side channel. We define this exposure as Skill Leakage: reconstructing proprietary skills from trajectories elicited by benign queries, without reference answers or success labels. We introduce SigLeak, a black-box framework that exploits recurring skill signatures in agent behavior. It constructs diverse, decision-rich diagnostic tasks, contrasts matched skill-enabled and skill-disabled trajectories, and iteratively refines a reconstructed skill from the isolated patterns. Across five scenarios, three model families, and three agent frameworks, SigLeak outperforms or matches three baselines in nearly every setting. It raises the success rate by 6.88 percentage points over the skill-disabled reference on average and achieves the highest overall SkillSim, our metric for coarse- and fine-grained semantic similarity. These results show that benign execution trajectories can expose proprietary procedural knowledge. The code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/SigLeak-D1DB.

</details>


### [80] [Matrix-Free Photoacoustic Image Reconstruction via Sensor-Token Self-Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25576)
*Mary John,Shibili Said,Imad Barhumi,Sherzod Turaev,Mohamed Yahia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Transformer-based Sensor Attention Network (SAN) enables real-time photoacoustic tomography reconstruction by processing raw sensor measurements directly without system matrix dependency at inference, achieving superior image quality and 10x faster reconstruction times.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current PAT reconstruction methods (iterative compressive sensing and unrolled deep networks) depend on the system matrix at inference, making real-time clinical reconstruction computationally expensive and impractical.

Method: SAN uses a Transformer architecture that treats each sensor's full time series as a token, mapping raw measurements directly to reconstructed images without invoking the system matrix at inference. Training uses an analytical k-space H-matrix validated against k-Wave with vessel-weighted loss on augmented samples.

Result: SAN achieves highest SSIM (0.522) and PSNR (22.09 dB), lowest NMSE (0.233), and statistical superiority over ISTA, SBTV, and LISTA (p < 1e-8). Reconstruction time reduced by at least 10x by bypassing H-matrix at inference.

Conclusion: SAN enables real-time PAT reconstruction with superior image fidelity by eliminating system matrix dependency at inference, representing a significant advancement for clinical translation of photoacoustic tomography.

Abstract: Photoacoustic tomography (PAT) combines the optical absorption contrast of biological tissue with the spatial resolution of ultrasound, yet recovering the initial pressure distribution from sparse-view sensor measurements remains an ill-posed inverse problem. Iterative compressive-sensing solvers and unrolled deep networks both retain a dependence on the system matrix at inference, which leaves real-time clinical reconstruction computationally expensive. This paper proposes the Sensor Attention Network (SAN), a Transformer-based architecture that treats the full time series of each sensor as a token and maps raw measurements directly to the reconstructed image without invoking the system matrix at inference. For training and benchmarking, an analytical k-space H-matrix is constructed and validated against the k-Wave pseudo-spectral solver under matched geometry, achieving a mean per-sensor Pearson correlation of 0.919 +/- 0.049, with k-space apodization and Gaussian temporal damping acting synergistically to reduce the energy-normalized mismatch by 49%. Trained with a vessel-weighted loss on 488 augmented samples and evaluated on 46 held-out samples against ISTA, split-Bregman total variation (SBTV), and learned ISTA (LISTA), SAN attains the highest mean SSIM (0.522) and PSNR (22.09 dB) and the lowest NMSE (0.233). Paired t-tests and Wilcoxon signed-rank tests confirm the superiority of SAN over LISTA on PSNR, NMSE, and Pearson correlation at p < 1e-8, and over ISTA and SBTV on all fidelity metrics. By bypassing the H-matrix at inference, SAN reduces reconstruction time by at least an order of magnitude, supporting real-time PAT reconstruction.

</details>


### [81] [How Small Can You Go? A Controlled Study of LoRA Rank, Target Modules, and Quantization Trade-offs for Text-to-SQL on a 60M-Parameter Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25583)
*Mahendra Singh Rathor,Anagheem Azzam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper conducts a controlled study on parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods, specifically examining LoRA and QLoRA on a small T5 model for text-to-SQL tasks, quantifying the accuracy-memory trade-offs across different rank settings and quantization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While PEFT and quantization are widely used for adapting language models under compute constraints, most research focuses on large billion-parameter models where comprehensive exploration is expensive. The paper addresses this gap by asking: on a small, reproducible 60M-parameter model (T5-small) and a specific benchmark (WikiSQL), how much task accuracy does each efficiency adjustment actually cost?

Method: The authors conduct a controlled, single-variable study on T5-small using the WikiSQL benchmark, systematically varying three efficiency knobs: (1) LoRA rank r (values: 2, 4, 8, 16, 32), (2) which modules are adapted, and (3) numerical precision (INT8 and NF4 quantization for QLoRA). They measure both task accuracy and system-level metrics including trainable parameters, peak training memory, inference latency, and throughput.

Result: Key findings: LoRA with rank r=16 recovers within 11.6 percentage points of full fine-tuning accuracy (59.6% vs 71.2% exact-match) while training less than 1% of parameters and consuming 31% less peak GPU memory. Beyond rank 16, no measurable accuracy gain is observed. QLoRA with INT8 and NF4 quantization achieves comparable accuracy (52.8% and 53.2%) at dramatically lower memory cost (0.60 GB each), offering compelling trade-offs for memory-constrained deployments.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that significant efficiency gains can be achieved with minimal accuracy loss on small models, providing concrete guidance for practitioners: LoRA rank 16 represents a sweet spot, and QLoRA offers dramatic memory savings with acceptable accuracy trade-offs. The authors frame adaptation as a constrained optimization problem rather than pure accuracy maximization, releasing all code and configurations for full reproducibility.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) and low-bit quantization are now standard tools for adapting language models under tight compute budgets, yet their interaction is most often studied on billion-parameter models where the design space is expensive to explore. We ask a complementary question: on a specific, fully reproducible 60M-parameter encoder-decoder model (T5-small) and a single-table text-to-SQL benchmark (WikiSQL), how much task accuracy does each efficiency knob actually cost? We run a controlled, single-variable study over (i) LoRA rank r in {2, 4, 8, 16, 32}, (ii) the set of adapted modules, and (iii) numerical precision. We report task accuracy alongside system-level metrics including trainable parameters, peak training memory, inference latency, and throughput, and frame adaptation as a constrained trade-off rather than an accuracy-only objective. Our results show that LoRA with r=16 recovers within 11.6 percentage points of full fine-tuning accuracy (59.6% vs. 71.2% exact-match) while training fewer than 1% of parameters and consuming 31% less peak GPU memory. Within this setting, rank beyond r=16 yields no measurable accuracy gain. QLoRA with INT8 and NF4 quantization achieves comparable accuracy (52.8% and 53.2%) at dramatically lower memory cost (0.60 GB each), demonstrating a compelling trade-off for memory-constrained deployments. All code, configurations, and logs are released for full reproducibility.

</details>


### [82] [A Density-Matrix Framework for Electronic-Structure Analysis of Functional-Group and Salt Effects in Lithium-Metal Electrolytes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25597)
*Mingkang Liu,Huize Yu,Yanbin Gao,Nan Yao,Xiang Chen,Lei Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EMolStudio is a density-matrix-centered AI platform for predicting and analyzing electronic structure in lithium-metal electrolyte design, applied to study functional group and salt effects on frontier orbitals, electrostatic potential, and Li⁺-donor interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding lithium-metal electrolyte reactivity requires analyzing electron density redistribution across donor, anion, and cation centers, which is best resolved from spatial electronic structure. Quantum-chemical calculations are accurate but computationally demanding for this multidimensional design space, and existing machine-learning models don't adequately cover chemically diverse solvation shells or electrolyte-specific readouts.

Method: Developed EMolStudio, a density-matrix-centered AI platform with workflow integrating: molecular functionalization, explicit Li⁺ first-shell assembly, density-matrix prediction with idempotency projection, and electronic-structure readouts including frontier orbitals, electrostatic potential, Li⁺-donor bond order, and electron localization. Applied to 163,655 functionalized molecules and 22,500 explicit Li⁺ first-shell clusters across four lithium salts.

Result: 1) At molecular scale: Functionalization distinguishes CO₂Me, CN, F/CF₃, and sulfonyl groups through chemically distinct changes in frontier levels, electrostatic potential, and Li⁺-donor contacts, consistent with π*-acceptor, inductive, and polarization contributions, with sublinear accumulation at higher functionalization degrees. 2) In explicit solvation shells: Anion identity reshapes frontier-orbital localization - LiTDI anchors HOMO on the anion across entire library, while LiDFOB pairs anion-hosted HOMO with strongly functional-group-dependent LUMO hosting.

Conclusion: EMolStudio successfully translates functional-group and salt choices into electronic-structure hypotheses relevant to lithium-bond formation, desolvation, and interphase reactions, providing a powerful platform for electrolyte design through electronic-structure prediction and analysis.

Abstract: The reactivity of lithium-metal electrolytes arises from the interplay of molecular functional groups, Li$^+$ solvation, and salt-anion participation. This interplay operates through the redistribution of electron density across donor, anion, and cation centers, which is most directly read out from the electronic structure resolved in space. Quantum-chemical calculations deliver such readouts faithfully, yet become computationally demanding across this multidimensional design space, and machine-learning electronic-structure models seldom cover chemically diverse solvation shells or electrolyte-relevant readouts. Here, we present a density-matrix-centered AI platform (EMolStudio) for electronic-structure prediction and analysis. Its workflow integrates molecular functionalization, explicit Li$^+$ first-shell assembly, density-matrix prediction with idempotency projection, and readouts of frontier orbitals, electrostatic potential, Li$^+$-donor bond order, and electron localization. We apply EMolStudio to 163,655 functionalized molecules and 22,500 explicit Li$^+$ first-shell clusters across four lithium salts. We find that 1) at the molecular scale, functionalization distinguishes CO$_2$Me, CN, F/CF$_3$, and sulfonyl groups by chemically distinct changes in frontier levels, electrostatic potential, and Li$^+$-donor contact, consistent with $π^*$-acceptor, inductive, and polarization contributions, with sublinear accumulation at higher degrees of functionalization; 2) in explicit solvation shells, anion identity reshapes frontier-orbital localization: LiTDI anchors the HOMO on the anion across the entire library, whereas LiDFOB pairs an anion-hosted HOMO with strongly functional-group-dependent LUMO hosting. EMolStudio thereby translates functional-group and salt choices into electronic-structure hypotheses relevant to lithium-bond formation, desolvation, and interphase reactions.

</details>


### [83] [Computational Extraction of Legal Causes via al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim: A Set-Theoretic Formalization for Closed Fiqh Chapters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25605)
*Elnaser Abdelwahab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A computational algorithm extracts minimal operational rules from truth tables of juristic verdicts using set-theoretic formalization of al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim method for finding legal causes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formalize the classical Islamic juristic method of Examination and Division (al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim) for extracting legal causes ('ilal) computationally, bridging traditional jurisprudence with modern computational methods.

Method: Set-theoretic formalization of al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim method, computational algorithm that extracts minimal operational rules from truth tables of juristic verdicts, focusing on closed chapters of jurisprudence.

Result: Given a complete truth table for a closed chapter, the algorithm computes minimal structural generators of the ruling and eliminates all logically redundant attributes, producing admissible candidate causes for juristic evaluation.

Conclusion: The framework provides a computational approach to traditional Islamic juristic reasoning, conditional on finite school-relative concept vocabulary and complete ruling tables, enabling systematic extraction of legal causes.

Abstract: This paper presents a set-theoretic formalization of the classical usuli method of al-Sabr wa al-Taqsim (Examination and Division) for extracting legal causes ('ilal) within closed chapters of jurisprudence. A computational algorithm is introduced that extracts minimal operational rules from a truth table of juristic verdicts. The principal result is that, given a complete truth table for a closed chapter, the algorithm computes the minimal structural generators of the ruling and eliminates all logically redundant attributes. The resulting structures constitute admissible candidate causes for subsequent juristic evaluation. The framework is conditional upon the availability of a finite school-relative concept vocabulary and a complete ruling table for the chapter under investigation.

</details>


### [84] [Multi-Sensor Alignment for Weather Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25612)
*Samsad Alam,Devyani Lambhate,Aditya Mohan,Vishal Kumar,Vaibhav Katewa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes two alignment methods (ReDAM and Unified-weather-edit) to make weather simulations match real-world weather characteristics for autonomous vehicle perception, showing that aligned simulations provide more realistic performance evaluation and can improve 3D object detection robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous vehicles need perception systems that work in adverse weather, but real-world weather datasets are scarce. Current weather simulations may not accurately represent real-world weather characteristics (intensity and particle positioning), leading to overly optimistic performance evaluation of 3D detection models.

Method: 1. ReDAM (Reference Dataset Alignment Method) for fog intensity alignment. 2. Unified-weather-edit (inspired by Weather-edit[1]) for rain and snow particle positioning alignment across sensors. Both methods are validated using statistical and geometrical tests respectively.

Result: 1. Non-aligned weather simulation versions tend to make 3D detection models appear overly optimistic compared to aligned versions. 2. Aligned multi-sensor simulations can effectively improve robustness of 3D object detection when used to finetune existing sensor fusion models.

Conclusion: Weather alignment methods are crucial for creating realistic simulation data for autonomous vehicle perception testing. Aligned weather simulations provide more accurate performance evaluation and can be effectively used to enhance 3D object detection robustness through finetuning.

Abstract: Perception tasks for autonomous vehicles need to work satisfactorily in adverse weather conditions. Due to lack of real-world weather datasets, weather simulations are a promising alternative. To ensure simulations closely mirror real-world weather data, it's crucial that they represent the same weather characteristics, including severity and particle positioning, across different sensors. To achieve this, we propose the Reference Dataset Alignment Method (ReDAM) for weather intensity alignment in fog and Unified-weather-edit (inspired by Weather-edit[1]) for particle positioning alignment in rain and snow. We validate both alignment methods using statistical and geometrical tests, respectively. We find that 3D detection models for non-aligned versions tend to be overly optimistic as compared to aligned versions. We also show the aligned-multi-sensor simulation's effectiveness for achieving robustness for 3D object detection task by finetuning existing sensor fusion models on it.

</details>


### [85] [Beyond Epistemia: Epistemic Schizologia and Large Language Models as Techno-Semiotic Machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25620)
*Federico Cabitza,Gianluca Colombo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper critiques the concept of "Epistemia" in LLMs, proposing "epistemic schizologia" as a broader socio-technical phenomenon where linguistic plausibility substitutes for genuine knowledge practices, and suggests design principles for responsible human-AI epistemic practices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond Quattrociocchi's diagnosis of "Epistemia" (knowledge-like experience without proper epistemic practices) by challenging the explanatory framework that compares embodied human knowers with isolated LLMs, and instead develop a socio-technical understanding of epistemic practices involving AI systems.

Method: Draws on Carlo Sini's philosophy of practices, writing, signs, and technics to reconceptualize LLMs as "techno-semiotic machines" that automate a phase of written semiosis. Analyzes the broader phenomenon of "epistemic schizologia" - the cleavage between signs as linguistic expressions and signs within socially embedded circuits of interpretation and responsibility.

Result: Identifies "epistemic schizologia" as the socio-technical cleavage between linguistic production and responsible understanding, reinforced by "eikotic closure" (plausible continuations presented as final epistemic results), algorithmic authority, and epistemic self-misrecognition. Argues the relevant unit is the complete practice involving LLM outputs, not the model alone.

Conclusion: Proposes reframing to preserve distinction between linguistic production and responsible understanding, with design principles centered on inspectable genealogy, contestability, distributed responsibility, epistemic agency, and evaluating hybrid human-AI practices. Shifts focus from individual agents to socio-technical epistemic practices.

Abstract: Quattrociocchi and colleagues warn that the fluent outputs of large language models may allow linguistic plausibility to substitute for epistemic evaluation, producing the condition they call *Epistemia*: the experience of possessing knowledge without undertaking the practices through which judgment would ordinarily be warranted. This article accepts that diagnosis but challenges its explanatory framework, which compares an embodied, socially situated human knower with an isolated generative model thereby locating epistemic legitimacy in capacities internal to autonomous agents. Drawing on Carlo Sini's philosophy of practices, writing, signs, and technics, we propose instead to understand a large language model (LLM) as a *techno-semiotic machine* that automates a phase of written semiosis by producing plausible linguistic configurations from the sedimented archive of human writing. From this perspective, *Epistemia* is one consequence of a broader phenomenon that we call *epistemic schizologia*: the socio-technical cleavage between signs as linguistically accomplished expressions and signs as moments within socially embedded circuits of interpretation, evidence, criticism, verification, and responsibility. This cleavage is reinforced by *eikotic closure*, through which a plausible continuation is presented with the finality of an epistemic result, and by algorithmic authority and epistemic self-misrecognition. The relevant unit is therefore not the model alone but the complete practice in which generated inscriptions are prompted, interpreted, verified, contested, used, and made consequential. This reframing preserves the distinction between linguistic production and responsible understanding while grounding a design programme centred on inspectable genealogy, contestability, distributed responsibility, epistemic agency, and the evaluation of hybrid human--AIpractices.

</details>


### [86] [Quotient Dynamics, Effective Curvature, and Implicit Bias in Positive Quadratic Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25624)
*Pengcheng Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how the quotient manifold structure of positive quadratic networks influences training dynamics, curvature, recovery guarantees, and interpolation bias selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Positive quadratic networks have a natural quotient structure where the factor matrix U is identifiable only up to orthogonal transformations (UU^⊤ represents a rank-r PSD matrix). This quotient geometry affects optimization behavior, curvature, recovery, and which interpolating solutions gradient methods select.

Method: The paper uses differential geometry to study the quotient manifold ℝ^{d×r}_*/O(r) representing rank-r PSD matrices. For smooth objectives, they show Euclidean gradient flow on factors projects exactly to Riemannian gradient flow on the quotient. They analyze quadratic regression with empirical Hessian restricted to tangent spaces. Under Gaussian rank-one measurements, they compute curvature, derive deviation bounds, construct spectral initialization, and prove convergence. For underdetermined commuting cases, they show factor gradient flow becomes entropy mirror flow in spectral coordinates.

Result: Key results: 1) Exact equivalence between factor gradient flow and quotient Riemannian gradient flow; 2) Effective Hessian characterization at interpolators; 3) Population curvature analysis and deviation bounds for Gaussian measurements; 4) Local exponential convergence for gradient flow and linear convergence for gradient descent; 5) In commuting regimes, gradient flow becomes entropy mirror flow, with positive initializations converging to Bregman projections; 6) Isotropic initialization yields minimum-trace solutions as initialization scale approaches zero.

Conclusion: The quotient manifold structure fundamentally governs optimization dynamics in positive quadratic networks. This geometric perspective explains training behavior, provides recovery guarantees, and reveals implicit regularization properties - specifically, gradient methods with positive initialization select minimum-trace interpolating solutions via weighted entropy regularization in the invariant joint spectral algebra.

Abstract: Positive quadratic networks admit the low-rank representation f_U(x)=x^top UU^top x, where Uinmathbb{R}^{dtimes r} is identifiable only up to right orthogonal multiplication, representing a rank-r PSD matrix Q=UU^top. We study how this quotient structure governs training dynamics, curvature, recovery, and interpolation bias. On the full-column-rank stratum, we identify mathbb{R}^{dtimes r}_*/O(r) with the rank-r PSD manifold. For smooth objectives L(U)=ell(UU^top), the Euclidean factor gradient is horizontal. Thus, factor gradient flow projects exactly to quotient Riemannian gradient flow, while finite-step gradient descent induces an exact congruence recursion for the predictor. For quadratic regression, we derive the effective Hessian at interpolators as the empirical measurement Gram form restricted to the tangent space relative to the quotient metric. Under Gaussian rank-one measurements, we compute population curvature, prove uniform deviation bounds for the empirical normal operator, construct a spectral initializer, and establish local exponential convergence for gradient flow and linear convergence for small-step descent. Recovery guarantees are explicit but conservative due to reliance on full-space second-moment control. In underdetermined commuting regimes, factor gradient flow becomes an exact entropy mirror flow in joint spectral coordinates. Strictly positive initializations converge to Bregman projections onto the interpolation set. With isotropic initialization q(0)=varepsilon^2mathbf{1}, predictors approach the minimum-trace solution set as varepsilondownarrow0, resolving nonuniqueness via weighted entropy within the invariant joint spectral algebra. Finite-step descent selects interpolants differing from continuous-time Bregman projections by O(eta). Numerical experiments verify these quotient identities, curvature predictions, recovery behaviors, and selection laws.

</details>


### [87] [Joint Text-Audio Alignment for EEG-to-Text Decoding in Chinese Speech Production and Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25626)
*Tian Zheng,Xurong Xie,Xinxin Zhu,Xiaolan Peng,Feng Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EEGAlign is a novel framework that jointly aligns EEG signals with both text semantics and audio features via contrastive learning to decode Chinese sentences from scalp EEG, achieving state-of-the-art performance in large-vocabulary Chinese sentence classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decoding speech from non-invasive scalp EEG provides a communication pathway for speech-impaired individuals. Chinese sentence decoding is particularly challenging due to high-dimensional character space, inter-subject variability, low signal-to-noise ratios, and the inability of existing single-axis methods (either text semantics or audio features) to satisfy both sentence-level discriminability and fine-grained temporal resolution needs simultaneously.

Method: EEGAlign uses a parameter-efficient framework that jointly aligns EEG signals with two complementary axes: 1) text alignment using BGE-M3 text embeddings, and 2) audio alignment using wav2vec 2.0 speech features via contrastive learning, followed by CTC character-sequence decoding.

Result: On ChineseEEG-2 data, EEGAlign achieved state-of-the-art closed-set sentence classification performance: 82.37% Top-1 accuracy on Reading Aloud EEG and 41.43% on Passive Listening EEG out of 101 candidate sentences. Ablation studies confirmed the complementary nature of the two alignment axes.

Conclusion: This study presents the first successful large-vocabulary Chinese sentence decoding from non-invasive EEG during overt speech production, demonstrating that joint text-audio alignment overcomes limitations of single-axis methods and enables effective neural communication decoding.

Abstract: Decoding speech information directly from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) into text provides a potential non-invasive neural communication pathway for individuals with severe speech and motor impairments. Compared with invasive approaches such as electrocorticography, EEG is safer and more widely deployable, yet substantially more challenging to decode.This challenge is exacerbated for Chinese sentence decoding, which must handle a high-dimensional output space with thousands of characters, severe inter-subject variability, and low signal-to-noise ratios for text alignment.Existing methods commit to a single supervisory axis---either text semantics or audio acoustic features---yet neither can simultaneously satisfy the demands of sentence-level discriminability and fine-grained temporal resolution required for large-vocabulary Chinese decoding. We introduce EEGAlign, a novel parameter-efficient framework that jointly aligns EEG with two axes---text alignment with BGE-M3 text embeddings and audio alignment with wav2vec~2.0 speech features via contrastive learning followed by CTC character-sequence decoding. On ChineseEEG-2 data, EEGAlign yields state-of-the-art closed-set sentence classification performance, reaching up to 82.37% Top-1 accuracy on Reading Aloud EEG and 41.43% on Passive Listening EEG out of 101 candidates. Ablation studies show that the two alignment axes are highly complementary: combining them yields consistently better performance than either alone. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study on decoding large-vocabulary Chinese sentences from non-invasive EEG during overt speech production, and achieving strong classification performance with relatively large closed-set candidate-sentence setting.

</details>


### [88] [AIriskEval-edu Demo: Auditing of Pedagogical Risks in Educational Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25634)
*Javier Irigoyen,Roberto Daza,Francisco Jurado,Julian Fierrez,Ruben Tolosana,Alvaro Ortigosa,Miguel Lopez-Duran,Aythami Morales*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AIriskEval-edu Demo is a platform that evaluates instructional explanations for pedagogical quality across five risk dimensions using both GPT-5.5 and a fine-tuned local Llama 3.1 8B evaluator, with the local model outperforming GPT-5.5 while keeping content within institutional infrastructure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to provide educational institutions with a tool to assess the pedagogical quality of instructional explanations by identifying various risks (factual accuracy, bias, appropriateness, etc.) while maintaining data privacy by keeping audited content within their own infrastructure rather than relying solely on external APIs.

Method: The platform integrates GPT-5.5 through external API and a self-hosted Llama 3.1 8B evaluator fine-tuned on AIriskEval-edu dataset. It evaluates explanations against five dimensions: factual accuracy, depth/completeness, focus/relevance, student-level appropriateness, and ideological bias. It operates in two modes: AI mode (assessing stored explanations from simulated teacher profiles) and human mode (real-time auditing of user-written explanations).

Result: The local Llama 3.1 8B evaluator outperforms GPT-5.5 on most reported metrics. The platform provides binary decisions with confidence scores, natural-language rationales, and localized evidence spans for detected risks across all dimensions except Depth and Completeness.

Conclusion: AIriskEval-edu Demo offers educational institutions a practical solution for auditing instructional explanations with both accuracy and privacy benefits, enabling them to maintain content control while benefiting from AI-powered pedagogical risk assessment.

Abstract: We present AIriskEval-edu Demo, a platform that audits the pedagogical quality of instructional explanations and provides explainable audit results. The platform evaluates an explanation against a rubric covering five dimensions of pedagogical risk: factual accuracy, depth and completeness, focus and relevance, student-level appropriateness, and ideological bias. For each dimension, it returns a binary decision and a confidence score. Detected risks also include a natural-language rationale and, except for Depth and Completeness, a localized evidence span. The platform integrates GPT-5.5 through an external API and a self-hosted Llama 3.1 8B evaluator that runs on consumer-grade GPUs. The local evaluator is fine-tuned on AIriskEval-edu, a dataset of K-12 instructional explanations with risk and explainability annotations. The platform operates in two modes: in AI mode, both evaluators assess stored explanations generated under six simulated teacher profiles, each representing a distinct pedagogical behavior and potential risk; in human mode, the local evaluator audits user-written explanations in real time. The local evaluator outperforms GPT-5.5 on most reported metrics, offering educational institutions a practical way to keep audited content within their own infrastructure.

</details>


### [89] [Engine-Equal, Human-Unequal: A Reproducible Outcome Skew in Engine-Assessed Equal Chess Positions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25655)
*Jesung Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Human chess results show systematic outcome skews even in positions engines judge as essentially equal, and these skews persist across different player groups and time periods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether chess positions that engines evaluate as equal actually produce balanced results in human play, or if certain positions systematically favor one side despite apparent equality.

Method: Analyzed 1,661 chess positions from Lichess (October 2025) that Stockfish 18 evaluates as essentially equal (±10 centipawns). Measured outcome skews (difference between actual results and rating-predicted results) and tested reproducibility across: 1) disjoint player account sets, 2) time periods, 3) different rating bands, and 4) an out-of-sample month eight months later.

Result: Found significant outcome skews that reproduce across all tests (replication slope of 0.69, CI [0.65, 0.74], rising to 0.94 for most popular positions). Skews are position-specific properties, with median effect size of about 2 percentage points White score. The disfavored side thinks longer, suggesting human perception aligns with these skews.

Conclusion: Even in positions that chess engines confidently evaluate as equal, human results show systematic biases. Engine evaluation is not a sufficient statistic for human outcomes, revealing a gap between theoretical engine assessment and practical human performance.

Abstract: Among chess opening positions that a strong engine judges essentially equal (Stockfish 18 evaluation within 10 centipawns of zero, depth-stable) and that humans actually reach on Lichess (October 2025; 1,661 positions, 16.1M occurrences), human results are not balanced. Positions carry outcome skews, each the gap between its games' actual results and what the players' ratings predict, whose directions are stable properties of the naturally-reached position: some positions favour White, others Black. These skews reproduce across three re-partitions -- disjoint player-account sets (primary), time, and disjoint rating bands -- and on an out-of-sample month eight months later. On the primary split, each position's skew is measured once in each account group, and the replication slope asks how well one measurement predicts the other after removing rating and opening-family effects: one means undiminished carry-over; zero, no linear relation. We find 0.69 (family-clustered 95% CI [0.65, 0.74]), rising to 0.94 on the most-popular, best-measured positions. The slope's value depends on the position mix. Existence is the invariant claim: it survives every tighter evaluation band, search depth, calibration, and popularity cutoff we test, and replicates within blitz and rapid separately. The typical skew is small (median $|δ| \approx 0.018$, about two percentage points of White score), yet it reproduces, position by position, across disjoint accounts. At these positions the disfavoured side also thinks longer. Even where the evaluation is most confident, it is not a sufficient statistic for human outcomes. The result is observational, and the causal question is left to a pre-registered randomised companion study.

</details>


### [90] [OrchBench: Evaluating Multi-Agent Orchestration Plans in Isolation via Deterministic Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25656)
*Zhenzhen Ren,Jiyan He,Xinpeng Zhang,Zhenxing Qian,Ke Han,Shuxin Zheng,GuoBiao Li,Xiaoqing Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OrchBench is a simulation-based benchmark for evaluating multi-agent orchestration plans that uses DAGs to model tasks, allowing efficient evaluation without real execution while strongly correlating with actual performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent system evaluations rely on end-to-end execution which conflates orchestration quality with other factors (worker capabilities, tools, noise), and real execution becomes expensive with scale due to time and token costs.

Method: OrchBench constructs directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) from real-world tasks to encode dependencies. Planners assign subtasks to agents with specified information transfers and retention. A deterministic simulator evaluates plans without invoking real agents, measuring result quality, makespan, and token costs.

Result: OrchBench achieves strong correlation with real Claude Code executions (Pearson r=0.816) while using only 1.3% of tokens and 10.3% of wall-clock time. Key findings show preserving task-critical information is more important than increasing agent count, and parallelism benefits diminish with coordination failures.

Conclusion: OrchBench provides an efficient and interpretable benchmark for comparing and diagnosing multi-agent orchestration plans, enabling systematic evaluation without the high costs of real execution.

Abstract: Complex tasks often decompose into parallelizable yet interdependent subtasks, making orchestration critical to the performance of multi-agent systems (MAS). Existing evaluations typically rely on end-to-end execution, which conflates orchestration-plan quality with worker capabilities, tool reliability, and environmental noise. Moreover, the time and token costs of real execution grow rapidly with workflow scale, making systematic evaluation expensive. We present OrchBench, a simulation-based benchmark for evaluating multi-agent orchestration plans in isolation. Starting from real-world tasks, OrchBench constructs directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) that encode task dependencies, with controlled sizes and degrees of parallelism. Given a DAG, a per-agent context limit, and an agent budget, the evaluated planner assigns subtasks to agents and specifies cross-agent information transfers and their retention ratios. A deterministic simulator evaluates the resulting plan without invoking worker agents and returns interpretable measures of result quality, makespan, and token cost. The simulated scores produced by OrchBench correlate strongly with quality scores from Claude Code executions, achieving a Pearson correlation of \(r=0.816\), while requiring only \(1.3\%\) of the tokens and \(10.3\%\) of the wall-clock time. Across diverse planners and workflow scales, we find that preserving task-critical information is more important than simply increasing the number of agents, and the benefits of parallelism diminish as coordination failures accumulate. These results establish OrchBench as an efficient and interpretable benchmark for comparing and diagnosing multi-agent orchestration plans.

</details>


### [91] [CoRT: Counterfactual Replay for Token-Level Rubric-Guided Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25659)
*Bo-Wen Zhang,Junwei He,Wen Wang,Song-Lin Lv,Wentao Ma,Rongyi Lin,Shuhan Zhong,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoRT is a token-level credit weighting method for GRPO-style RL that uses counterfactual replay to allocate credit within responses without needing auxiliary token scoring models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GRPO-style reinforcement learning pipelines reduce structured rubric-based evaluations to scalar response-level rewards and broadcast them uniformly to all tokens, lacking mechanisms for allocating credit to specific spans or semantic choices within responses, even when different criteria apply to different parts of the output.

Method: CoRT uses counterfactual replay: it rescales the same sampled response under the original rubric-conditioned prompt and a matched criteria-free prompt. The resulting tokenwise log-likelihood contrasts serve as proxies for dependence on rubric context. These contrasts are mapped to bounded, response-normalized weights to redistribute signed GRPO advantage across tokens.

Result: Experiments show CoRT improves over matched response-level GRPO in most comparisons, with average gain of 4.4 percentage points. It remains competitive with learned token-level credit baselines while avoiding separate relevance-learning stages.

Conclusion: Policy-internal counterfactual likelihood contrasts provide effective training signals for within-response credit allocation while maintaining the simplicity and stability of GRPO, offering a practical solution to the token-level credit assignment problem in rubric-based RL.

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning enriches language model training by evaluating model outputs against explicit criteria. Yet in GRPO-style pipelines, these structured judgments are reduced to a scalar response-level reward and converted into a response-level advantage, which is broadcast uniformly to all generated tokens. This leaves no explicit mechanism for allocating credit within a response, even when different criteria are grounded in different spans, formatting decisions, or semantic choices. We propose CoRT, a token-level credit weighting method for rubric-conditioned GRPO. Instead of training an auxiliary token scoring model, CoRT uses counterfactual replay to rescore the same sampled response under the original rubric-conditioned prompt and a matched criteria-free prompt. The resulting tokenwise log-likelihood contrasts serve as a proxy for dependence on the rubric context. CoRT maps these contrasts to bounded, response-normalized weights and uses them to redistribute the signed GRPO advantage across tokens, without introducing an auxiliary scorer or changing the response-level reward. Experiments across instruction-tuned models and reward granularities show that CoRT improves over matched response-level GRPO in the vast majority of comparisons, with an average gain of 4.4 percentage points. The method remains competitive with learned token-level credit baselines while avoiding a separate relevance-learning stage. These results suggest that policy-internal counterfactual likelihood contrasts provide an effective training signal for within-response credit allocation while retaining the simplicity and stability of GRPO.

</details>


### [92] [Localized Adaptation Reveals Distinct Learning Signatures in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25663)
*Rebecca Ramnauth,Brian Scassellati*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Different adaptation site placements (early/middle/late/full-stack LoRA) produce distinct learning geometries across objectives, revealing adaptation location as a critical design parameter for controlling what models learn and generalize.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer adaptation is typically distributed across model depth even for narrow changes. The researchers want to understand how adaptation site location shapes what models learn, their generalization capabilities, and selective application of updates.

Method: Created a controlled benchmark with five objectives: lexical binding, factual association, behavioral policy learning, causal mapping, and procedural reasoning. Tested each with different adaptation geometries using LoRA at early-, middle-, or late-layers vs full-stack. Examined acquisition, transfer, and boundedness patterns under these configurations.

Result: Each objective exhibited distinct adaptation geometries: lexical binding favored early layers for acquisition/boundedness but broader updates for transfer; factual association favored later layers; behavioral learning separated late-layer action acquisition from middle-layer policy gating; causal and procedural transfer benefited most from middle- or full-stack adaptation. Patterns persisted under parameter-matched controls and replicated across five model families.

Conclusion: Adaptation site is a key design variable for controlling what models learn, generalize, and leave unchanged. Different objectives have characteristic "adaptation geometries" that inform optimal placement of adaptation mechanisms in transformer models.

Abstract: Transformer adaptation is typically distributed across model depth, even when the intended change is narrow. We investigate how adaptation site shapes what a model learns, how well that learning generalizes, and how selectively it is applied. We introduce a controlled benchmark spanning five objectives (lexical binding, factual association, behavioral policy learning, causal mapping, and procedural reasoning) and define each objective's "adaptation geometry" as its profile of acquisition, transfer, and boundedness under full-stack and early-, middle-, or late-layer LoRA. The objectives exhibit distinct geometries. Lexical binding favors early-layer adaptation for acquisition and boundedness but requires broader updates for transfer; factual association favors later layers among localized adapters; behavioral learning separates late-layer action acquisition from middle-layer policy gating; and causal and procedural transfer benefit most from middle- or full-stack adaptation. These patterns largely persist under parameter-matched controls, and most corresponding directional contrasts replicate across five model families. These findings establish adaptation site as a key design variable for controlling what models learn, generalize, and leave unchanged.

</details>


### [93] [OmniDelta: Skill-Driven Budget Allocation for Token Compression in OmniLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25669)
*Haoyang Huang,Wenjie Huang,Tianqi Xu,Hongyaoxing Gu,Kang Tan,Yikai Fu,Yuhao Shen,Tianyu Liu,Baolin Zhang,Jun Zhang,Xinyi Hu,Jun Dai,Shuang Ge,Lei Chen,Yue Li,Mingchen Wang,Meng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniDelta is a training-free framework that improves efficiency in Omni-modal LLMs by dynamically allocating token retention budgets across audio and video modalities based on query intent and content characteristics, achieving better accuracy-efficiency trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omni-modal LLMs that handle text, audio, and video suffer from high memory and inference costs due to long audio-video token sequences. Existing compression methods focus on token selection under fixed budgets but fail to properly allocate budgets between modalities or within them, leading to unreliable similarity-based allocations and missed key evidence.

Method: OmniDelta uses a skill-driven framework with two main components: 1) Intent-aware inter-modal allocation that constructs audio/video skill pools to shift budgets based on query demand, and 2) Content-aware intra-modal allocation that reallocates modality budgets using local complexity and temporal redundancy across audio segments and video frames. It works with existing pruning strategies while preserving total retained-token ratios.

Result: Experiments on four audio-video benchmarks with Qwen2.5-Omni models show OmniDelta establishes new accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontiers across pruning ratios. At 25% token retention on Qwen2.5-Omni-7B, it reduces GPU memory by 22.0% and achieves 1.64x end-to-end speedup over full-token inference.

Conclusion: OmniDelta effectively addresses the budget allocation problem in Omni-modal LLM compression by coupling intent-aware inter-modal allocation with content-aware intra-modal allocation, achieving significant efficiency improvements without training while maintaining accuracy.

Abstract: Emerging Omni-modal Large Language Models (OmniLLMs) enable unified understanding of text, audio, and video, but their long audio-video token sequences introduce substantial memory and inference costs. Existing compression methods mainly focus on selecting important tokens under fixed budgets, leaving the preceding budget-allocation problem underexplored. We show that direct query-to-audio/video similarity is unreliable for inter-modal budget allocation, and that uniform intra-modal budgets can miss key evidence while retaining redundant content. To address these limitations, we propose OmniDelta, a training-free, skill-driven framework that couples intent-aware inter-modal allocation with content-aware intra-modal allocation. OmniDelta first constructs audio and video skill pools to shift the fixed retained-token budget according to query demand, then reallocates modality budgets over audio segments and video frames using local complexity and temporal redundancy. The resulting local budgets can be combined with existing pruning strategies, preserving the total retained-token ratio while changing where the budget is spent. Experiments on four audio-video benchmarks with two Qwen2.5-Omni models show that OmniDelta establishes a new accuracy-efficiency Pareto frontier across pruning ratios. At 25% token retention on Qwen2.5-Omni-7B, OmniDelta reduces GPU memory by 22.0% and achieves a 1.64x end-to-end speedup over full-token inference.

</details>


### [94] [DecoEvo: Score-Decoupled Co-Evolution of Solver and Rubric-Generator Skills in Text Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25675)
*Jiangwang Chen,Zixin Song,Junlin Liu,Shuaiyu Zhou,Haiyan Wu,Haihan Shi,Chenxi Zhou,Hanqing Li,Xiao Yang,Da Zhu,Guanjun Jiang,Hai Wan,Xibin Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DecoEvo is a new text-space optimization method that co-evolves LLM solvers and evaluation rubrics without using gold standards, improving on open-ended tasks where fixed evaluation rubrics create bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-space optimization methods for LLMs keep evaluation rubrics fixed, which becomes problematic for open-ended tasks. Once a solver improves on the criteria measured by a rubric, other important dimensions remain invisible to optimization. Simply evolving rubrics based on solver scores is unreliable because apparent progress may just come from making rubrics easier to satisfy rather than genuinely improving solver performance.

Method: DecoEvo (Decoupled Co-Evolution) co-evolves solver skills and rubric-generator skills under decoupled objectives without using gold rubrics. The solver is updated using criterion-level feedback, while the rubric-generator is revised through complementary audits of requirement coverage and response discrimination that are independent of aggregate solver scores. This separation prevents repeated emphasis on criteria the solver already satisfies and focuses on newly exposed weaknesses.

Result: DecoEvo outperforms all compared methods across five benchmarks and three LLM backbones under official evaluations. It achieves 2.8-5.0% relative gains over SkillOpt in the five-benchmark average, demonstrating significant improvements in text-space optimization for open-ended tasks.

Conclusion: DecoEvo successfully addresses the limitations of fixed evaluation in text-space optimization by co-evolving solvers and rubrics with decoupled objectives, leading to substantial performance improvements on open-ended tasks while maintaining the inspectability advantages of text-space approaches.

Abstract: Text-space optimization adapts large language models (LLMs) by editing external natural-language artifacts rather than model weights, so the optimized artifacts remain inspectable and the model can be treated as a black box. However, most existing text-space methods keep evaluation fixed. On open-ended tasks, this can become a bottleneck: once the solver improves on the criteria a rubric measures, omitted dimensions remain invisible to the optimization signal. Simply evolving the rubric is also unreliable when updates are selected by the current solver's score, because apparent progress can come from making the rubric easier to satisfy. We introduce DecoEvo (Decoupled Co-Evolution), which co-evolves a solver skill and a rubric-generator skill under decoupled objectives without using gold rubrics during optimization. The solver skill is updated using criterion-level feedback, while the rubric-generator skill is revised through complementary audits of requirement coverage and response discrimination that are independent of aggregate solver score. This separation focuses generator updates on newly exposed solver weaknesses, reducing repeated emphasis on criteria the solver already satisfies. Under each benchmark's official evaluation, DecoEvo outperforms all compared methods across five benchmarks and three LLM backbones, yielding 2.8--5.0\% relative gains over SkillOpt in the five-benchmark average.

</details>


### [95] [Cognivia: A Cognitive Behavioral Therapy Copilot for Evidence-Based Mental Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25681)
*Qi Chen,Siria Xiyueyao Luo,Jian Wang,Yuan Shi,Haocong Rao,Xuejiao Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Cognivia is an AI therapist framework that automatically identifies cognitive distortions and generates rational responses using fine-tuned LLMs trained on authoritative CBT texts and mental health Q&A data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cognitive distortions amplify negative emotions and contribute to mental health disorders. While CBT is effective, its large-scale application is limited by therapist shortages. Existing LLM-based mental health applications suffer from limited domain specificity, overly flattering responses, and lack of well-defined cognitive distortion annotations.

Method: Built on authoritative CBT texts (core paradigms and standard references), augmented with mental health Q&A data. Uses multi-stage prompting and structured generation strategies supervised by behavioral science experts. Fine-tunes a lightweight LLM on this augmented CBT dataset to create Cognivia.

Result: Cognivia consistently outperforms baseline methods in both cognitive distortion recognition and rational response generation, as evaluated through lexical metrics, LLM-based Judges with complementary criteria, and human evaluation by 10 behavioral science experts.

Conclusion: Cognivia demonstrates effective integration of automatic cognitive distortion identification and rational response generation, providing an evidence-based AI therapist framework that addresses limitations of existing LLM-based mental health applications.

Abstract: Cognitive distortion amplifies negative emotions and contributes to mental health disorders. Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) is an effective way to address cognitive distortions, but its large-scale application is limited by the shortage of professional therapists. Although large language models (LLMs) have recently been explored for mental health applications, existing methods still suffer from limited domain specificity, overly flattering responses, and the absence of well-defined annotations for cognitive distortions. This paper proposes Cognivia, an evidence-based artificial intelligence therapist that integrates automatic cognitive distortion identification and rational response generation. Our framework is built on authoritative CBT texts widely regarded as core paradigms and standard references. It is further augmented with mental health question-answer (Q and A) data, and employs multi-stage prompting and structured generation strategies under the supervision of behavioral science experts. Then we fine-tune a lightweight LLM on this augmented CBT dataset to obtain Cognivia. In addition, we propose the first hierarchical quality evaluation framework for assessing LLM-generated rational responses, developed through collaboration between AI researchers and behavioral science experts. Cognivia is evaluated using lexical metrics, LLM-based Judges with two complementary criteria, and human evaluation by 10 behavioral science experts. It consistently outperforms the baseline methods in cognitive distortion recognition and rational response generation, demonstrating its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/SNOWTEAM2023/Cognivia.

</details>


### [96] [Nudging Sustainable Choices through LLM-Generated Recommendation Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25726)
*Haya Halimeh,Dietmar Jannach,Oliver Müller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-generated sustainability explanations framed with behavioral nudges (social norms) significantly increase sustainable choices in both low and high involvement domains, while plain disclosure only improves perception without changing behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recommender systems influence daily consumption decisions and could encourage sustainable choices. Prior research shows explanations affect user perceptions and decisions, but this study argues explanations can also serve as behavioral nudges by highlighting sustainability information at decision moments.

Method: Used generative AI to create sustainability-aware explanations based on nudge theory, validated through human evaluation and LLM-as-judge audits. Conducted two randomized studies (N=529) in low involvement (instant coffee) and high involvement (hotel bookings) domains where participants chose among preference-matched recommendations with different explanation framings.

Result: Across both domains: plain sustainability disclosure doesn't change choices but improves explanation evaluations; behavioral framing (especially descriptive social norms) significantly increases sustainable selections and eases decision-making. Perception and behavior diverge - disclosure improves evaluations but doesn't lead to more sustainable choices.

Conclusion: LLMs can generate theory-grounded explanations at scale for social good interventions. The findings point to practical explanation-based interventions where framed sustainability information (particularly social norms) effectively nudges sustainable behavior, with implications for adaptive explanation design using generative AI.

Abstract: Recommender systems mediate everyday consumption, offering a promising channel for encouraging sustainable choices. Prior research shows that explanations influence users' perceptions of recommendations and can support more informed decisions. We argue that explanations can also serve as behavioral nudges by foregrounding sustainability information at the moment of choice. This study investigates how different behavioral framings of sustainability information in recommendation explanations affect user choices and perceptions. Using generative AI, we generate sustainability-aware explanations by drawing on nudge theory and validate them through human evaluation and LLM-as-a-judge audits. Building on this foundation, we conduct two randomized studies ($N = 529$) in a low involvement domain (instant coffee) and a high involvement domain (hotel bookings), in which participants choose among preference matched recommendations accompanied by these explanations. Our results show that, across both domains, merely disclosing sustainability information in explanations does not change choices, whereas framing that information or invoking a descriptive social norm significantly increases sustainable selections and eases decision-making. Notably, perception and behavior diverge, as plain disclosure improves explanation evaluations without translating into more sustainable selection behavior. Our work demonstrates how LLMs can generate theory-grounded explanations at scale, pointing toward practical explanation-based interventions for social good. We conclude by discussing implications for adaptive explanation design with generative AI.

</details>


### [97] [Loss Invariance Determines What Concept Layers Encode: Volume Grounding in Echocardiography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25748)
*Hyunkyung Han,Min Jung Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Concept bottleneck models for echocardiography require physical scale supervision of intermediate variables (ventricular volumes) to ensure meaningful interpretations, not just prediction accuracy, due to invariance properties of ratio-based outputs like ejection fraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current validation of concept bottleneck models focuses only on how accurately intermediate variables are predicted, but this may be insufficient when intermediate variables have physical meaning and outputs are ratios (like ejection fraction from ventricular volumes). The paper examines whether concept accuracy alone ensures meaningful interpretability in clinical models.

Method: Trained a video transformer encoder on echocardiography data using end-systolic and end-diastolic volumes as concept layer, from which ejection fraction was computed analytically (no residual path). Compared: 1) Training with only ejection fraction objective, 2) Training with additional volume supervision in absolute units (milliliters). Evaluated on 1276 held-out studies.

Result: Concept bottleneck performed similarly to direct regression for ejection fraction (6.89 vs 7.13 MAE). Without volume supervision: predicted volumes collapsed to 0.1 mL spread (vs reference 35.7-45.7 mL) while correlations were preserved - due to ejection fraction's invariance to volume scaling. Adding absolute volume supervision reduced volume error from 89.8 to 25.8 mL with only 0.4 increase in ejection fraction error.
