Conclusion: Concept prediction accuracy alone can mask a concept layer lacking physical scale, making interpretability meaningless. Clinical models with interpretable intermediate variables must be validated against training objective's invariance structure, not just prediction accuracy.

Abstract: Objective: Concept bottleneck models route prediction through interpretable intermediate variables, and their validity is normally judged by how accurately those variables are predicted. We ask whether that judgement is sufficient, using left ventricular volumes as the concepts underlying ejection fraction estimation from echocardiographic video.
  Methods: A video transformer encoder was trained on a publicly available echocardiography dataset. End-systolic and end-diastolic volumes formed a concept layer from which ejection fraction was computed analytically, with no residual path to the output. We compared training under an ejection fraction objective alone against training with additional supervision of the volumes in millilitres, and evaluated both on 1276 held-out studies.
  Results: The concept bottleneck did not increase ejection fraction error relative to direct regression, at 6.89 against 7.13 mean absolute error. Without volume supervision, however, the spread of predicted volumes collapsed to 0.1 millilitres against reference spreads of 35.7 and 45.7 millilitres, while correlation was partly preserved. We show that this follows from an invariance property of the objective: ejection fraction is a ratio and is unchanged when both volumes are rescaled, so the loss determines the concept layer only up to scale. Supervision in absolute units reduced volume error from 89.8 to 25.8 millilitres at a cost of 0.4 in ejection fraction error.
  Conclusion: Concept accuracy alone can conceal a concept layer that carries no physical scale.
  Significance: Interpretable intermediate variables in clinical models should be validated against the invariance structure of the training objective, not only against prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [98] [Speculate While You Reason: Teaching Agents to Predict Their Next Tool Call via Joint Agent-Speculator RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25816)
*Jiabao Ji,Yujian Liu,Li An,Rohit Jain,Gungor Polatkan,Siyu Zhu,Shiyu Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A self-speculating agent approach where a single LLM acts as both agent and speculator to hide tool-call latency by predicting its own next tool calls using the same model and KV cache.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language model agents waste substantial wall-clock time waiting for tool call results, creating latency issues. Existing speculation methods use separate draft models or cached traces that don't align well with the deployed agent's behavior, creating a speculator-agent gap.

Method: Introduces a self-speculating agent - a single model that operates in both agent mode (solving tasks) and speculator mode (predicting next tool calls from partial trajectories). Uses joint agent-speculator reinforcement learning with speculation targets derived from the agent's own rollouts, alternating agent and speculator updates while fully reusing prefix KV cache.

Result: Improves average next tool-call Hit@1 from 44.1 to 61.2 for Qwen3-4B and from 48.9 to 66.3 for Qwen3.5-4B across agentic search QA and conversational tool-use tasks, while preserving agent task success rates.

Conclusion: The target agent itself is a strong next-call speculator, enabling a simpler unified design where the same model serves as both agent and speculator. This approach effectively bridges the speculator-agent gap and reduces tool-call latency without compromising agent performance.

Abstract: Large language model agents often spend substantial wall-clock time waiting for tool call results. Tool-call speculation can hide this latency by predicting and pre-executing an agent's next tool call if the prediction matches the agent's eventual tool call, but existing speculators are typically separate draft models or cached traces that are poorly aligned with the deployed agent's own behavior. We identify this speculator-agent gap and show that the target agent itself is a strong next-call speculator. This points to a simpler design: unifying the agent and speculator within the same model. In this paper, we introduce the self-speculating agent, a single model that both solves tasks in agent mode and predicts its next tool call from partial trajectories in speculator mode, fully reusing prefix KV cache. To enable this dual-mode agent without degrading performance, we propose a joint agent-speculator reinforcement learning method, which derives speculation targets from the agent's own rollouts and alternates agent and speculator updates. Across agentic search QA and conversational tool-use agentic tasks, our method improves average next tool-call Hit@1 from 44.1 to 61.2 for Qwen3-4B and from 48.9 to 66.3 for Qwen3.5-4B, while preserving agent task success.

</details>


### [99] [Distributed Constraint Optimization via Online Learning and Iterative Pricing with Application to Large-Scale Satellite Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25835)
*Itai Zilberstein,Pranav Rajbhandari,Steve Chien,Tuomas Sandholm*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses large-scale DCOPs through two approaches: adapting online learning algorithms from potential games, and proposing a hierarchical decomposition framework with iterative pricing, achieving near-optimal results (99% fulfillment) on real-world satellite scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world distributed constraint optimization problems (DCOPs) are too large to solve monolithically, particularly in applications like decentralized satellite scheduling where communication is limited and scale is massive.

Method: 1) Revisits DCOP-potential game connection and adapts modern online learning algorithms for equilibrium finding to DCOPs. 2) Proposes decomposition framework separating DCOPs into meta-DCOP for task allocation and independent local optimization problems for scheduling. 3) Develops iterative pricing method to couple levels using feedback from local optimizers.

Result: The online learning algorithms are competitive with representative incomplete DCOP algorithms. The combined approach (online learning + iterative pricing framework) achieves near-optimal performance on real-world decentralized satellite scheduling, fulfilling over 99% of observation requests compared to 87% for state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: The paper successfully addresses large-scale DCOP challenges through a dual approach of adapting game theory techniques and developing novel decomposition frameworks with iterative coordination mechanisms, demonstrating superior performance on practical satellite scheduling problems.

Abstract: Distributed constraint optimization problems (DCOPs) provide a popular framework for distributed decision making under limited communication, but many real-world instances are too large to solve monolithically. We address this challenge from two complementary directions. We revisit the connection between DCOPs and potential games, and adapt modern online learning algorithms for equilibrium finding to DCOPs. We show that these algorithms are competitive with representative incomplete DCOP algorithms. We then turn to decomposition frameworks for large-scale DCOPs, motivated by large-scale decentralized satellite scheduling. We propose a new framework that separates a DCOP into two interacting subproblems: a high-level meta-DCOP for task allocation, and independent local optimization problems for scheduling. To couple the two levels, we develop a novel iterative pricing method that updates the meta-level utilities using feedback from the local optimizers. Combining our online learning methods with our iterative pricing framework, we obtain near-optimal performance on real-world decentralized satellite scheduling problem instances, fulfilling over 99% of observation requests compared with 87% for state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [100] [HiSkill: Empowering LLM Agents with Hierarchical Skill Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25853)
*Yu Hao,Jinxuan Cai,Qi Zhang,Yawen Li,Zhiqiang Zhang,Chuan Shi,Cheng Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HiSkill organizes LLM agent experience into hierarchical skill graphs connecting high-level skills to executable actions via typed relations, enabling efficient task execution with reduced token usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill methods produce flat collections of high-level textual skills stored independently, leaving skill relations underutilized and maintaining a gap between high-level skills and executable actions.

Method: HiSkill organizes interaction trajectories into directed graphs with skill nodes, AtomicOp nodes, and typed edges capturing decomposition, temporal transition, compatibility, support, and recovery relations. At inference, it retrieves compact task-relevant subgraphs and performs subgraph-guided execution with symbolic task states.

Result: Experiments on three interactive environments show HiSkill outperforms state-of-the-art baselines while reducing inference token consumption.

Conclusion: The hierarchical skill graph framework effectively bridges high-level skills and executable action grounding, demonstrating improved task execution efficiency in interactive environments.

Abstract: Skills have become an important abstraction for enabling large language model (LLM) agents to reuse past experience in long-horizon interactive tasks. However, existing trajectory-to-skill methods often produce flat collections of high-level textual skills that are stored and retrieved independently, leaving skill relations underutilized and maintaining a gap between high-level skills and executable actions. In this paper, we propose HiSkill, a hierarchical skill graph framework that organizes interaction trajectories into a directed graph with skill nodes, AtomicOp nodes, and typed edges. Specifically, the graph connects reusable high-level skills with executable action templates, while also capturing decomposition, temporal transition, compatibility, support, and recovery relations among them. At inference time, HiSkill retrieves a compact task-relevant subgraph and performs subgraph-guided task execution, where a symbolic task state, an active skill, and the retrieved subgraph guide the LLM agent to switch skills, select AtomicOps, and ground executable actions iteratively. Experiments on three interactive environments show that HiSkill outperforms state-of-the-art baselines while reducing inference token consumption, demonstrating the effectiveness of bridging high-level skills and executable action grounding through a hierarchical skill graph. Our data and code is available at https://github.com/BUPT-GAMMA/HiSkill.

</details>


### [101] [Runtime Uncertainty Monitoring for LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems Using Bayesian Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25877)
*Bart Custers,Koorosh Aslansefat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM framework for actuarial risk modeling with uncertainty quantification using token-level log-probabilities and Bayesian Networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Actuarial workflows are high-stakes decision-support settings where unreliable outputs from LLMs could lead to incorrect risk assessment, unfair pricing, and regulatory non-compliance. Uncertainty arises from the probabilistic nature of LLMs and dependencies between agents in multi-agent systems.

Method: Proposes a multi-agent framework with specialized agents (data preparation, modeling, review, explanation) under a central hub. Uses token-level log-probabilities transformed into calibrated task-level confidence estimates through length-normalization, then propagates uncertainty through a Bayesian Network.

Result: The framework reproduces baseline actuarial performance while providing additional insight into workflow stability and runtime uncertainty propagation.

Conclusion: Multi-agent LLM systems can support actuarial risk modeling when combined with proper uncertainty quantification methods that transform token-level probabilities into meaningful workflow-level confidence estimates.

Abstract: This paper investigates how multi-agent systems (MAS)-based on large language models (LLMs) can support actuarial risk modelling, with a particular focus on uncertainty quantification. Actuarial workflows represent a high-stakes decision-support setting where unreliable outputs may lead to incorrect risk assessment, unfair pricing, and regulatory non-compliance. To address uncertainty introduced by the probabilistic nature of LLMs and dependencies between agents, a multi-agent framework is proposed in which specialised agents perform data preparation, modelling, review, and explanation tasks under a central hub. The main contribution is a novel approach to uncertainty propagation using token-level log-probabilities and a Bayesian Network. Importantly, log probabilities are not treated as direct probabilities of correctness or task success. Instead, length-normalised log-probability summaries are transformed into calibrated task-level confidence estimates before incorporation into the Bayesian Network. Results show that the framework reproduces baseline actuarial performance while providing additional insight into workflow stability and runtime uncertainty propagation.

</details>


### [102] [Distributing Security Controls Through Harness Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25890)
*William Robert Gore*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A custom security harness called SHarD demonstrates that off-the-shelf security controls can be effectively implemented on commercial AI coding agents, achieving 100% security score matching best commercial agents with single-command distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI coding agents are being rapidly adopted but security concerns remain the main barrier to organizational scaling. Existing security controls aren't systematically distributed to engineering teams, and vendor-native solutions create ecosystem dependencies that may not fit all deployment contexts.

Method: Phased testing methodology across four agent configurations using 23-test suite from OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. Tested: commercial agents with/without controls, baseline harness, and security-hardened harness (SHarD). SHarD was built on Pi agent harness with OS sandboxing, skill scanning, and tool restriction controls.

Result: SHarD achieved adjusted score of 100%, matching best securely configured commercial agent, with no regression across test categories. Demonstrated three security control categories can be embedded and distributed via single install command while retaining equivalent efficacy to direct installation on commercial agents.

Conclusion: Off-the-shelf security controls can be effectively implemented on commercial AI coding agents through custom harnesses like SHarD, enabling secure scaling across distributed user bases. Model non-determinism produces inconsistent security outcomes, and OS sandboxing effectively mitigates boundary-crossing autonomous behaviors. Proposed initial characteristics for control harness fitness framework.

Abstract: AI coding agents are being adopted at historic speed, yet security and risk concerns remain the primary barrier to scaling agentic AI across organizations. Existing security controls for coding agents are not systematically distributed to engineering teams, and vendor-native solutions introduce ecosystem dependencies that may not suit every deployment context. This paper investigates whether off-the-shelf security controls can be implemented on commercial AI coding agents and scaled to a distributed user base via a custom agent harness. A phased testing methodology was applied across four agent configurations --- two commercial agents with and without controls, a baseline harness, and a security-hardened harness --- using a 23-test suite derived from the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications. SHarD (Secure Harness Distribution), a distributable harness built on the Pi agent harness, demonstrated that three categories of security controls --- OS sandboxing, skill scanning, and tool restriction --- can be embedded and distributed via a single install command while retaining equivalent efficacy to direct installation on commercial agents. SHarD achieved an adjusted score of 100\%, matching the best securely configured commercial agent, with no regression across any test category. Notable observations include evidence that model non-determinism produces inconsistent security outcomes and that autonomous agent behavior can cross system boundaries in ways that OS sandboxing directly mitigates. Initial characteristics toward a control harness fitness framework are proposed, and a third research question is identified for future investigation.

</details>


### [103] [Messier: A High-Resolution Corpus for Cross-Benchmark Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25891)
*Stefan Krsteski,Charlotte Meyer,Guillaume Allegre,Tony O'Halloran,Alexandre Sallinen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Messier is a unified corpus of 957,253 standardized evaluation records for AI agents across 30 benchmarks, providing infrastructure for agent capability scaling, benchmark auditing, and evaluation analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agent evaluation is fragmented with incompatible tasks, scaffolds, verifiers, and scoring rules across different benchmarks. Existing efforts are narrow in scope, limited in scale, or require costly reruns, making empirical results incomparable.

Method: Created Messier - a unified corpus of 957,253 records spanning 30 benchmarks, 714 agents, 11,891 tasks, and 74,205 verifiers. Standardized records by model, scaffold, environment, task, verifier, and aggregation rule. Included SOC/NAICS classifications for occupational analysis and supplemented with five-agent runs across six underrepresented domains including legal.

Result: Shows uneven progress across benchmark types: "function calling" saturated, "programming" improving fastest, "enterprise workflows" most challenging. Counterfactual rescoring reveals strict all-pass aggregation can obscure progress and alter rankings. Derived capability scales aligned with Epoch's Evaluation Capability Index (Spearman ρ=0.81), can be specialized by domain/occupation/action/verifier.

Conclusion: Messier provides foundational, reusable infrastructure for agent capability scaling, benchmark auditing, and fine-grained analysis of evaluation failures, addressing fragmentation in AI agent evaluation.

Abstract: Evaluating AI agents in interactive environments is hindered by fragmented tasks, scaffolds, verifiers, and scoring rules. Existing efforts focus on narrow settings, remain limited in scale, or require costly reruns, leaving much of the empirical record incomparable. We introduce Messier, a unified corpus of 957,253 records that span 30 benchmarks, 714 agents, 11,891 tasks, and 74,205 verifiers. Messier consolidates public benchmark scores and supplements them with five-agent runs across six underrepresented professional and scientific domains, including a recent legal benchmark. Each record is standardized by model, scaffold, environment, task, verifier, and aggregation rule, with SOC/NAICS classifications for occupational and industry analysis. Using this corpus, we show frontier progress is uneven across benchmark types, with "function calling" saturated, "programming" improving the fastest, and "enterprise workflows" remaining the most challenging. Furthermore, counterfactual rescoring shows that strict all-pass aggregation in multi-verifier tasks can obscure progress and artificially alter agent rankings. From these standardized records, we derive capability scales that align with Epoch's Evaluation Capability Index rankings at Spearman \r{ho} = 0.81 and can be specialized by domain, occupation, action space, or verifier type. Messier provides a foundational, reusable infrastructure for agent capability scaling, benchmark auditing, and fine-grained analysis of evaluation failures.

</details>


### [104] [Interactive Reward Agent: GUI Task Evaluation via Environment-State Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25904)
*Chenrui Shi,Yuwei Wu,Yang Liu,Ruining Feng,Zirui Shang,Zhi Gao,Lifeng Fan,Che Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IRA is an interactive reward agent for automated GUI task evaluation that uses a propose-then-verify framework to check task completion by interacting with environment states beyond just screenshots, achieving 86.9% accuracy on a new benchmark and enabling effective GUI agent training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automated GUI task evaluation methods are unreliable because they often rely only on screenshots of execution trajectories, missing crucial environmental state information like system configurations, file data, and application settings that are needed to accurately judge task completion.

Method: Propose-then-verify framework: 1) Given task instruction and post-execution GUI environment, IRA first proposes task completion conditions, 2) Then verifies these conditions by interactively invoking system tools, application tools, and GUI tools to gather evidence from both visible interfaces and underlying environment states.

Result: IRA achieves 86.9% accuracy on GUI-RewardBench (321 GUI task trajectories across 10 Ubuntu desktop apps), outperforming existing evaluator baselines. When applied to reinforcement learning of GUI agents, IRA enables achievement of 34.0% OSWorld success rate, demonstrating effective reward signals for training.

Conclusion: The interactive reward agent (IRA) with propose-then-verify framework provides reliable GUI task evaluation by accessing environment states beyond screenshots, enabling accurate automated evaluation that can serve as effective reward signals for both testing and training GUI agents.

Abstract: Graphical user interface task evaluation aims to determine whether a GUI agent has successfully completed a user instruction. Automated GUI task evaluation has received increasing attention because the evaluation results can serve as reward signals for both test-time scaling and post-training. However, reliable GUI task evaluation remains challenging because the judgments often require access to environment states, such as system configurations, file data, and application settings, beyond the screenshots of execution trajectories. In this paper, we propose an interactive reward agent (IRA) based on a propose-then-verify framework to acquire and verify evidence from the post-execution environment. Given a task instruction and a GUI environment after the GUI agent execution, IRA first proposes the task completion conditions and then verifies them by invoking system tools, application tools, and GUI tools. This design combines evidence from both visible interfaces and the environment state in an interactive process. We further introduce GUI-RewardBench, a benchmark of 321 GUI task trajectories spanning 10 Ubuntu desktop application categories. Experiments show that IRA achieves 86.9% accuracy on GUI-RewardBench, outperforming existing evaluator baselines. We further apply IRA to reinforcement learning of GUI agents, achieving a 34.0% OSWorld success rate, which demonstrates that IRA can provide effective reward signals for training GUI agents.

</details>


### [105] [Toward Standardized Cross-Vendor Agent Tool Trust Management in Autonomous Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25914)
*Ravi Kant Sharma,Ashutosh Uttam,Ajay Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentToolMO is a proposed 3GPP NRM information model that provides standardized cross-vendor trust management for AI agents in autonomous networks, enabling real-time trust degradation detection and containment to prevent cascading service failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous Network Levels 4-5 require AI agents to invoke tools across vendor boundaries without human oversight, but existing standards lack mechanisms for cross-vendor trust visibility. When a vendor's tool is compromised, agents from other vendors continue using it unaware, causing cascading service failures.

Method: AgentToolMO proposes a 3GPP NRM information model with: (1) formally defined trust state machine with provable graduated enforcement, (2) damped cascade propagation with bounded convergence, (3) cross-vendor trust notifications via existing Management Services interfaces, and (4) retroactive impact assessment through NRM dependency graph traversal.

Result: Simulation-based evaluation shows standardized cross-vendor notifications reduce blast radius from hours-scale undetected propagation to near-real-time containment. Cascade convergence is guaranteed in bounded iterations with sub-linear notification scaling across vendor domains. The framework operates within existing 3GPP infrastructure.

Conclusion: AgentToolMO provides a practical standardization pathway for trustworthy multi-vendor autonomous network management, leveraging existing protocols and infrastructure while preventing cascading failures through real-time trust visibility and containment.

Abstract: Autonomous Network Levels 4-5 require AI agents to invoke tools across vendor boundaries without human oversight, yet existing management standards lack a standardized mechanism for cross-vendor trust visibility. When a tool from Vendor B is compromised, agents from Vendor A continue invoking it -- unaware of the trust degradation -- causing cascading service impact. We present AgentToolMO, a proposed 3GPP NRM information model for agent tool trust management. The model comprises: a formally defined trust state machine with provable graduated enforcement, damped cascade propagation with bounded convergence, cross-vendor trust notifications via existing Management Services (MnS) interfaces, and retroactive impact assessment through NRM dependency graph traversal. Simulation-based evaluation across multi-vendor topologies shows that standardized cross-vendor notifications reduce blast radius from hours-scale undetected propagation to near-real-time containment bounded by MnS notification delivery, with cascade convergence guaranteed in bounded iterations and sub-linear notification scaling across vendor domains. The framework operates within existing 3GPP management infrastructure, leverages existing protocols, and provides a standardization pathway for trustworthy multi-vendor autonomous network management.

</details>


### [106] [Penelope: Localized Latent Recurrence for Efficient Structured Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25915)
*Yutong Chen,Shouqian Shi,Xinran Liu,Haochen Wang,Jiaying Wang,Tianxing Xu,Yuanxi Wang,Zirui Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Penelope introduces a latent-reasoning framework that localizes recurrent computation to a selected decoder interval in pretrained Transformers, enabling efficient structured reasoning without generating long visible reasoning traces or repeatedly executing the full decoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language models approach structured reasoning either by scaling up parameters (costly) or using chain-of-thought tokens that serialize intermediate steps (ties reasoning to autoregressive output length). Both approaches have efficiency limitations.

Method: Penelope uses a lower decoder prefix to create problem-conditioned boundary memory, then iteratively refines it through time-modulated GRU dynamics and recurrent readout states. A progressive CoT-to-latent curriculum transfers visible reasoning into internal recurrent paths, allowing computation in latent space without full decoder execution.

Result: Experiments on open-source structured-reasoning benchmarks show Penelope achieves competitive accuracy relative to established latent-reasoning models while reducing inference latency at validation-selected latent budgets.

Conclusion: Latent refinement can be localized to a narrow decoder interval, reducing repeated full-decoder execution without generating long visible reasoning traces, providing a practical accuracy-efficiency tradeoff for decoder-only Transformer models.

Abstract: Complex structured reasoning tasks often require additional computation, yet current language models obtain it mainly by increasing parameter scale or by serializing intermediate steps as chain-of-thought (CoT) tokens. The former raises training and deployment costs, while the latter ties reasoning computation to autoregressive output length. We introduce Penelope, an efficient latent-reasoning framework for pretrained decoder-only Transformers that localizes recurrent computation to a selected decoder interval. The lower decoder prefix is evaluated once to construct a problem-conditioned boundary memory, which is then iteratively refined through time-modulated GRU dynamics and recurrent readout states before answer generation. A progressive CoT-to-latent curriculum transfers visible reasoning into this internal recurrent path, allowing additional computation to be allocated in latent space without repeatedly executing the complete decoder or generating a long intermediate trace. Experiments on open-source structured-reasoning benchmarks show that, at validation-selected latent budgets, Penelope attains competitive accuracy relative to established latent-reasoning models while reducing measured inference latency. These results show that latent refinement can be localized to a narrow decoder interval, reducing repeated full-decoder execution without generating a long visible reasoning trace and providing a practical accuracy-efficiency tradeoff for decoder-only Transformer models.

</details>


### [107] [dtControl2+$\varepsilon$: Trading Optimality for Explainability in MDPs via Decision Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25925)
*Tereza Kinská,Jan Křetínský,Tobias Meggendorfer,Sabine Rieder,Maximilian Weininger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Extends dtControl2 with ε-optimal decision trees for Markov decision processes, allowing tunable simplification while guaranteeing performance within ε bounds


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decision tree controllers from tools like dtControl2 become too complex and not human-comprehensible for large systems or those with many corner cases. However, reducing tree size is challenging because missing crucial cases can make controllers incorrect.

Method: Extends dtControl2 with ε functionality: given allowed imprecision ε ≥ 0, constructs smaller decision trees that distill the controller's essence while guaranteeing ε-optimality. This enables tunably simpler explanations by omitting controllable amounts of detail.

Result: The tool constructs decision trees that are orders of magnitude smaller than the state of the art while maintaining ε-optimality guarantees.

Conclusion: The approach successfully addresses the complexity-explanation tradeoff in decision tree controllers for Markov decision processes, providing a practical method to obtain simpler, more comprehensible representations without sacrificing correctness guarantees.

Abstract: Over the past decade, decision trees have been used to represent controllers (a.k.a. policies) in an explainable way, with dtControl2 as a current state-of-the-art tool. However, for systems that are large or have many corner cases, even such representations tend to be too complex and not human-comprehensible. Unfortunately, reducing the size of the decision tree is not straightforward, as missing just a single crucial case might result in an incorrect controller. We tackle this issue in the setting of Markov decision processes, extending dtControl2 by "$\varepsilon$" functionality: Given an allowed imprecision $\varepsilon \geq 0$, we construct a smaller decision tree, distilling the essence of the controller, while still guaranteeing its $\varepsilon$-optimality. This enables us to provide tunably simpler explanations, omitting a controllable amount of detail. Our tool constructs decision trees that are orders of magnitude smaller than the state of the art.

</details>


### [108] [A Cost-Effective Multimodal LLM Reasoning Framework for Question Answering over Irregular Clinical Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25947)
*Frank Nie,Ethan B Liu,Yuan Zhu,Wei Fan,Jindong Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ClinPRISM is a multimodal LLM framework for question answering over irregular clinical time series that addresses sparsity and irregular sampling patterns through multi-scale encoding and token compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal time-series LLMs are poorly equipped to handle the sparsity, asynchrony, and irregular sampling patterns of clinical observations, limiting their effectiveness for healthcare QA applications.

Method: 1) Irregularity-aware multi-scale encoder to capture sparse clinical evidence at diverse temporal scales. 2) Temporal evidence distiller to integrate representations across scales and compress them into LLM-compatible tokens. 3) Progressive alignment strategy to align irregular trajectories with LLM's textual embedding space. 4) Constructed 30,000 clinical time series with multi-scale descriptions and 41,000 instruction-tuning instances for training.

Result: ClinPRISM achieves state-of-the-art performance on held-out evaluation benchmarks using only a 4-billion-parameter LLM backbone, 16 time-series tokens, and achieves an average inference latency of 0.15 seconds per question.

Conclusion: ClinPRISM provides a cost-effective solution for QA over irregular clinical time series by effectively addressing irregular sampling patterns while maintaining efficiency with minimal token usage and low latency.

Abstract: Question answering (QA) over irregular clinical time series (ICTS) plays a pivotal role in a wide range of healthcare applications. Although recent multimodal time-series large language models (LLMs) have shown considerable promise in general-purpose time-series QA, they remain poorly equipped to model the sparsity, asynchrony, and irregular sampling patterns of clinical observations. To fill this gap, we propose ClinPRISM, a cost-effective multimodal LLM reasoning framework for question answering over ICTS data. First, we devise an irregularity-aware multi-scale encoder to capture sparse clinical evidence at diverse temporal scales. Then, we propose a temporal evidence distiller to integrate representations across these scales and compress them into a small number of LLM-compatible tokens. Moreover, we introduce a progressive alignment strategy that sequentially aligns the irregular trajectories with the LLM's textual embedding space. To facilitate training, we construct 30,000 clinical time series paired with multi-scale descriptions, together with 41,000 instruction-tuning instances spanning 11 tasks. Using a 4-billion-parameter LLM backbone, ClinPRISM achieves state-of-the-art performance on the held-out evaluation benchmark while using only 16 time-series tokens and achieving an average inference latency of 0.15 seconds per question.

</details>


### [109] [Large Language Model for Operations Research Formulation Selection in Multi-Warehouse Inventory Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25956)
*Jintao Xu,Yingzheng Ma,Jiong Dong,Yongzhi Qi,Jianshen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A solver-guided LLM framework for instance-wise OR formulation selection in multi-warehouse inventory allocation problems improves selection accuracy and allocation quality over fixed formulations and preference-trained selectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-warehouse inventory allocation is typically formulated as MIP problems, but no single formulation consistently performs well across heterogeneous instance-level regimes influenced by demand concentration, inventory imbalance, replenishment scale, service constraints, and forecast volatility.

Method: Proposes a solver-guided LLM framework for OR formulation selection where each OR expert corresponds to a MIP formulation encoding distinct allocation priorities. Uses supervised fine-tuning (SFT) for schema learning, then converts solver-evaluated allocation-quality gaps into margin-weighted IPO preferences and expert-score metadata for reward lookup during Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO).

Result: GRPO substantially improves expert-selection accuracy relative to SFT+IPO selector, producing higher realized allocation quality than both preference-trained selector and best fixed formulation. Hit Ratio@1 increases from 21.45% to 50.42%, Hit Ratio@2 from 70.47% to 82.31%. Achieves 12.57 percentage point allocation accuracy gain over incumbent baseline and reduces gap to ex-post oracle to 4.85 percentage points.

Conclusion: The proposed solver-guided LLM framework with GRPO training effectively selects optimal OR formulations for multi-warehouse inventory allocation instances, significantly outperforming fixed formulations and demonstrating practical value for real-world e-commerce inventory management.

Abstract: Multi-warehouse inventory allocation is typically formulated as a mixed-integer programming (MIP) problem, yet no single formulation consistently matches heterogeneous instance-level regimes induced by demand concentration, inventory imbalance, replenishment scale, service constraints, and forecast volatility. We study this issue as instance-wise operations research (OR) formulation selection, where each allocation instance is assigned to a solver-executable formulation from a candidate OR expert library. We propose a solver-guided large language model (LLM) framework for OR formulation selection, in which each OR expert corresponds to a MIP formulation encoding a distinct allocation priority. To train the selector, the framework first constructs balanced expert-conditioned supervised fine-tuning (SFT) records for schema learning, and then uses MIP solver evaluation on historical instances to convert solver-evaluated allocation-quality gaps into margin-weighted identity preference optimization (IPO) preferences and per-instance expert-score metadata for reward lookup during group relative policy optimization (GRPO) to assign rewards to sampled responses. Experiments on multi-warehouse inventory allocation instances from JD$\mathord{.}$com, one of China's largest e-retailers, demonstrate that GRPO substantially improves expert-selection accuracy relative to the SFT+IPO selector and, more importantly, produces higher realized allocation quality than both the preference-trained selector and the best fixed formulation. With GRPO, Hit Ratio@1 and Hit Ratio@2 increase from 21.45% to 50.42% and from 70.47% to 82.31%. The resulting selector achieves an allocation accuracy gain of 12.57 percentage points over the incumbent baseline, outperforming both the SFT+IPO selector and the best fixed OR expert, and reduces the gap to the ex-post oracle to 4.85 percentage points.

</details>


### [110] [CHARM: A Multimodal Graph Foundation Model with Hierarchical Context Modeling for Zero-Shot Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26023)
*Ankang Yang,Jitao Zhao,Di Jin,Yuxiao Huang,Dongxiao He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CHARM is a multimodal graph foundation model using hierarchical context modeling for zero-shot transfer across graph domains without target-domain adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph foundation models struggle with zero-shot transfer on multimodal graphs. GNN-based models require downstream adaptation, while LLM-based methods mainly handle unimodal graphs or single domains. Collecting labels and adapting models for each new graph domain is costly and often infeasible.

Method: CHARM uses hierarchical context modeling that replaces isolated raw nodes with hierarchical graph contexts capturing multimodal semantics and cross-modal relations. These contexts map domain-specific node patterns to shared high-level concepts. A modality-aware graph context encoder integrates multimodal information with graph structure and converts representations into graph tokens for a large language model.

Result: Experiments show consistent improvements on zero-shot multimodal graph tasks, demonstrating the model's ability to transfer knowledge across domains without target-domain supervision or adaptation.

Conclusion: CHARM addresses key challenges in zero-shot transfer for multimodal graphs by using hierarchical context modeling to capture transferable cross-modal relations and disentangle domain-specific structures from shared concepts, enabling effective generalization to unseen domains.

Abstract: Graph foundation models (GFMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for transferring knowledge across graph domains and tasks. Real-world graphs associate nodes with text, images, and other modalities, making multimodal graphs essential for representing complex entities and relations. Moreover, collecting labels and adapting models for every new graph domain is costly and often infeasible, motivating zero-shot transfer. Unfortunately, zero-shot transfer on multimodal graphs remains underexplored. Existing GNN-based graph foundation models typically require downstream adaptation, whereas LLM-based graph methods mainly address unimodal graphs or tasks within a single domain. This setting presents two key challenges. First, models must generalize knowledge from individual modalities while capturing transferable cross-modal relations. Second, without target-domain fine-tuning, node representations remain entangled with domain-specific structures and modality-specific characteristics, obscuring shared concepts in unseen domains. To address these challenges, we propose CHARM, a multimodal graph foundation model with hierarchical context modeling for zero-shot transfer. CHARM replaces isolated raw nodes with hierarchical graph contexts that capture multimodal semantics and cross-modal relations. These contexts map domain-specific node patterns to shared high-level concepts, reducing reliance on target-domain supervision or adaptation. A modality-aware graph context encoder integrates multimodal information with graph structure and converts the resulting representations into graph tokens for a large language model . Experiments show consistent improvements on zero-shot multimodal graph tasks.

</details>


### [111] [Falling Behind Drives Unsafe Development in an Idealised AI Race Experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26034)
*Elias Fernández Domingos,The Anh Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Behavioral experiment on AI races shows unsafe development emerges from strategic dynamics (competitive pressure, opponent behavior) rather than individual risk preferences, suggesting policy should focus on reducing competitive pressure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the tension between speed and safety in technological races, particularly relevant to AI development debates where competitive pressure is argued to incentivize riskier, less safety-conscious development.

Method: Framed behavioral experiment using idealized AI race with paired participants repeatedly choosing between Safe and Unsafe development under uncertain time horizon. Three risk treatments (10%, 60%, 90% maximum risk). Evolutionary model with four strategies to interpret experimental results.

Result: No support for pre-registered hypotheses about risk levels or elicited risk preferences. Exploratory analyses show unsafe behavior shaped by strategic race dynamics: participants more likely to choose Unsafe after opponent does, being ahead reduces Unsafe play while falling behind increases it, and first-round choices predict later behavior. Evolutionary model reproduces treatment effect and shows conditional Unsafe behavior favored by competitive dynamics.

Conclusion: Unsafe development emerges from early behavioral momentum, opponent behavior, and fear of falling behind rather than from risk preferences alone. Policy implications: focus on reducing competitive pressure and promoting cooperation in AI development rather than only addressing individual risk.

Abstract: Technological races create tension between speed and safety: actors may gain by moving faster than competitors, even when risky development is harmful. This is prominent in debates about artificial intelligence (AI), where competitive pressure is often argued to incentivise riskier, less safety-conscious development. We study this using a framed behavioural experiment based on an idealised AI race, in which paired participants repeatedly chose between Safe and Unsafe development under an uncertain time horizon. Unsafe development gave faster progress and higher immediate payoffs but accumulated private risk up to a treatment-specific maximum of 10\%, 60\%, or 90\%; the race's competitive structure was held constant, and only this maximum risk varied. Neither the pre-registered comparison between risk levels nor the role of elicited risk preferences was supported by the data. Instead, exploratory analyses motivated by the task's repeated structure show that Unsafe behaviour is shaped less by risk preferences than by the evolving strategic state of the race: participants are more likely to choose Unsafe after their opponent does so, being ahead reduces Unsafe play while falling behind increases it, and first-round choices predict later behaviour. To interpret these effects we introduce a reduced evolutionary model with four strategies -- Always Safe, Always Unsafe, Conditionally Safe, and Conditionally Antisocial Safe -- which reproduces the treatment effect and shows how conditional Unsafe behaviour can be favoured by competitive race dynamics. Together, the experiment and model show that unsafe development can emerge from early behavioural momentum, opponent behaviour, and fear of falling behind, rather than from risk preferences alone, suggesting policy should focus on reducing competitive pressure and promoting cooperation in AI development rather than only individual risk.

</details>


### [112] [Desktop-Delta Bench: Do Computer-Use Models Understand Desktop GUI Transitions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26041)
*Abhishek Pillai,Samir Kumar Nayak,Yuan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DDB (Desktop-Delta Bench) is an offline step-level benchmark for computer-use agents that focuses on verifying causal action transitions in GUI interactions, rather than just end-task success or single-frame grounding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks for computer-use agents primarily measure end-task success or single-frame grounding, but don't isolate whether models can reconstruct the causal, task-relevant transitions produced by actions. This is crucial for rejecting stale observations, verifying progress, and recovering from failures in asynchronous desktop GUI environments where observations can be delayed, occluded, transient, or unrelated.

Method: The authors created Desktop-Delta Bench (DDB) - an offline step-level benchmark with 2,013 human-verified instances from novel, multi-app Linux trajectories across ~15 applications and 50 task domains. It targets 3 failure dimensions (state verification, source tracking, and context-aware control) through 2 complementary tasks: 463 3-frame temporal-ordering instances (including 105 with cross-trajectory decoys) and 1,550 before-after pairs labeled with 5 action types plus their payloads.

Result: Evaluation of 8 closed and open-source model families across 32 ordering and 16 single-action settings showed consistent gaps. Ordering tasks remain unsaturated (best non-decoy: 65.1%, decoy: 65.7%). Task context improved decoy identification by 6.9 percentage points but reduced non-decoy exact match by 2.2 points. Single-action results showed inferring action family is harder than locating it (click F1: 0.96 vs drag: 0.76), though recognized drags are generally localized well.

Conclusion: DDB complements end-to-end benchmarks by filling the missing diagnostic layer between GUI grounding and final task success, enabling targeted improvements to desktop computer-use agent verification, reliability, and recovery capabilities.

Abstract: Computer-use agents (CUAs) increasingly act through desktop GUIs to complete long-horizon tasks. Current benchmarks primarily measure end-task success or single-frame grounding. Neither isolates whether a model can reconstruct the causal, task-relevant transition produced by an action- crucial for rejecting stale observations, verifying progress, and recovering from failure. This is difficult because inference, remote input, app rendering, and screenshot capture are asynchronous: the next observation may be delayed, occluded, transient, or unrelated, then misread as progress and carried into subsequent planning. We introduce Desktop-Delta Bench (DDB), an offline step-level benchmark with 2,013 human-verified instances from novel, multi-app Linux trajectories across ~15 applications and 50 task domains. DDB trajectories targets 3 failure dimensions- state verification, source tracking, and context-aware control- through 2 complementary tasks: 463 3-frame temporal-ordering instances, including 105 with a cross-trajectory decoy, and 1,550 before-after pairs labeled from 5 actions + its payload. We evaluate 8 closed and open-source model families across 32 ordering and 16 single-action settings, observing consistent gaps. Ordering remains unsaturated: best non-decoy and decoy exact-match rates are 65.1% and 65.7%. Task context improves decoy identification by 6.9 percentage points but reduces non-decoy exact match by 2.2 points; error analysis reveals systematic copying of the presented A-B-C order. Single-action results show that inferring the action family is harder than locating it: click F1 is 0.96 vs, 0.76 for drag, while recognized drags are generally localized well. DDB, thus, complements end-to-end benchmarks by filling the missing diagnostic layer between GUI grounding and final task success, enabling targeted improvements to desktop CUA verification, reliability, and recovery.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [113] [FinAbstain: Uncertainty-Calibrated Multimodal RAG for Selective Financial Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24875)
*Dorothy Torres,Wei Cheng,Henan Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FinAbstain is a framework for uncertainty-calibrated multimodal RAG with selective prediction in financial forecasting, using abstention when evidence is insufficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can synthesize financial narratives but express high confidence even when evidence is sparse, stale, or contradictory, which is especially problematic in forecasting where different data sources may disagree.

Method: Multimodal RAG with selective prediction using point-in-time retrieval, multiple specialized agents (fundamental, news, technical, risk, verification), aggregation of probabilistic assessments with retrieval relevance, evidence contradiction, repeated-sample consistency, and historical calibration statistics, plus uncertainty calibration techniques and selective abstention.

Result: The paper presents simulated results (not empirical claims) showing calibrated abstention can trade coverage for lower selective error and drawdown, evaluated across various metrics including accuracy, calibration, risk-coverage, citation, trading, latency, and cost.

Conclusion: The contribution is a time-safe architecture, composite uncertainty formulation, and reproducible evaluation blueprint for evidence-grounded selective financial forecasting with calibrated abstention.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can synthesize financial narratives but may express high confidence when evidence is sparse, stale, or contradictory. This failure is especially consequential in forecasting, where filings, news, prices, volume, and technical signals can disagree. We present FinAbstain, a research framework for uncertainty-calibrated multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with selective prediction. A point-in-time retriever admits only information public at the forecast timestamp and supplies modality-specific evidence to fundamental, news, technical, risk, and verification agents. Their probabilistic assessments are aggregated with retrieval relevance, evidence contradiction, repeated-sample consistency, and historical calibration statistics. Temperature scaling, isotonic regression, conformal prediction, and a proposed hybrid uncertainty score are evaluated under a common chronological protocol. A controller predicts bullish, bearish, or neutral outcomes only when uncertainty is below a validated threshold; otherwise it abstains, requests evidence, reduces exposure, or routes the case to human review. The evaluation covers one- and five-day abnormal-return direction, twenty-day volatility intervals, and abstention decisions, using accuracy, calibration, risk--coverage, citation, trading, latency, and cost metrics. To make the design auditable before a full data collection is complete, we report explicitly labeled simulated results rather than empirical claims. These results illustrate the intended hypothesis: calibrated abstention may trade coverage for lower selective error and drawdown. The contribution is a time-safe architecture, a composite uncertainty formulation, and a reproducible evaluation blueprint for evidence-grounded selective financial forecasting.

</details>


### [114] [Human Preference aligned Tabular Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24880)
*Frederik Hoppe,Astrid Franz,Marianne Michaelis,Lars Kleinemeier,Udo Göbel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Standard tabular embeddings optimized for prediction tasks fail to produce human-preferred similarity rankings, requiring human-aligned evaluation for trustworthy similarity search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Task-agnostic tabular embeddings are widely used for similarity search in business systems like PLM, but current approaches are optimized for prediction tasks rather than human preference-aligned similarity rankings. Standard evaluation metrics are insufficient to assess embedding trustworthiness for similarity search.

Method: The authors propose a concrete evaluation procedure for human preference aligned similarity ranking, illustrating the problem through a Product Lifecycle Management (PLM) use case.

Result: The paper demonstrates that existing embedding approaches produce suboptimal similarity rankings that don't align with human preferences, revealing a gap between prediction-optimized embeddings and human-preferred similarity results.

Conclusion: Human preference aligned evaluation is a necessary and currently missing component for assessing embedding trustworthiness in similarity search applications, requiring new evaluation approaches beyond standard downstream metrics.

Abstract: Task-agnostic tabular embeddings are increasingly used for similarity search in real-world business systems such as Product Lifecycle Management (PLM). However, leading embedding approaches are optimized primarily for prediction tasks - not for producing human preference aligned similarity rankings. We argue that standard downstream metrics are insufficient to fully assess embedding trustworthiness for similarity search and that human preference aligned evaluation is a necessary and currently missing component. We present a concrete evaluation procedure and illustrate the problem through a PLM use case.

</details>


### [115] [Behavior-Driven Explainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24881)
*Caroline Dominik,Rolf Drechsler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BDX (Behavior-Driven Explainability) uses BDD scenarios from system specifications to automatically generate explanations at any development stage, demonstrated via a RISC-V processor case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As systems become more complex, especially safety-critical systems, it's increasingly difficult for teams to fully understand all aspects across the development lifecycle. Explainability is becoming essential for building trust in modern systems.

Method: The paper introduces Behavior-Driven Explainability (BDX), which leverages Behavior-Driven Development (BDD) scenarios. These structured scenarios describe expected system behavior as sequences of actions for each functionality, and can be directly translated into explanations.

Result: The approach is demonstrated through a case study focusing on explanation of exceptions in a RISC-V processor, showing how BDX supports system design by providing explanations derived from specifications.

Conclusion: BDX provides a practical method for achieving explainability by reusing existing BDD specifications, making it applicable across all development stages and abstraction levels while building trust in system designs.

Abstract: As system complexity has vastly increased, it has become significantly more challenging for a single person or a team to fully understand all aspects of an entire system. Particularly, this holds when considering all the different stages of a system's development life cycle, such as, e.g., design or maintenance. But especially for safety-critical systems it is essential that the final design can be trusted. Because of this, explainability is becoming an important requirement for modern systems. In this paper, we aim to achieve this goal by utilizing Behavior-Driven Development (BDD), where the expected system behavior is given in the form of structured scenarios. These scenarios give a sequence of actions for each functionality, and by this can be directly translated into explanations. We introduce this method of deriving explanations based on the specification as Behavior-Driven Explainability (BDX). While applicable at any development stage or abstraction level, a case study for the explanation of exceptions in a RISC-V processor shows the support this concept adds during system design.

</details>


### [116] [Eliminating Propagation Delay: Attention-Based Spatial-Temporal Fusion Graph Convolution Network for Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24885)
*Jinpeng Chen,Ziyu Yu,Tao Wang,Jun Ma,Hongbo Gao,Senzhang Wang,Zufeng Zhang,Kaimin Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel Attention-Based Spatial-Temporal Fusion Graph Convolution Network (A-STFGCN) that addresses propagation delay errors and computational efficiency issues in traffic flow prediction, achieving state-of-the-art performance on five real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph convolution-based traffic flow prediction models focus mainly on spatial-temporal and semantic correlations in topological relationships, but neglect two critical issues: 1) They overlook different information propagation delays between adjacent nodes in convolution, and 2) They stack too many complex structures, increasing computational time and compromising timeliness requirements.

Method: Proposes A-STFGCN with a spatial-temporal fusion block that removes propagation delay errors and captures both long-term and short-term temporal characteristics using a multi-head self-attention mechanism based on a mask matrix.

Result: Extensive experiments on five real-world datasets demonstrate the method achieves the best overall performance while maintaining good computation and data utilization efficiency compared with eight baseline methods.

Conclusion: The proposed A-STFGCN effectively addresses the limitations of existing approaches by handling propagation delays and improving computational efficiency, making it a superior solution for traffic flow prediction with practical utility in transportation systems.

Abstract: Predicting traffic flow is crucial to optimizing transportation systems and improving urban mobility. Many graph convolution-based models have been proposed to extract spatial-temporal features and predict traffic flow. However, most focus on spatial-temporal and semantic correlation in topological relationships. There are two primary problems to address. Firstly, the convolutional structure in the model focuses on utilizing static spatial dependencies and spatial-temporal relationships in topological structures, while neglecting the different information propagation delays between adjacent nodes in the convolution. Secondly, these methods often stack a large number of complex structures, resulting in a substantial increase in computational time during the model training phase, thereby disregarding the model's requirements for timeliness. In this paper, we propose a novel network called the Attention-Based Spatial-Temporal Fusion Graph Convolution Network (A-STFGCN). We design a spatial-temporal fusion block to extract the spatial-temporal feature correlations with propagation delay errors removed and to capture both long-term and short-term temporal characteristics of the data within a multi-head self-attention mechanism based on a mask matrix. Extensive experiments on five real-world datasets demonstrate that our method achieves the best overall performance while having good computation and data utilization efficiency compared with the eight baseline methods.

</details>


### [117] [Mechanisms of Width Scaling in Normalized Residual Networks: The Effective Alignment Dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24887)
*Jinhao Zhang,Zeyu Liu,Zicheng Yan,Yunquan Zhang,Guangming Tan,Fangming Liu,Daning Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Wider neural networks have larger effective alignment dimensions, reducing misalignment between training and test gradients, which predicts test-risk improvement without requiring asymptotic assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural network width theories focus on asymptotic limits but don't provide practical guidance on whether expansion directions identified from finite training data remain beneficial for unseen test data, creating a need for finite-sample analysis.

Method: Introduced the effective alignment dimension metric to measure signal-noise geometry of activation gradients. Derived exact mean and variance of inner product between independently estimated training and test gradients, obtaining finite-sample upper bound on misalignment probability. Integrated this certificate into train-test residual-expansion framework.

Result: Derived misalignment probability bound depends only on effective alignment dimension and effective sample size, requiring minimal assumptions. Experimental results across LLaMA-style Transformers, Pythia, and ResNet-20 show wider models have larger effective alignment dimensions and lower empirical misalignment, and alignment statistics predict held-out loss changes.

Conclusion: The effective alignment dimension is a practical, measurable quantity that characterizes gradient alignment and predicts test-risk improvement for neural network width expansion without requiring asymptotic analysis or strong distributional assumptions.

Abstract: Existing theories of neural-network width characterize asymptotic limits, but provide limited guidance on whether an expansion direction identified from finite training data remains beneficial on unseen data. We study this problem for function-preserving residual expansion and introduce the effective alignment dimension, a measurable quantity describing the signal-noise geometry of activation gradients. By deriving the exact mean and variance of the inner product between independently estimated training and test gradients, we obtain a finite-sample upper bound on misalignment probability. The bound depends only on the effective alignment dimension and an effective sample size, requiring finite second moments and a nonzero population gradient, without covariance spectral assumptions or prescribed width-growth rates. We integrate this certificate into the train-test residual-expansion framework, yielding a high-probability condition for test-risk improvement. Experiments across width-controlled LLaMA-style Transformers, Pythia, and ResNet-20 show that wider models exhibit larger effective alignment dimensions and lower empirical misalignment. Direct residual interventions confirm that the alignment statistic predicts the sign and magnitude of held-out loss changes.

</details>


### [118] [GAUGE: Grading Agent-Built Financial Models Without a Golden Answer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24889)
*Jiacheng Lu,Sinuo Wang,Wentao Zhao,Rui Sun,Cheng Hua,Tao Song,Hui Cai,Beidi Luan,Zhengze Wu,Lingjing Teng,Yijia He,Jing Li,Daxin Jiang,Zuo Bai,Haibing Guan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GAUGE is a new benchmark for evaluating AI-built financial models against professional analyst consensus rather than single-reference answers, showing current AI models are good at mechanical construction but weak at valuation judgment compared to human analysts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing financial model evaluation benchmarks use single-reference answers, but professional analysts often disagree on forecasts and valuations, so current benchmarks unfairly penalize reasonable alternative answers.

Method: GAUGE uses 1,001 analyst workbooks to establish observed-practice envelopes, with a 196-task evaluation set featuring three-layer practice envelopes, 56 auditable facets, eight validity gates, and deterministic structural checks. Validation includes 55-participant known-groups study and company-grouped cross-fitting.

Result: The best AI agent scored 53.4 on failure-aware φ₀ score (below all senior analysts and most juniors, but above student mean), passing 93% mechanical facets but only 78% judgment facets. Human performance: senior analysts 88.3, juniors 66.0, finance students 43.2.

Conclusion: Current AI agents are substantially better at mechanical model construction than valuation judgment, highlighting the need for benchmarks that measure against professional consensus rather than single-point answers.

Abstract: Financial models combine public disclosures with analyst assumptions to produce forecasts and valuations. While some components can be checked mechanically, forecasts, discount rates, and target prices often admit multiple reasonable answers. Existing benchmarks nevertheless tend to grade such outputs against a single expert reference. Using independently built analyst models for the same companies, we find that across 108 directed pairs covering 65 companies, the median single-reference score is 0.33, 92.6% score below 0.70, and no same-vintage pair agrees on implied price within 10%. Point-tolerance grading can therefore penalize disagreement already present among professionals. We introduce GAUGE, a benchmark for evaluating agent-built valuation models against observed analyst practice rather than a single point answer. GAUGE uses 1,001 vendor-classified analyst workbooks and a 196-task evaluation set, with a three-layer observed-practice envelope, 56 auditable facets, eight validity gates, and deterministic structural checks. We validate the benchmark with a 55-participant known-groups study, company-grouped cross-fitting, and judge-stability audits. On the failure-aware score $φ_0$, senior analysts average 88.3, juniors 66.0, and finance students 43.2. Across 24 agents and 1,011 scored generations, the best agent scores 53.4, above the student mean but below every senior and most juniors. It passes 93% of mechanical facets and 78% of judgment facets, with a fleet-median gap of 26 points. Current agents are substantially stronger at model construction than valuation judgment. We release the methodology, a gated de-identified data tier, a controlled training split, a versioned 48-task evaluation core, and a withheld refresh pool.

</details>


### [119] [LLM as Forecasting Planner: Training-Free Text Conditioning for Time-Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24892)
*Huu Hiep Nguyen,Dung Nguyen,Minh Hoang Nguyen,Dai Do,Hung Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LAF-Planner is a training-free framework that combines time-series foundation models (TSFMs) with large language models (LLMs) for text-conditioned forecasting by treating forecasting as a planning problem using Monte Carlo tree search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-conditioned time-series forecasting requires both reliable numerical forecasting (handled by TSFMs) and interpretation of contextual information (handled by LLMs), but directly combining them can distort temporal structure captured by TSFMs.

Method: Formulates forecasting as a planning problem: frozen TSFM acts as a simulator proposing numerical continuations, while LLM acts as policy and value function using Monte Carlo tree search. Uses "Ranker" LLM as policy and "Judge" LLM as value function.

Result: Experiments on Context-is-Key and Time-MMD datasets with two TSFM backbones (Chronos and TimesFM) and four LLMs show consistent improvements across model choices, supporting sequential search as effective training-free approach.

Conclusion: LAF-Planner successfully bridges the modality gap between numerical forecasting and textual reasoning without retraining either model, demonstrating that treating forecasting as a planning problem with LLMs guiding TSFM trajectories is an effective framework.

Abstract: Text-conditioned time-series forecasting predicts a series from both its numerical history and natural-language context, allowing forecasts to account for events and constraints that the past alone cannot reveal. This requires both reliable numerical forecasting and the ability to interpret contextual information. Time-series foundation models (TSFMs) provide strong numerical forecasts, while large language models (LLMs) can reason over text, but combining their strengths remains challenging because asking an LLM to generate or revise forecast values directly can distort the temporal structure captured by the TSFM. We instead formulate forecasting as a planning problem over TSFM-generated trajectories. The frozen TSFM acts as a simulator that proposes numerical continuations, while the LLM acts as a policy and value function that guides candidate selection and evaluates completed trajectories against the context. We instantiate this as \rc{} (\textbf{L}LM \textbf{A}s \textbf{F}orecasting \textbf{P}lanner), a training-free framework that bridges the modality gap without retraining either model, using Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) over the forecast horizon with a \emph{Ranker} LLM as policy and a \emph{Judge} LLM as value function. Experiments on Context-is-Key and Time-MMD across two TSFM backbones (Chronos and TimesFM) and four LLMs show that \rc{} delivers consistent improvements across model choices, supporting sequential search as an effective training-free approach to text-conditioned forecasting.

</details>


### [120] [Inverse RL Helps Align AI by Imitating Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24900)
*Michał Wiliński,Liu Leqi,Chirag Nagpal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PARED learns explicit reward functions from demonstration data using inverse reinforcement learning, enabling AI alignment without human preference annotations while supporting contextual adaptation to different audience preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current alignment methods (supervised fine-tuning or RL with human feedback) leave underexplored whether demonstrations alone can yield implicit rewards that can be inspected, reused, and optimized on-policy for AI alignment.

Method: PARED uses inverse reinforcement learning to recover implicit rewards from expert demonstrations as explicit functions over response-level features. It employs a lightweight discriminator to separate demonstrations from policy samples in feature space, requiring no task-specific preference annotations.

Result: The recovered reward improves base policies without supervised loss, yields additional gains after standard supervised fine-tuning, enables inference-time reranking and adversarial on-policy RL, and supports contextual alignment for different audience preferences.

Conclusion: PARED demonstrates that demonstrations alone can provide sufficient supervision for learning explicit reward functions, enabling more flexible and context-aware AI alignment without dependence on task-specific preference annotations.

Abstract: Language model alignment aims to make model behavior reliably reflect desirable properties such as helpfulness, safety, and instruction following. Current approaches typically use supervised fine-tuning on demonstrations or reinforcement learning with rewards derived from verifiers or human feedback. These paradigms leave an important question underexplored: can demonstrations alone yield an implicit reward that can be inspected, reused, and optimized on-policy to align AI? Motivated by inverse reinforcement learning, we introduce Projected Alignment Reward Estimated from Demonstrations (PARED). PARED recovers the implicit reward underlying expert demonstrations as an explicit function over a small set of response-level features, learned by a lightweight discriminator that separates demonstrations from the policy's own samples in this feature space. Unlike a standard reward model, PARED requires no task-specific preference annotations: demonstrations provide the task-specific supervision, which can be augmented with AI feedback as additional dimensions of supervision. Through experiments involving inference-time reranking and adversarial on-policy RL, we show that the recovered reward improves a base policy without a supervised loss and yields further gains when optimized after standard supervised fine-tuning. Additionally, we demonstrate that PARED can be used for contextual alignment, in which a single policy can be tailored to the preferences of different audiences.

</details>


### [121] [Multiclass Classification without Labels via Posterior Simplex Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24943)
*Raphaël Bonnet-Guerrini,Johann Ioannou-Nikolaides,Troels Petersen,Vincenzo Piuri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multiclass classification without labels using multiple unlabeled mixtures with different class proportions, extending binary CWoLa to K>2 classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many real-world classification problems, obtaining reliable instance-level labels is difficult or impossible, but it's often possible to collect unlabeled datasets from different sources/conditions with varying latent class proportions.

Method: Extends CWoLa to multiclass case (K>2) using multiple unlabeled mixtures with unknown class proportions. Shows Bayes-optimal mixture classifier maps data to (K-1)-simplex in mixture-posterior space. Proposes two approaches: 1) train standard classifier to distinguish mixture identities, then extract latent classes via simplex fitting, 2) use bottleneck architecture.

Result: Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and Galaxy10 DECaLS demonstrate that mixture identity alone can recover latent classes and their proportions in mixtures, narrowing gap between weakly supervised and fully supervised performance.

Conclusion: Provides mathematically grounded, scalable tool for multiclass discovery in label-scarce domains by leveraging geometry of mixture-posterior space without needing class labels or mixture proportions.

Abstract: In many classification problems, reliable instance-level labels are unavailable. However, it is often possible to construct weakly enriched unlabeled samples: datasets selected by different cuts, sources, populations, or experimental conditions that change latent class proportions without revealing them. Classification without Labels (CWoLa) shows that, in the binary case ($K=2$), a classifier trained to distinguish two impure mixtures with different class proportions can recover an optimal class discriminator without knowing the mixture proportions. We extend this principle to multiclass learning from several unlabeled mixtures ($K>2$), where the learner observes only mixture identity and neither latent class labels nor class-prior matrices. We prove that, for a multiclass mixture model, the Bayes-optimal mixture classifier $g^\star$ maps data points into a $(K-1)$-simplex embedded in mixture-posterior space. The $K$ vertices of this simplex are induced by the latent classes through the unknown mixing matrix. Leveraging this geometry, we propose prior-free procedures that train a standard classifier to distinguish mixture identities and then extract latent class structure using either post-hoc simplex fitting or a bottleneck architecture. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and Galaxy10 DECaLS show that mixture identity alone can recover latent classes and their fractions in the mixture. By narrowing the gap between weakly supervised and fully supervised performance, we provide a mathematically grounded, scalable tool for multiclass discovery in label-scarce domains.

</details>


### [122] [Stable FP4 Training via Transposition-Invariant Block Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24953)
*Mehdi Rahimifar,Amin Darabi,Mehran Taghian Jazi,Xing Huang,Yao Wang,Zhijun Tu,Yufei Cui,Yunke Peng,Hongliang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel 2D block FP4 quantization framework enables stable low-precision LLM training by addressing scale inconsistency issues from tensor transposition, achieving near-BF16 performance with minimal degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current FP4 training for large language models is unstable due to scale inconsistency problems in existing microscaling approaches, specifically caused by tensor transposition creating biased gradient updates.

Method: Proposes a 2D block FP4 quantization framework with transposition-invariant scaling, truncation-free scaling, stochastic rounding for error control, and mixed-precision design using MXFP8 for attention query/key projections.

Result: Achieves stable end-to-end FP4 training on LLMs up to 7B parameters and 30B Mixture-of-Experts models, trained on 100B tokens, with less than 1.3% degradation in perplexity and downstream accuracy compared to BF16.

Conclusion: Enforcing forward-backward scaling consistency through 2D block quantization is sufficient for practical FP4 training at scale, providing an effective pathway for more efficient LLM training.

Abstract: Reducing training precision is a key lever for improving the e ciency of large language model (LLM) training, but pushing beyond FP8 to 4-bit oating point (FP4) remains challenging due to instability during optimization. We identify a fundamental source of this instability in existing microscaling approaches: scale inconsistency induced by tensor transposition. In conventional 1D block quantization, forward and backward passes assign di erent scaling factors to the same values after transposition, leading to biased and unstable gradient updates. To address this issue, we propose a low-precision training framework based on 2D block FP4 quantization, which enforces transposition-invariant scaling and preserves consistency between forward and backward computations. We further combine this with truncation-free scaling and stochastic rounding to control quantization error and maintain unbiased gradients.
