To handle the sensitivity of attention mechanisms, we adopt MXFP8 quantization for query and key projections, yielding a practical mixed-precision design. We evaluate our method on dense LLMs up to 7B parameters and a 30B Mixture-of-Experts model, trained on up to 100B tokens. Across all settings, our approach achieves stable end-to-end FP4 training and closely matches BF16 performance, with less than 1.3% degradation in perplexity and downstream accuracy. These results demonstrate that enforcing forwardbackward scaling consistency is su cient to enable practical FP4 training at scale, providing a simple and e ective pathway toward more e cient LLM training.

</details>


### [123] [Generative Distributionally Robust Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24983)
*Ziwei Zhang,Jonathan Yu-Meng Li,Zhihao Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GDRO is a framework that uses any sampleable conditional generator as the nominal model while restricting worst-case laws to a chosen generator family, using sampler-Sinkhorn pairing to compare distributions without likelihood access, with strong empirical performance improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DRO approaches with generative models have limitations: methods accepting arbitrary samplers don't restrict worst-case laws to generator families, while generator-parameterized adversaries require model-specific access like likelihoods, scores, or training data. There's a need for a principled framework that works with any conditional generator while maintaining proper adversarial structure.

Method: Proposes Generative Distributionally Robust Optimization (GDRO) framework that accepts any sampleable conditional generator as nominal model and restricts worst-case laws to a chosen conditional generator family. Key innovation is the sampler-Sinkhorn pairing: samplers represent conditional laws exactly, while Sinkhorn divergence compares induced distributions without needing likelihood access (estimatable from samples alone). Enables finite-sample approximation and differentiable primal-dual implementation at active decision context.

Result: For Lipschitz losses, the population Sinkhorn radius bounds downstream degradation. Empirical results show: across explicit and implicit generators, reduces rare-context inventory regret by 60% and SocialGAN navigation collisions by 50% relative to nominal decisions.

Conclusion: GDRO provides a principled framework for distributionally robust optimization with generative models that works with any sampleable conditional generator while properly restricting adversarial distributions to generator families, achieving significant practical improvements without requiring model-specific access to likelihoods or training data.

Abstract: Generative models are increasingly adopted in distributionally robust optimization (DRO), but existing approaches trade off model compatibility and adversarial structure: methods that accept arbitrary samplers do not restrict worst-case laws to a generator family, while generator-parameterized adversaries rely on model-specific access such as likelihoods, scores, or training data. We propose Generative Distributionally Robust Optimization (GDRO), a principled framework that accepts any sampleable conditional generator as the nominal model and restricts worst-case laws to a chosen conditional generator family. The key is the sampler-Sinkhorn pairing: samplers represent the conditional laws exactly, while Sinkhorn divergence compares their induced distributions without likelihood access and can be estimated from samples alone. The resulting population problem admits a direct finite-sample approximation and differentiable primal-dual implementation at the active decision context. For Lipschitz losses, the population Sinkhorn radius bounds downstream degradation. Across explicit and implicit generators, our method reduces rare-context inventory regret by 60% and SocialGAN navigation collisions by 50% relative to nominal decisions.

</details>


### [124] [Calibrated Partial Resets: Preventing Policy Collapse in Continual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24996)
*Luc McCutcheon,Evangelos Chatzaroulas,Saber Fallah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CPR is a novel optimizer that partially resets low-utility neurons toward initialization with utility-scaling, maintaining plasticity without destabilizing training in continual learning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks suffer from accumulating dormant neurons and loss of expressivity during training, especially in non-stationary data settings like continual learning. Full neuron resets can sacrifice peak performance and cause training instability/policy collapse, creating a need for more balanced approaches.

Method: Calibrated Partial Resets (CPR) periodically pulls low-utility neurons toward their initialization, with pull strength scaled by each neuron's utility. Unlike binary resets, it uses partial adjustments; unlike uniform decay, it concentrates adjustments on units that need it most based on their utility.

Result: CPR avoids policy collapse over 400M training steps in SlipperyAnt and outperforms prior decay and reset-based methods on Continual MetaWorld and Continual MinAtar benchmarks. Ablations reveal a tunable trade-off between plasticity and peak performance.

Conclusion: Utility-scaled partial reinitialization is a promising direction for continual learning, offering a way to preserve plasticity without destabilizing training, as demonstrated by CPR's superior performance compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Neural networks are hindered by accumulating dormant neurons and loss of expressivity throughout training, particularly in non-stationary data settings, such as continual supervised and reinforcement learning. Recently, neuron resets have been used to maintain gradient flow and restore plasticity. However, full unit reinitialization often sacrifices peak performance and can destabilize training, leading to policy collapse.
  To preserve plasticity without destabilizing training, we propose Calibrated Partial Resets (CPR), an optimizer that periodically pulls low-utility neurons toward their initialization, with pull strength scaled by each neuron's utility. Unlike binary reset methods, partial resets avoid brittleness; unlike uniform decay, calibrated utility-scaling concentrates adjustment on the units that need it most.
  Among compared methods, only CPR avoids policy collapse over 400M training steps in SlipperyAnt, and it outperforms prior decay and reset-based methods on Continual MetaWorld and Continual MinAtar benchmarks. Ablations reveal a tunable trade-off between plasticity and peak performance, highlighting utility-scaled reinitialization as a promising direction for continual learning.

</details>


### [125] [Conformal Cascade: Distribution-Free Accuracy Guarantees for Multi-Tier LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25018)
*Yifan Dou,Shikan Fang,Shibo Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conformal Cascade (CC) is a multi-tier LLM inference framework that uses conformal prediction set size as a deferral rule, providing distribution-free finite-sample accuracy guarantees without requiring confidence calibration or threshold tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM cascades use confidence thresholds for routing queries, but LLM confidence scores are miscalibrated, thresholds require per-model/domain tuning, and no setting provides formal accuracy bounds.

Method: CC uses conformal prediction set size as the deferral rule: accept when the calibrated set collapses to a single answer, defer otherwise. It provides a distribution-free finite-sample accuracy guarantee with per-tier union bounds.

Result: Across 18 multiple-choice benchmarks, CC strictly improves over calibration-tuned heuristic cascades on most model family-benchmark pairs, with largest gains on reasoning-heavy benchmarks. On easier benchmarks, it routes most queries to small models with no accuracy cost.

Conclusion: CC provides formal accuracy guarantees without model training or white-box access, outperforming heuristic confidence-based cascades, though extension to open-ended generation requires future work on answer clustering.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) cascades reduce inference cost by routing easy queries to a small model and deferring hard queries to a larger one. Production cascades govern this deferral through a confidence threshold, but LLM confidence scores are miscalibrated, the threshold must be tuned per model pair and per domain, and no setting yields a formal bound on cascade accuracy. We introduce \textbf{Conformal Cascade} (CC), a multi-tier inference framework that uses conformal prediction set size as the deferral rule: accept when the calibrated set collapses to a single answer, defer otherwise. The procedure delivers a distribution-free, finite-sample accuracy guarantee. By a per-tier union bound, the prediction set at the accepting tier covers the correct answer with probability at least $1 - Kα$ for any user-specified $α$; under a selection-preservation condition (consistent with, but not strictly implied by, our marginal coverage results), the bound tightens to $1 - α$. We further characterise expected cascade cost as an explicit function of $α$ and the calibration-set acceptance rate. Across 18 multiple-choice benchmarks spanning science, medicine, commonsense, and standardized exams, evaluated on two-tier cascades drawn from four open-weight model families, CC strictly improves over the strongest calibration-tuned heuristic cascade on the majority of family--benchmark pairs, with the largest gains on reasoning-heavy benchmarks where majority vote is unreliable; on easier benchmarks the cascade commits the vast majority of queries to the small model at no accuracy cost. Extension to open-ended generation requires an answer-clustering step that we leave for future work. The method requires no model training and only black-box API access.

</details>


### [126] [Lantern: Conflict-Aware Gradient Blending for Physics-Guided Diffusion Models in Calorimeter Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25060)
*Farzana Yasmin Ahmad,Vanamala Venkataswamy,Geoffrey Fox*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Lantern is a physics-guided diffusion model for fast calorimeter shower simulation that improves correlation fidelity while maintaining sample quality by integrating physics-aware losses through a novel gradient blending technique.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diffusion models for calorimeter shower simulation have limitations: their denoising objective is purely statistical (ignoring physical correctness), and existing physics-informed methods cannot be applied because showers don't have closed-form governing laws or per-sample constraints. Standard metrics also fail to evaluate correlation structure across calorimeter layers and voxels.

Method: Introduced Correlation Frobenius Distance (CFD) metric for evaluating correlation fidelity. Proposed Lantern: a physics-guided diffusion surrogate with two physics-aware auxiliary losses (variance-stabilized voxel residual loss and graph Laplacian loss over detector geometry) integrated via GradBlend - a technique that anchors step magnitude to denoising gradient while letting auxiliary losses steer direction.

Result: Lantern improves both Frechet Physics Distance (FPD) and CFD on CaloChallenge Dataset 2. GradBlend successfully integrates physics losses without regression, while traditional multi-task learning methods (PCGrad, GradNorm, etc.) inflate FPD by 2-100x. The voxel residual loss requires terminal denoising-only phase due to gradient conflicts, while the Laplacian loss is schedule-insensitive.

Conclusion: The paper presents an effective approach for physics-guided generative modeling in calorimeter shower simulation, demonstrating that careful integration of physics constraints through gradient steering can improve correlation fidelity without sacrificing sample quality, addressing key limitations of purely statistical diffusion models.

Abstract: Monte Carlo simulation of calorimeter showers is a principal bottleneck for the High-Luminosity LHC, and diffusion models have emerged as fast, high-fidelity surrogates. Their denoising objective is purely statistical, however: a model can minimize it while placing the physics wrong. Existing physics-informed generative methods cannot close this gap, because they assume a closed-form law, a governing PDE residual or a hard per-sample constraint, that a shower does not supply: no per-sample PDE governs a stochastic cascade, and energy conservation fixes only one scalar per shower. Standard metrics ignore the correlation structure across calorimeter layers and voxels, comparing showers only in a physics feature space. We address both gaps. We introduce the Correlation Frobenius Distance (CFD), a single normalized score for correlation fidelity at layer-wise and voxel-wise scales. We then encode the soft per-sample structure available in a shower as two physics-aware auxiliary losses: a variance-stabilized voxel residual loss grounded in counting statistics, and a graph Laplacian loss over the detector geometry. We combine both with denoising through GradBlend, which anchors the step magnitude to the denoising gradient while letting the auxiliary steer its direction, yielding Lantern, a physics-guided diffusion surrogate. On CaloChallenge Dataset 2, injecting the physics losses through task-symmetric rules such as PCGrad, GradNorm, IMTL-G, and ConFIG inflates FPD by 2-100x relative to denoising alone, whereas GradBlend admits the same signal without regression and, with the Laplacian loss, Lantern improves both FPD and CFD. Our ablation on the auxiliary loss scheduler shows that the voxel residual loss, whose gradient conflicts with denoising, requires a terminal denoising-only phase to preserve shower fidelity, whereas the non-conflicting Laplacian loss is insensitive to the schedule.

</details>


### [127] [Score-Based Stabilization for Time-Dependent Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25119)
*Eshed Gal,Eldad Haber,Uri Ascher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Score-based stabilization framework for PDE simulation that uses learned score models to correct numerical updates, enforcing physical consistency and improving robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard numerical simulations of PDEs can suffer from nonphysical instabilities and fail to preserve qualitative dynamics. There's a need for stabilization methods that can enforce physical structure and consistency during time-stepping.

Method: Proposes a score-based stabilization framework where a learned score model defines a stabilization operator. This operator is applied to provisional numerical updates in standard time-stepping schemes, driving iterates toward the manifold of physically admissible states through corrective actions.

Result: The stabilization operator acts as a contraction toward the admissible manifold, providing basin-conditional stability. Numerical experiments on Advection, KdV, Nonlinear Schrodinger, and Burgers' equations show improved robustness, suppression of nonphysical instabilities, and better preservation of qualitative dynamics.

Conclusion: Score-based stabilization provides an effective framework for enhancing numerical PDE simulations by leveraging learned physical priors to enforce consistency and improve stability while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: We propose a score-based stabilization framework for numerical simulation of partial differential equations, in which a learned score model defines a stabilization operator applied to provisional numerical updates. This operator augments standard time-stepping schemes by enforcing structure and physical consistency through a correction that drives iterates toward the manifold of admissible states. We show that the stabilization operator acts as a contraction toward this manifold, yielding a correction mechanism with basin-conditional stability. Numerical experiments on Advection, Korteweg-de Vries (KdV), Nonlinear Schrodinger (NLS), and Burgers' equations demonstrate improved robustness, suppression of nonphysical instabilities, and preservation of qualitative dynamics.

</details>


### [128] [Semantic Space Search Trajectory Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25122)
*Julian Agudelo,Alberto Tonda,Gabriela Ochoa,Vincent Guigue,Cristina Manfredotti,Evelyne Lutton*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Semantic Space Search Trajectory Networks (STNs) extend STNs to high-dimensional semantic spaces using discretization via clustering, enabling comparison of learning dynamics across different ML algorithms and revealing structural differences in generalization behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional STNs are limited to low-dimensional or combinatorial settings due to their reliance on search space discretization. This prevents analysis of learning algorithms in high-dimensional semantic spaces where most machine learning operates.

Method: Proposes constructing STNs in semantic spaces by discretizing semantic vectors (model predictions on fixed sample sets) via agglomerative clustering with complete linkage under normalized Hamming distance, enabling comparison of any predictor regardless of algorithm family.

Result: Applied semantic STNs to classification/regression tasks with different algorithms, recovering known qualitative differences. Showed training on real labels produces denser, more efficient, centralized graphs than training on shuffled labels (Zhang et al. randomization regime), revealing consistent structural differences.

Conclusion: Semantic space STNs capture functional training dynamics from algorithm-data interactions, providing a tool for analyzing and comparing learning dynamics across diverse ML models and training regimes in high-dimensional spaces.

Abstract: Search Trajectory Networks (STNs) are a graph-based tool for visualizing and characterizing the behavior of optimization algorithms. STNs' reliance on discretization of the search space has largely confined them to low-dimensional or combinatorial settings. We introduce a methodology for constructing STNs in semantic spaces, defined as the space of a model's predictions on a fixed sample set. Our approach discretizes semantic vectors and aggregates them into network nodes via agglomerative clustering with complete linkage under a normalized Hamming distance. Since any predictor can be summarized by its semantic vector, this method enables comparison of learning dynamics across otherwise incomparable algorithm families. We apply semantic space STNs to classification and regression tasks solved using different machine learning algorithms, recovering known qualitative differences between them. Additionally, we use semantic space STNs to study neural network generalization by contrasting standard training with the label randomization regime of Zhang et al. (2017). The resulting STNs exhibit consistent structural differences, training on real labels produces denser, more efficient and more centralized graphs than training on shuffled labels. Together, our results show that semantic space STNs capture functional training dynamics arising from the interaction between learning algorithms and data, providing a tool for analyzing and comparing learning dynamics across machine learning models and training regimes.

</details>


### [129] [Endpoint Replay: Compressing the Recency Buffer in Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25123)
*Parham Mohammad Panahi,Armin Ashrafi,Haoyu Du,Andrew Patterson,Martha White,Adam White*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A compression method for experience replay stores representative transitions from n-step sequence endpoints in a smaller buffer, matching large buffer performance with 10x less storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experience replay typically uses large, uniformly sampled recency buffers (1M transitions) without major improvements beyond prioritized replay. The paper questions whether we can store less data, reduce redundancy, or better chain experiences to speed value propagation while maintaining performance.

Method: The approach uses simple compression by storing representative transitions derived from the endpoints of connected n-step sequences. These curated endpoints are maintained in a smaller recency buffer, effectively maintaining memory horizon comparable to large buffers.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows the method prevents systematic bias inherent in naive compression strategies and matches performance of traditional large buffers in both Pinball environment and Atari 2600 benchmark, while requiring an order of magnitude less storage.

Conclusion: The proposed compression approach effectively reduces experience replay buffer size by 10x while maintaining performance, offering a practical improvement for deep reinforcement learning systems.

Abstract: Experience replay remains one of the most practical and useful algorithmic tools in the deep reinforcement learning (DRL) toolbox. Aside from the limited success of prioritized replay and specialized approaches for large asynchronous systems, most DRL algorithms make use of a large, uniformly sampled recency buffer---even the size, one million, remains unchanged. Could we store less data, reduce redundancy, or more effectively chain experience together to speed up value propagation and still retain the performance of large buffers? In this paper, we investigate a simple compression approach that stores representative transitions derived from the end-points of a chain of connected $n$-step sequences. By curating these end-points in a smaller recency buffer, our method maintains an effective memory horizon comparable to a standard large buffer while requiring an order of magnitude less storage. Through empirical evaluation, we demonstrate that this approach prevents the systematic bias inherent in naive compression strategies and matches the performance of traditional large buffers in the Pinball environment and the Atari 2600 benchmark.

</details>


### [130] [Interpretable GOHR Agents via Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25132)
*Shiwei Tan,Yusong Zhao,Weiyi Qin,Wentian Wang,Jacob Feldman,Lazaros K. Gallos,Paul B. Kantor,Vladimir Menkov,Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transformers trained on the Game of Hidden Rules implicitly learn rule representations without explicit labels, and sparse autoencoders can extract interpretable concepts like probing strategies from their decision embeddings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A central challenge in AI interpretability is determining whether learned decision-making systems contain internal concepts that explain their behavior, especially when systems must infer rules implicitly from interaction history without explicit rule labels.

Method: Train a tokenized autoregressive Transformer agent on a two-rule Game of Hidden Rules task where both rules map object shapes to target buckets with different permutations. The agent never receives rule labels and must infer rules from interaction history. Then train sparse autoencoders (SAEs) on the agent's decision-token embeddings to extract interpretable representations.

Result: SAEs successfully recover rule structure from the Transformer's embeddings. SAE dimensions show high selectivity for simple concepts like chosen shapes or buckets, covering most decisions where those concepts are present. Individual SAE dimensions correspond to interpretable strategies such as probing one rule hypothesis and switching after negative feedback.

Conclusion: Transformer agents can implicitly learn rule representations in environments with hidden rules, and sparse autoencoders provide an effective interpretability method for extracting meaningful concepts and strategies from their decision-making process, even when rules must be inferred from interaction history.

Abstract: A central challenge in interpreting learned decision-making systems is to determine whether their internal representations contain concepts that help explain their behavior. We report interpretability experiments for a tokenized autoregressive Transformer agent in the Game of Hidden Rules (GOHR). We focus on a compact two-rule task in which both hidden rules map object shapes to target buckets, but with different permutations. The policy is trained on episodes sampled from these two hidden rules and then evaluated with fixed weights. It is never given a rule label and does not use an explicit rule classifier; any rule information must be inferred implicitly from interaction history. In this setting, the correct rule is not identifiable before the agent tries an informative move and observes accept/reject feedback. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) trained on the agent's decision-token embeddings recover this structure. When held-out decisions are labeled by simple concepts such as the chosen shape or bucket, SAE dimensions that are highly selective for a concept cover most decisions where that concept is present. Individual SAE dimensions also correspond to interpretable strategies such as probing one rule hypothesis and switching after negative feedback.

</details>


### [131] [Physics-Informed CNN-LSTM for Street-Scale Urban Flood Prediction: Reconciling Aggregate Accuracy and Street-Level Plausibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25148)
*Luc DCosta,Yidi Wang,Jonathan L. Goodall,Rohan Chandra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-informed CNN-LSTM flood prediction model uses three differentiable loss terms (gravity, continuity, TWI-modulated false-alarm penalty) to produce physically-constrained urban flood predictions, balancing accuracy with physical plausibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard deep learning surrogate models trained with mean-squared-error produce statistically accurate but physically unconstrained flood predictions (water flowing uphill, appearing spontaneously, smoothing over street-level features), which limits practical applications like traffic routing.

Method: Developed physics-informed training framework for CNN-LSTM models predicting urban flood depths at 15 min intervals on 128x128 grid. Uses three differentiable penalty terms: (1) gravity loss penalizing depth increases against water-surface-elevation gradient, (2) continuity loss enforcing local mass conservation with rainfall-adaptive thresholds, (3) topography-aware false-alarm penalty modulated by topographic wetness index (TWI). Evaluated on Norfolk, Virginia flood dataset with two storm events.

Result: Physics-constrained model achieves near-zero gravity violations (order 1e-6) and highest street-channel recall (0.77±0.09 vs 0.44±0.10 baseline). Advantage more than doubles on held-out storm. TWI-modulated penalty recovers 60% higher street recall at lowest MAE among constrained variants and best street-level F1. Shows fundamental tension between pixel-level error and physical plausibility.

Conclusion: Terrain-aware loss modulation offers principled resolution to tension between aggregate pixel-level error and application-specific physical plausibility. Physics-informed training can produce both accurate and physically-constrained flood predictions essential for practical applications like traffic routing.

Abstract: Deep learning surrogate models trained with mean-squared-error loss produce statistically accurate but physically unconstrained flood predictions: water may flow uphill, appear spontaneously, or smooth over street-level corridors. We develop a physics-informed training framework for CNN-LSTM models that predict urban flood depths at 15 min intervals over a 128x128 spatial grid. Three differentiable penalty terms are embedded into the loss: (i) a gravity loss penalizing depth increases against the water-surface-elevation gradient, (ii) a continuity loss enforcing local mass conservation with rainfall-adaptive thresholds, and (iii) a topography-aware false-alarm penalty modulated by the topographic wetness index (TWI). We evaluate on the Norfolk, Virginia flood dataset spanning two storm events (August 2017 and September 2022, 300 samples), with all variants trained on identical splits and robustness assessed over repeated random splits and leave-one-storm-out tests. A road-proximal evaluation restricted to a TWI-derived street mask quantifies street-level skill. The physics-constrained model achieves near-zero gravity violations (order 1e-6) and the highest street-channel recall (0.77 +/- 0.09 vs 0.44 +/- 0.10 for the unconstrained baseline), the capability most relevant to traffic routing, and its advantage more than doubles on a held-out storm; a uniform false-alarm variant attains 16% lower mean absolute error but suppresses street recall to 0.25. The TWI-modulated penalty reconciles this trade-off: it improves on the uniform variant on every metric, recovering 60% higher street recall at the lowest MAE among constrained variants and the best street-level F1. These results expose a fundamental tension between aggregate pixel-level error and application-specific physical plausibility, and show that terrain-aware loss modulation offers a principled resolution.

</details>


### [132] [Accurate structural modeling of chemically diverse molecular interfaces with Vilya-2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25156)
*Vilya Research,:,Pascal Sturmfels,Naozumi Hiranuma,Milad Salem,Benjamin D. Sellers,Stephen Rettie,CJ San Felipe,Chase A. P. Wood,Jeffrey K. Holden,Adam P. Moyer,Patrick J. Salveson,Ivan Anishchanka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vilya-2 is an advanced diffusion transformer that accurately models peptide-protein interactions and small-molecule docking, outperforming existing methods and enabling de novo peptide design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current structure-prediction networks based on co-evolutionary statistics work well for proteins but fail for peptide therapeutics due to their non-canonical residues, macrocyclization, and complex topologies. There's a need for accurate modeling of peptide-target interactions for therapeutic design.

Method: Vilya-2 extends Vilya-1's all-atom representation using a diffusion transformer approach to model molecular interactions with protein targets. It enables transfer learning between different molecular types and generates diverse structural ensembles ranked with calibrated confidence metrics.

Result: Vilya-2 achieves 59.1% recovery of peptide interfaces to sub-2 Å backbone RMSD, far exceeding co-folding models. It demonstrates state-of-the-art small-molecule docking performance, generalizes to novel protein-small molecule complexes, handles larger molecules than seen in training, and can be fine-tuned for hit-to-lead optimization.

Conclusion: Vilya-2 establishes the all-atom approach as a general foundation for de novo peptide therapeutic design by unifying predictive accuracy with broad generalizability across chemical space, serving as the structure-prediction oracle needed for peptide design pipelines.

Abstract: Structure-prediction networks built on co-evolutionary statistics have transformed protein-based drug discovery, yet their accuracy does not extend to peptide therapeutics--an increasingly important modality defined by non-canonical residues, macrocyclization, and complex topologies. We introduce Vilya-2, a diffusion transformer that extends the all-atom representation of Vilya-1 from modeling individual molecules to modeling their interactions with protein targets. This all-atom representation enables transfer learning between different molecular types, and delivers highly accurate structural modeling of peptides across sizes, classes, and compositions bound to therapeutically relevant targets. By generating diverse structural ensembles and ranking them with calibrated confidence, Vilya-2 recovers 59.1% of peptide interfaces to sub-2 Å backbone RMSD, far exceeding the performance of a representative co-folding model even when that model is given the bound receptor as a template. In addition, Vilya-2 is state-of-the-art at small-molecule docking, and generalizes to novel protein-small molecule complexes unlike those seen in training. It also generalizes to modeling molecular conformations of diverse macrocycles and disulfide-stapled miniproteins several-fold larger than any molecule seen in training. Finally, Vilya-2 can be used as a foundation model, and fine-tuned to enrich for active compounds in hit-to-lead campaigns. By unifying predictive accuracy with broad generalizability across chemical space, Vilya-2 is the structure-prediction oracle that de novo peptide design pipelines require--establishing the all-atom approach as a general foundation for the design and evaluation of de novo peptide therapeutics.

</details>


### [133] [CondPSE: A Polynomial-Filtered Structural Encoder with Conditional Modulation for Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25169)
*Woohyun Lee,Hogun Park*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CondPSE is a learned positional/structural encoding encoder that uses a polynomial filter bank and conditional modulation to better capture graph topology than standard message-passing GNNs, achieving near-perfect performance on synthetic structural discrimination benchmarks but showing limited advantage on real molecular tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard message-passing graph neural networks are limited by the 1-WL test and fail to distinguish certain non-isomorphic graphs. Positional and structural encodings (PSE) can inject topology information, but existing learned PSE encoders like GPSE may not fully capture complex structural relationships.

Method: CondPSE applies a learnable polynomial graph filter bank to Gaussian node probes, then refines the structural-response branches through FiLM-style modulation conditioned on cross-filter, local message-passing, and graph-level signals. It's pretrained to reconstruct node-level positional/structural targets and graph-level invariants, then frozen for downstream use.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks, CondPSE significantly outperforms GPSE: CSL accuracy from 42.9% to 97.3% and EXP accuracy from 68.3% to 99.9%. Most gains come from the polynomial filter bank. However, on real molecular property prediction (ZINC), CondPSE performs comparably to GPSE without consistently surpassing it.

Conclusion: While CondPSE dramatically improves structural discrimination on synthetic benchmarks, strong synthetic performance doesn't necessarily translate to downstream advantages on real-world tasks like molecular property prediction, possibly due to integration challenges and mismatch between pretraining targets and actual property labels.

Abstract: Message-passing graph neural networks are bounded by the 1-WL test and can miss topological structure that distinguishes non-isomorphic graphs. Positional and structural encodings (PSE) inject such topology-derived signals, and learned PSE encoders such as GPSE pretrain a single encoder to produce these signals from random node probes, which can then be frozen and reused as inputs across downstream graph models. We present CondPSE, a learned PSE encoder that applies a learnable polynomial graph filter bank to standard Gaussian node probes and refines the resulting structural-response branches through FiLM-style modulation conditioned on cross-filter, local message-passing, and graph-level signals. CondPSE is pretrained to reconstruct node-level positional/structural targets and graph-level invariants, and is then frozen for use as a downstream input encoding. On synthetic structural-discrimination benchmarks, CondPSE separates graph structures that 1-WL-bounded message passing cannot: it raises CSL accuracy from 42.9% to 97.3% and EXP accuracy from 68.3% to 99.9% relative to GPSE, and ablations show that the polynomial filter bank accounts for most of this gain. On real molecular property prediction, the picture is more limited. With a hybrid local-message-passing/global-attention backbone, CondPSE performs comparably to GPSE without surpassing it, and a ZINC backbone sweep shows no consistent ordering between the two encoders. We report these results and discuss why strong synthetic structural discrimination does not, on its own, yield a downstream advantage for frozen learned PSE encoders, including the role of downstream integration and possible mismatch between structural pretraining targets and molecular property labels.

</details>


### [134] [Rethinking CD: A Reproducibility Study and Extension on the Ineffectiveness of Contrastive Decoding at Mitigating Object Hallucinations in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25196)
*Arnav Bendre,Guneesh Gupta,Kavish Grover,Chayan Aggarwal,Shreyansh Modi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Contrastive decoding, a training-free approach for reducing object hallucinations in multimodal LLMs, shows superficial benchmark improvements but fails to provide genuine visual grounding gains, with its effects being dataset-specific and often spurious.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is contradictory evidence about whether contrastive decoding (CD) genuinely improves visual grounding in multimodal LLMs or just creates the illusion of performance gains on benchmarks like POPE. The authors aim to verify previous findings and understand CD's limitations across different types of datasets.

Method: The researchers reproduce and extend previous work by: 1) Testing CD's output distribution shift across discriminative datasets, 2) Examining generalizability across different datasets, 3) Verifying that adaptive plausibility constraint reduces to greedy search, 4) Analyzing logit distributions on generative datasets, 5) Proposing proxy methods for comparison, and 6) Investigating hallucination signal propagation through model layers. Experiments were conducted on MME, POPE, and CHAIR benchmarks using LLaVA and Qwen models.

Result: Experimental results validate that CD's apparent improvements are often spurious and don't consistently translate to stronger visual grounding for reducing hallucinations. The effects are dataset-specific, with the adaptive plausibility constraint essentially performing greedy search. The findings show CD fails to provide reliable hallucination mitigation.

Conclusion: Current contrastive decoding strategies are ineffective for genuinely reducing object hallucinations in multimodal LLMs, challenging their practical utility and motivating the need for more reliable approaches that provide consistent visual grounding improvements.

Abstract: Contrastive decoding (CD) has been proposed as a training-free strategy for mitigating object hallucinations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs), with reported gains on benchmarks such as POPE. However, recent work has questioned whether these gains reflect genuine improvements in visual grounding. In this study, we reproduce and extend the findings of "The Mirage of Performance Gains: Why Contrastive Decoding Fails to Mitigate Object Hallucinations in MLLMs." Specifically, we test the claim that CD induces a unidirectional output distribution shift in discriminative datasets and examine its generalizability across datasets. We also verify that the adaptive plausibility constraint (APC) reduces sampling to greedy search on both discriminative and generative benchmarks. Beyond reproduction, we rigorously study the effects of CD across generative and discriminative datasets. We conduct several experiments that provide additional insights: we analyze the logit distributions induced by different CD strategies on generative datasets, propose a proxy method and compare its performance against CD techniques, and investigate how hallucination signals propagate through each layer of the expert and amateur models. Experimental results across MME, POPE, and CHAIR using LLaVA and Qwen validate the original claims and show that the apparent improvements from CD are often spurious and do not consistently translate into stronger visual grounding for reducing hallucinations. These findings challenge the effectiveness of current contrastive decoding strategies and motivate the development of more reliable approaches for mitigating hallucinations in MLLMs.

</details>


### [135] [Algorithmic Separation between Constant-Depth and Logarithmic-Depth Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25200)
*Yunwei Ren,Zihao Wang,Jason D. Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First algorithmic separation proving constant-depth networks require constant error while logarithmic-depth networks can learn efficiently on hierarchical Boolean functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current theoretical depth separations focus on approximation power rather than algorithmic efficiency, with most results limited to comparisons between 2-3 layer networks. Need to understand when deeper networks offer algorithmic advantages.

Method: Identified class of Boolean functions with hierarchically structured Fourier spectra. Proved logarithmic-depth networks can learn efficiently using layerwise coordinate descent by hierarchically reconstructing spectra. Analyzed limitations of constant-depth polynomial-width networks with regular activations and controlled spectral norms.

Result: First algorithmic separation: logarithmic-depth networks can learn hierarchical Boolean functions efficiently while constant-depth networks must incur constant L^2 approximation error under uniform distribution over hypercube.

Conclusion: Depth provides algorithmic advantages beyond just approximation power. Hierarchical function structure favors deeper networks, establishing a theoretical basis for depth selection in neural network architecture design.

Abstract: Despite the empirical advantages of deep networks over shallow ones, theoretical depth separations largely concern approximation power, while algorithmic results are mostly limited to comparisons between two- and three-layer networks. In this work, we prove the first algorithmic separation between constant-depth and logarithmic-depth networks.
  Specifically, we identify a class of Boolean functions with hierarchically structured Fourier spectra that logarithmic-depth networks can learn efficiently using layerwise coordinate descent by reconstructing the spectra hierarchically and adaptively. We also exhibit a subclass for which every constant-depth, polynomial-width network with sufficiently regular activations and controlled spectral norms must incur constant $L^2$ approximation error under the uniform distribution over the hypercube.

</details>


### [136] [A Unified Algorithmic Framework for Hybrid Reinforcement Learning in Tabular MDPs with Shifted Transition Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25207)
*Zheshun Wu,Renjie Zheng,Jinhang Zuo,Zenglin Xu,Fang Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified framework with two algorithms (MIN-UCB-VI and MAX-LCB-VI) for hybrid RL in tabular MDPs, addressing transition shifts between online and offline environments through fine-grained bias information exploitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid reinforcement learning combining online interactions with offline historical data is valuable but challenging when offline data comes from outdated environments with shifted transition dynamics, making naive integration ineffective.

Method: Proposes a unified algorithmic framework with two algorithms: MIN-UCB-VI for regret minimization and MAX-LCB-VI for best policy identification. Both leverage fine-grained bias information to effectively exploit offline data under general transition shifts between source and target environments.

Result: Theoretical guarantees including both instance-dependent and independent upper bounds on regret and sub-optimality gap. Matching lower bounds demonstrate optimality. Extensive experiments validate theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively addresses transition shifts in hybrid RL, achieving optimal performance bounds through fine-grained bias exploitation, bridging online interactions with potentially outdated offline data.

Abstract: This paper investigates a hybrid reinforcement learning setting in tabular Markov Decision Processes (MDPs), where an agent aims to learn an optimal policy by combining online interactions with a target environment and offline data from a source environment. A central challenge is that offline data may be collected from outdated environments with shifted transition dynamics, making naive integration of historical data ineffective. To address this, we propose a unified algorithmic framework featuring two algorithms: MIN-UCB-VI for regret minimization and MAX-LCB-VI for best policy identification. Both algorithms leverage fine-grained bias information to more effectively exploit offline data under general transition shifts. We provide theoretical guarantees for our framework, including both instance-dependent and independent upper bounds on regret and sub-optimality gap. Furthermore, we establish matching lower bounds to demonstrate the optimality of our approach and validate our theoretical findings through extensive experiments.

</details>


### [137] [Neurai-VN Benchmark: Standardized Machine Learning Models for Multimodal Digital Phenotyping in Mental Health Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25232)
*Quoc-Cuong Pham,Hoang-Thuy-Duong Vu,Thi-Thanh-Huong Ha,Huy-Hieu Pham*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A reproducible benchmark for digital phenotyping in mental health using the Neurai-VN dataset, evaluating four clinically relevant binary classification tasks with standardized methods to address reproducibility issues in the field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Progress in digital phenotyping for mental health monitoring is difficult to evaluate due to heterogeneous datasets and inconsistent preprocessing pipelines, creating reproducibility challenges in the field.

Method: Built a reproducible benchmark using the Neurai-VN dataset (multimodal passive sensing + active assessment from wearables/smartphones, 100 Vietnamese adults, 2 weeks). Defined four binary classification tasks with standardized subject-wise cross-validation. Evaluated linear, tree-based, and neural baseline models across predefined feature configurations.

Result: Mean subject-level F1 scores: 0.71 for Healthy Control vs. Depression and Healthy Control vs. Clinical; 0.69 for Healthy Control vs. Anxiety; 0.56 for Depression vs. Anxiety. These provide reproducible baselines for future research.

Conclusion: The benchmark establishes standardized evaluation methods and reproducible baselines for digital phenotyping in mental health classification, addressing current reproducibility issues and providing a foundation for future research comparisons.

Abstract: Digital phenotyping (DP) using smartphones and wearable devices has shown considerable potential for mental health monitoring. However, progress remains difficult to evaluate due to heterogeneous datasets, inconsistent preprocessing pipelines. In this study, we present a reproducible benchmark built upon the Neurai-VN dataset, a high-resolution, multimodal dataset comprising passive sensing and active assessment from wearable and smartphone devices, collected from 100 Vietnamese adults over two weeks. The benchmark defines four clinically relevant binary classification tasks evaluated using standardized subject-wise cross-validation. Representative linear, tree-based, and neural baseline models are evaluated across predefined feature configurations. Mean subject-level F1 scores across five cross-validation folds reached 0.71 for Healthy Control vs. Depression and Healthy Control vs. Clinical, while Healthy Control vs. Anxiety and Depression vs. Anxiety achieved 0.69 and 0.56, respectively. These benchmark results provide reproducible baselines for future research on multimodal DP for mental health classification tasks.

</details>


### [138] [Beyond Single-Episode Optimization: Sliding-Window Aware Generative Auto-Bidding for Long-Term Advertising Effectiveness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25233)
*Binglin Wu,Chuan Yue,Yingyi Zhang,Xianneng Li,Ruyue Deng,Weiru Zhang,Xiaoyi Zeng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SWAG-Bid: A hierarchical auto-bidding framework that considers sliding-window efficiency constraints (7 days) instead of per-day constraints to handle sparse value scenarios, using a planner with masked trajectory modeling and a controller with adaptive gating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing auto-bidding systems treat each day as independent, but sparse advertiser value production makes per-day efficiency ratios statistically unreliable. Platforms now evaluate efficiency over 7-day sliding windows for fair evaluation and long-term effectiveness, creating cross-episode coupling where each day's bids affect multiple overlapping windows.

Method: Hierarchical framework with episode-level planning and step-level execution. Planner uses Masked Trajectory Model to forecast markets and generate candidate plans, scored across all overlapping windows via Multi-Window Model Predictive Control Sampling with exponential confidence decay. Controller uses Per-Step Gated Adaptive Layer Normalization (PSG-AdaLN) to adjust reliance on planner guidance, with Return-to-Go and Cost-to-Go channels for budget/constraint information.

Result: Experiments on AuctionNet-Sparse dataset and online A/B tests on AliExpress show SWAG-Bid achieves competitive constraint satisfaction and value acquisition under sliding-window evaluation compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: SWAG-Bid successfully addresses the challenge of sliding-window efficiency constraints in auto-bidding systems through its hierarchical decomposition and adaptive control mechanisms, improving reliability for advertisers with sparse value production while maintaining performance.

Abstract: Auto-bidding systems optimize bids to maximize value under efficiency constraints such as Cost-Per-Action (CPA). Existing methods treat each day as an independent episode. However, many advertisers produce value so sparsely that per-day efficiency ratios become statistically unreliable, undermining advertiser retention. Platforms therefore evaluate window-level efficiency over sliding windows of $W{=}7$ days, ensuring fair evaluation and long-term advertising effectiveness. This creates cross-episode coupling: each day's bidding decisions affect up to $W$ overlapping windows, so setting daily targets requires anticipating future market conditions. We propose SWAG-Bid (Sliding-Window Aware Generative Auto-Bidding), a hierarchical framework decomposing this challenge into episode-level planning and step-level execution. The planner uses a Masked Trajectory Model to forecast markets and generate candidate plans, scored across all overlapping windows by Multi-Window Model Predictive Control Sampling (MWMS) with exponential confidence decay. The controller adjusts reliance on this guidance through a state-adaptive gate, Per-Step Gated Adaptive Layer Normalization (PSG-AdaLN), complemented by Return-to-Go and Cost-to-Go channels carrying budget and constraint information. Experiments on AuctionNet-Sparse and online A/B tests on AliExpress show that SWAG-Bid achieves competitive constraint satisfaction and value acquisition under sliding-window evaluation.

</details>


### [139] [Bridging Compute- and Data-Optimal Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25271)
*Tian Qin,Kimia Hamidieh,David Alvarez-Melis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Compute-Data (CD) scaling laws that extend classical scaling laws by introducing a token-effectiveness function to model the value of derived tokens (from multi-epoch repetition or paraphrasing) relative to fresh tokens, revealing diminishing returns when substituting compute for data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical compute-optimal scaling laws assume unlimited fresh pretraining data, but current trends show compute growing faster than availability of high-quality data, creating a need for a framework that accounts for data scarcity.

Method: Introduces Compute-Data (CD) scaling laws with a token-effectiveness function η that quantifies value of derived tokens. Fits η for multi-epoch repetition and paraphrasing across model sizes (14M to 600M parameters) using the Dolma-3 corpus.

Result: Token effectiveness is not constant; depends on model size, tokens-per-parameter ratio, and derived data amount, and saturates with corpus expansion. Shows diminishing returns when substituting compute for data. Identifies three operational regimes: compute-bound, data-bound, and model-bound.

Conclusion: Classical compute-optimal allocation is suboptimal in most practical settings. The CD scaling framework provides better guidance for training under data scarcity, revealing fundamental tradeoffs between compute and data resources.

Abstract: Classical compute-optimal scaling laws assume an unbounded supply of fresh pretraining data, yet pretraining is increasingly entering a regime in which compute grows faster than the availability of high-quality data. We propose Compute-Data (CD) scaling laws, a unified framework that bridges compute-optimal scaling, where data scales freely with compute, and data-optimal scaling, where the corpus is fixed while compute can grow without bound. CD scaling extends classical scaling laws by introducing a token-effectiveness function, $η$, which quantifies the value of a derived token-produced, for example, through multi-epoch repetition or paraphrasing-relative to a fresh token, ranging from a perfect substitute to having no value. We fit $η$ for two data-expansion strategies, multi-epoch repetition and paraphrasing, across model sizes from 14M to 600M parameters using the Dolma-3 corpus. We find that token effectiveness is far from constant: it depends jointly on model size, the tokens-per-parameter ratio, and the amount of derived data, and it saturates as the corpus is expanded. The functional form of $η$ implies diminishing returns when substituting compute for data as either model size or data availability increases. It also partitions training into three operational regimes---compute-bound, data-bound, and model-bound---and shows that classical compute-optimal allocation is suboptimal across most practically relevant settings.

</details>


### [140] [HeAD-CP: Heterophily-Aware Diffused Conformal Prediction Sets for Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25273)
*Phan Binh Nguyen Lam,Nguyen Thai Anh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HeAD-CP is a new conformal prediction method for graphs that uses node-wise diffusion based on local homophily estimates, outperforming the baseline DAPS method especially on heterophilic graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current graph-aware diffusion methods like DAPS use uniform diffusion coefficients that assume graph homophily, which harms performance on heterophilic graphs by increasing prediction set sizes.

Method: Proposes HeAD-CP with node-wise diffusion coefficients determined by label-free local-homophily estimates from GNN softmax outputs. Includes three variants: signed-γ for extreme heterophily, edge-compatibility for intermediate heterophily, and DAPS-baseline-with-correction for moderate-to-high homophily.

Result: HeAD-CP family stays at or below plain APS on all datasets while DAPS exceeds APS on 6/10. The oracle over the family improves over DAPS on 8/10 datasets with largest gains on heterophilic graphs (10.3% on Texas). Preserves marginal coverage guarantee.

Conclusion: HeAD-CP addresses DAPS's homophily assumption limitation through node-wise diffusion with local homophily estimates, significantly improving performance on heterophilic graphs while maintaining theoretical guarantees. The main remaining challenge is designing a calibrated label-free selector to approach the oracle performance.

Abstract: Conformal prediction (CP) provides distribution-free uncertainty quantification, and its extension to graphs is an active research direction. Diffused Adaptive Prediction Sets (DAPS) is a widely used graph-aware diffusion baseline, propagating Adaptive Prediction Sets (APS) non-conformity scores along edges with a uniform coefficient $λ$. We identify a fundamental shortcoming of this design: the uniform low-pass diffusion presupposes graph homophily and proves detrimental on heterophilic graphs, enlarging the mean prediction-set size by up to 10.6% relative to plain APS. To mitigate this, we propose HeAD-CP, a family of node-wise diffusion variants whose coefficients are determined by a label-free local-homophily estimate derived from the GNN softmax. Three variants, namely signed-$γ$, edge-compatibility, and a DAPS-baseline-with-correction, are most effective at extreme heterophily, intermediate heterophily, and moderate-to-high homophily, respectively, and all preserve the marginal coverage guarantee. On ten benchmarks, the HeAD-CP family stays at or below plain APS on every dataset, while DAPS exceeds APS on six. The post-hoc oracle over the family improves over DAPS on 8/10 datasets at $p<0.01$ (paired Wilcoxon), with the largest gains on heterophilic graphs (10.3% on Texas); on the two homophilic datasets where DAPS still wins (CiteSeer, PubMed), it retains a marginal advantage of at most 0.002, statistically insignificant on CiteSeer ($p=0.23$). Designing a calibrated label-free selector that approaches this oracle is the main outstanding empirical question.

</details>


### [141] [When Does Deep Representation Learning Help Single-Cell Clustering? A Sensitivity-Aware Diagnostic Benchmark for Biomedical AI Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25288)
*Nguyen Thanh Phong,Truong Viet Vu,Nguyen Ha Thu,Tran An Ky,Tran Hoang Thong,Le Pham Thuy Hien,Nguyen Thai Anh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Single-cell RNA-seq clustering analysis shows contrastive autoencoder achieves highest mean performance, but no statistically significant superiority over PCA baselines, with dataset characteristics determining optimal pipeline choice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To help practitioners decide whether additional deep representation stages are worth compute/tuning costs versus classical PCA pipelines for scRNA-seq clustering in precision medicine workflows.

Method: Diagnostic benchmark of 9 clustering pipelines on 10 real datasets (90-5,685 cells) with comprehensive protocol including Optuna hyperparameter search, repeated-run robustness, statistical testing (Friedman/Wilcoxon-Holm/TOST), and Sobol sensitivity analysis.

Result: Contrastive autoencoder achieved highest mean Adjusted Rand Index (0.7872), but no statistical dominance. Three reproducible regimes identified: probabilistic VAE variants help on smallest datasets, deep autoencoders win on mid-scale data, PCA remains competitive when linear projection captures dominant variation.

Conclusion: Provides dataset-aware, compute-conscious decision framework for biomedical AI pipelines rather than universal superiority claim, with Sobol analysis showing learning rate and latent dimensionality as key tuning parameters.

Abstract: Single-cell ribonucleic acid sequencing (scRNA-seq) is a foundational technology for precision-medicine workflows that contribute to United Nations Sustainable Development Goal 3 on Good Health and Well-being, and unsupervised clustering is the analytical step that turns raw expression matrices into interpretable cell populations. Practitioners therefore face a recurring engineering decision: is an additional deep representation stage worth its compute and tuning cost, or do classical principal component analysis (PCA) pipelines already suffice? We address this question with a diagnostic benchmark of nine clustering pipelines on ten real datasets (90-5,685 cells, 19,046-41,480 genes, 4-11 cell types), augmented by a partial scVI V2 specialized comparison on seven datasets. The protocol integrates Optuna hyperparameter search, repeated-run robustness, Friedman/Wilcoxon-Holm/TOST testing, and Sobol total-order sensitivity analysis. The contrastive autoencoder achieved the highest mean Adjusted Rand Index (0.7872), but Holm-corrected tests did not establish dominance over the strongest baselines. Per-dataset analysis reveals three reproducible regimes: probabilistic variational autoencoder (VAE) variants help on the smallest datasets, deep autoencoders win on mid-scale data with multi-batch or many-type structure, and classical PCA pipelines remain competitive when linear projection already captures the dominant variation. Sobol indices identify learning rate ($S_T=0.70$) and latent dimensionality ($S_T=0.56$) as the dominant variance contributors, indicating where limited tuning budgets should be allocated. The contribution is therefore a dataset-aware and compute-conscious decision framework for biomedical AI pipelines supporting sustainable healthcare analytics, rather than a universal superiority claim.

</details>


### [142] [AMRD: Adaptive Multi-Teacher Relational Distillation for Lightweight Speech Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25289)
*Yuqi Li,Yi-Cheng Lin,Xianglong Wang,Kuo Yang,Xiaoqin Feng,Yixuan Wang,Huiran Duan,Yingli Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AMRD improves edge device speech emotion recognition by adaptively weighting multiple teachers based on batch coherence and using relational distillation to capture inter-sample structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large self-supervised SER models are too computationally expensive for edge devices, and existing multi-teacher distillation suffers from unreliable teacher consistency across batches and ignores important inter-sample relational information.

Method: AMRD uses one-class SVM on each teacher's logit similarity matrix to assign per-batch weights favoring more coherent teachers, plus a relational distillation loss that aligns teacher and student similarity matrices.

Result: On IEMOCAP and CREMA-D datasets across four student architectures, AMRD outperforms single-teacher distillation baselines in most settings, with ablations confirming both components provide complementary gains.

Conclusion: The proposed AMRD framework effectively addresses teacher reliability and relational structure issues in knowledge distillation, enabling better compression of SER models for edge deployment.

Abstract: On-device speech emotion recognition (SER) is critical for real-time applications, yet large self-supervised models that excel at SER are too costly for edge devices. Multi-teacher knowledge distillation can compress them into a lightweight student, but two challenges remain: teacher reliability varies across batches, and logit-level distillation ignores inter-sample relational structure. We propose Adaptive Multi-teacher Relational Distillation (AMRD) to address both. A one-class SVM on each teacher's logit similarity matrix assigns per-batch weights favoring more coherent teachers. A relational distillation loss aligns teacher and student similarity matrices, capturing structure that logit matching misses. On IEMOCAP and CREMA-D datasets across four student architectures, AMRD outperforms single-teacher distillation baselines in most settings, and ablations confirm both components yield complementary gains.

</details>


### [143] [Breaking the Periodicity Assumption: Robust Tensorial Multi-View Clustering via Graph-Spectral Low-Rank Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25295)
*Jintian Ji,Xingsu Li,Songhe Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tensorial multi-view clustering methods suffer from performance degradation when sample ordering is randomized, revealing their reliance on implicit class-based arrangement rather than genuine high-order correlations. A new graph-spectral approach using Graph Fourier Transform is proposed to overcome this limitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing t-SVD-based tensorial multi-view clustering methods critically depend on an implicit "periodicity assumption" where samples are arranged by class. When samples are randomly permuted, these methods show severe performance degradation, indicating that reported performance may be due to privileged sample arrangement rather than genuine high-order structure modeling.

Method: The paper proposes a graph-spectral low-rank tensor learning framework based on Graph Fourier Transform (GFT), which replaces the fixed Fourier basis along the sample mode with a data-driven graph spectral basis. This captures intrinsic manifold structure without relying on particular sample ordering. An anchor-based variant is also developed for large-scale datasets.

Result: Extensive experiments on various benchmarks validate the findings about performance degradation under random permutation and demonstrate the competitive or superior performance of the proposed GFT-based methods compared with state-of-the-art TMC approaches.

Conclusion: The widely adopted FFT-based low-rank regularization in tensorial multi-view clustering relies on implicit sample ordering by class. The proposed GFT-based framework addresses this fundamental flaw by being permutation-invariant and better capturing intrinsic data structures, leading to more robust clustering performance.

Abstract: Tensorial multi-view clustering (TMC) has achieved strong performance due to its ability to capture high-order correlations across multiple views. Most existing t-SVD-based TMC frameworks apply the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) along the sample mode to impose frequency-domain low-rank constraints. However, we reveal that this widely adopted design critically relies on an implicit ``periodicity assumption'' induced by the sample arrangement. When samples are ordered by class, neighboring indices tend to be semantically similar, creating artificial local continuity along the sample mode and a favorable spectral structure for FFT-based low-rank regularization. Once this ordering is removed by random permutation, existing t-SVD-based TMC methods suffer severe performance degradation. This strong sensitivity to class ordering conflicts with the permutation-invariant nature of clustering and indicates that part of the reported performance may be attributed to a privileged sample arrangement rather than genuine high-order structure modeling. In this paper, we systematically investigate this phenomenon and its underlying algebraic and spectral mechanisms. To address this fundamental flaw, we further propose a graph-spectral low-rank tensor learning framework based on the Graph Fourier Transform (GFT), which replaces the fixed Fourier basis along the sample mode with a data-driven graph spectral basis, thereby capturing the intrinsic manifold structure without relying on a particular sample ordering. Moreover, we develop an anchor-based variant to address large-scale datasets efficiently. Extensive experiments on various benchmarks validate our findings and demonstrate the competitive or superior performance of the proposed methods compared with state-of-the-art TMC approaches.

</details>


### [144] [Zhinv: Real-time hub-height wind field reconstruction using only local sparse observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25298)
*Zongwei Zhang,Chin Chun Ooi,Lianlei Lin,Sheng Gao,Tiantian He,Yew Soon Ong,Junkai Wang,Hangyi Yu,Jiaqi Zhang,Hanqing Zhao,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Zhinv is an end-to-end framework that reconstructs fine-grid wind fields from sparse, irregular local observations, achieving ~66% error reduction compared to traditional Kriging methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Increasing wind power integration requires fine-grained regional wind field knowledge, but available operational data is sparse, discrete, and irregularly distributed, limiting direct use for wind power regulation, resource assessment, and low-altitude perception tasks.

Method: Zhinv is an end-to-end reconstruction framework that directly processes sparse and irregular observations to generate fine-grid wind fields at hub-height. It bypasses traditional Numerical Weather Prediction (NWP) and complex assimilation processes.

Result: Experiments across Northeast China, Europe, and Southeast Asia show Zhinv accurately, robustly, efficiently reconstructs fine-grid wind fields from sparse observations, reducing error by ~66% compared to Kriging. Enables direct real-time wind resource assessment using local wind-power observations.

Conclusion: Zhinv provides a practical solution for wind power centers to perform direct, real-time wind resource assessment without relying on NWP and complex assimilation, significantly improving reconstruction accuracy from sparse local observations.

Abstract: The high proportion of wind power connected to the grid places higher demands on fine-grained knowledge of regional wind fields. Since the wind information directly obtainable in actual operations is mostly sparse, discrete, and irregularly distributed local observations, it is difficult to directly meet the needs of tasks such as wind power regulation, wind resource assessment, and low-altitude environmental perception of continuous regional wind fields. Therefore, we propose Zhinv, an end-to-end reconstruction framework that directly weaves sparse and irregular observations into a fine-grid wind field at hub-height. Experiments in Northeast China, Europe, and Southeast Asia demonstrate that Zhinv can accurately, robustly, and efficiently reconstruct fine-grid wind fields from sparse observations, reducing the error by about 66% compared with Kriging. With local wind-power observations as input, Zhinv enables wind power centers to bypass NWP and complex assimilation processes, supporting direct and real-time wind resource assessment from locally available data.

</details>


### [145] [Retraction-Free Optimization over the Stiefel Manifold for the LoRA Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25299)
*Yuan Zhang,Jiang Hu,Zhijian Lai,Lin Lin,Zaiwen Wen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A retraction-free, penalty-free algorithm for Stiefel manifold optimization that lands directly on the manifold, with applications to LoRA fine-tuning for LLMs via geometry-accelerated adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Stiefel manifold optimization methods either require costly orthonormalization operations (retraction-based methods) or rely on careful step size selection and penalty parameter tuning (landing methods), making them inefficient or difficult to use in practice, especially for large-scale problems.

Method: Proposes a retraction-free and penalty parameter-free algorithm that directly lands on the Stiefel manifold. Leverages the strongly-convex-like property of quadratic penalty functions and proximal smoothness of the manifold. Applies this to LoRA fine-tuning by reformulating it as a manifold optimization problem (Manifold-LoRA) with a carefully designed step size strategy for accelerated training.

Result: Establishes global convergence guarantees with best-known iteration complexities under both constant and diminishing step sizes. Numerical experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate efficiency and strong downstream performance of Manifold-LoRA for LLM fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The proposed retraction-free, penalty-free algorithm provides an efficient alternative for Stiefel manifold optimization, and its application to LoRA fine-tuning via Manifold-LoRA enables geometry-accelerated adaptation for large language models with demonstrated effectiveness.

Abstract: Optimization over the Stiefel manifold plays a significant role in various machine learning tasks. Existing methods either use the retraction operators, requiring costly orthonormalization for large-scale matrices, or employ landing methods that rely on careful step size selection and penalty parameter tuning. To address these challenges, we propose a retraction-free and penalty parameter-free algorithm that directly lands on the manifold. By leveraging the strongly-convex-like property of the quadratic penalty function and the proximal smoothness of the Stiefel manifold, we establish global convergence guarantees with the best-known iteration complexities under both constant and diminishing step sizes. Then, we reformulate the low-rank adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning problem for large language models as a manifold optimization problem, introducing Manifold-LoRA for geometry-accelerated adaptation. This approach employs the proposed landing technique and a carefully designed step size strategy to accelerate the training process. Numerical experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate the efficiency and strong downstream performance of the proposed method.

</details>


### [146] [Guiding Posterior Exploration with Optimizer-Derived Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25312)
*Moritz Schlager,Emanuel Sommer,Thomas Möllenhoff,David Rügamer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Using curvature information from adaptive optimizers like AdamW to precondition MCMC sampling in Bayesian neural networks, eliminating burn-in and improving stability at no extra cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling-based methods for Bayesian neural networks are computationally expensive due to high-dimensional multimodal posteriors. While Bayesian Deep Ensembles (warmstarting from multiple optimized solutions) helps, there's still a need to reduce computational overhead and improve sampling efficiency.

Method: Leverage curvature estimates computed during the warmstart phase (as a byproduct of adaptive optimizers like AdamW) to inform the sampling phase. These optimizer-derived geometries provide preconditioning for sampling, eliminating lengthy burn-in phases and improving numerical stability.

Result: The proposed preconditioned sampling strategy substantially reduces or eliminates burn-in phase, leads to greater numerical stability, maintains or improves predictive performance and uncertainty quantification, and requires no additional computational costs. Results are consistent across various datasets and network architectures.

Conclusion: Optimizer-derived curvature information can effectively precondition MCMC sampling for Bayesian neural networks, providing an efficient, stable, and cost-free enhancement to Bayesian Deep Ensembles methodology.

Abstract: Sampling-based methods offer a principled approach to uncertainty quantification in Bayesian neural networks. Their practical use, however, is often challenged by the computational cost of exploring high-dimensional and multimodal posterior distributions. To overcome these difficulties, Bayesian Deep Ensembles, i.e., warmstarting the sampling from several optimized solutions, have proven to be an effective strategy. In this paper, we demonstrate that curvature estimates computed during the warmstart as a byproduct in adaptive optimizers such as AdamW can inform the sampling phase at negligible additional cost. Specifically, our proposed preconditioned sampling strategy based on optimizer-derived geometries can substantially reduce or even eliminate the need for a lengthy sampling burn-in phase and leads to greater numerical stability. This approach consistently maintains or improves predictive performance and uncertainty quantification without any additional computational costs. We confirm the consistency of our findings across various datasets and network architectures.

</details>


### [147] [Explainable AI for Chronic Kidney Disease Prediction Using Simulated Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25348)
*Md Zahid Hasan Ontor,Md Al Amin,Anik Dev Nath,Bikash Kumar Paul*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study proposes a federated learning framework with VotingClassifier for early CKD detection, achieving 99% average accuracy while addressing data privacy concerns through decentralized training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CKD is a major public health challenge where early detection is critical for preventing complications and improving patient outcomes. The research aims to develop a predictive system that maintains patient data privacy while achieving high diagnostic accuracy.

Method: The study uses Federated Learning (FL) with a VotingClassifier ensemble combining Random Forest, AdaBoost, and XGBoost. GridSearchCV was applied on the client side for hyperparameter optimization, and explainable AI (XAI) techniques were incorporated to interpret model predictions for transparency.

Result: The global model achieved an average accuracy of 99% in predicting CKD, demonstrating the effectiveness of the federated learning approach with ensemble methods.

Conclusion: Interpretable federated learning models show significant potential for early CKD diagnosis while maintaining data privacy, representing an advancement in data-driven healthcare solutions that balance accuracy with trustworthiness.

Abstract: Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD), characterized by the gradual loss of kidney function, remains a significant public health challenge. Early detection is crucial for preventing severe complications and enhancing patient outcomes. In this study, Federated Learning (FL) with a VotingClassifier was used to predict CKD using a clinical dataset, where Random Forest, AdaBoost, and XGBoost were utilized to compare and identify the best-fitting model for the global server. Additionally, GridSearchCV was applied to optimize the models' performance on the client's side. To enhance model transparency and trustworthiness, explainable AI (XAI) techniques were incorporated to interpret the prediction mechanisms. The global model's average accuracy was 99%, highlighting the potential of interpretable FL models in supporting early CKD diagnosis and advancing data-driven healthcare solutions.

</details>


### [148] [Raven: High-Recall Sequence Modeling with Sparse Memory Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25357)
*Arshia Afzal,Aviv Bick,Eric P. Xing,Volkan Cevher,Albert Gu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Raven is a new linear-time sequence model that improves long-context recall by combining sparse memory updates (like sliding-window attention) with state maintenance (like state-space models) through learned routing to specific memory slots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing linear-time sequence models face a tradeoff in long-context recall: state-space models suffer from interference due to dense state updates, while sliding-window attention suffers from information loss when tokens exit the fixed window. There's a need for a model that can maintain long-range content more effectively.

Method: Raven introduces a fixed set of memory slots and at each step decays and updates only a selected subset via learned, input-dependent routing. This approach interpolates between dense state updates (SSMs) and sparse explicit token storage (SWA), mitigating position-based overwriting and hard eviction while reducing interference.

Result: Raven achieves competitive or superior performance on recall-intensive benchmarks compared to prior linear-time baselines, maintaining strong long-context recall where both SWA and SSMs degrade sharply.
